### B.18 · L-0075 — Vijay Madanlal Choudhary and the PMLA procedural architecture

*Selected because: the contested spread — lowest id in one of the seven grounds; put in question by the first external review — contested with a pending outside adjudication.*

`contested` · T2 · governance · event · 2022-07 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-01 · contested ground: interpretation

**History.** Created 2026-08-01. Never edited since.

**What this is.** A three-judge bench upheld the PMLA provisions on search, seizure, attachment, arrest, the reverse burden of proof in Section 24, the twin bail conditions in Section 45, and the admissibility of statements to ED officers under Section 50, and held that the ECIR need not be supplied to the accused. Review petitions were admitted in August 2022 on two of those holdings and remain pending.

**Strongest case for.** The Court held that money laundering is a distinct offence with transnational reach, that the ECIR is an internal document rather than an FIR and so attracts different disclosure obligations, and that Parliament may calibrate procedure to the gravity of an offence. The twin bail conditions replicate a legislative approach the Court has upheld in other special statutes. The judgment was a considered constitutional ruling on a fully argued challenge, not an administrative accommodation.

**Strongest case against.** The combination of a reverse burden, admissible statements recorded in custody by officers who are not police, and a bail bar requiring the court to be satisfied of innocence at the threshold, inverts ordinary criminal-procedure protections in a single statute. The Court admitted review petitions within a month on exactly two points - non-supply of the ECIR and the reversed presumption of innocence - which is an unusual acknowledgment that the reasoning may not hold. Four years on, the review has not been heard.

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-52

**Sources** (1, T1×1):

- `T1` Vijay Madanlal Choudhary v. Union of India, Supreme Court of India, July 2022 — https://main.sci.gov.in/

### B.19 · L-0079 — Agency action against news organisations

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — contested on a missing-evidence ground.*

`contested` · T2 · governance · episode · 2021–2024 · confidence medium · last researched 2026-08-01 · contested ground: evidence-unobservable

**History.** Created 2026-08-01. 1 commit(s) touched it afterwards: 2026-08-01 (differentFacts, differentFactsNote).

**What this is.** Income tax surveys at BBC India offices in February 2023, income tax raids on Dainik Bhaskar and Bharat Samachar in July 2021, and the arrest of NewsClick's founder and HR head under the UAPA in October 2023. The Supreme Court declared the NewsClick arrest and remand invalid in May 2024 for failure to furnish written grounds of arrest.

**Strongest case for.** Tax and foreign-funding law applies to media companies as it does to any other company, and exempting them would create a protected class. The CBDT stated specific allegations in the BBC matter concerning transfer pricing and diversion of profits, and in the Dainik Bhaskar matter alleged evasion of a defined magnitude. The NewsClick investigation followed published reporting on foreign funding, not on editorial content. In each instance the outlets continued publishing.

**Strongest case against.** The timing in each case is close enough to critical coverage to require explanation: the BBC survey came weeks after a documentary on the Prime Minister, the Dainik Bhaskar raids followed its reporting on the second COVID wave. The Supreme Court found the NewsClick arrest unlawful, which establishes at least one instance where the process itself failed the legal standard. The Committee to Protect Journalists recorded seven journalists jailed in India on its 1 December census in each of 2020 through 2023, most held under the UAPA.

**Sources** (3, T1×2 T3×1):

- `T1` Supreme Court of India, NewsClick arrest and remand, 15 May 2024 — https://main.sci.gov.in/
- `T1` CBDT statements on the BBC survey, February 2023 — https://www.incometaxindia.gov.in/
- `T3` Committee to Protect Journalists prison census, 2020-2023 — https://cpj.org/

### B.20 · L-0082 — Sedition kept in abeyance, then replaced

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — contested with a pending outside adjudication.*

`contested` · T2 · governance · reform · 2022–2024 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-01 · contested ground: time

**History.** Created 2026-08-01. 1 commit(s) touched it afterwards: 2026-08-01 (differentFacts, differentFactsNote, revisitTrigger).

**What this is.** The Supreme Court kept IPC Section 124A in abeyance in May 2022, freezing pending trials and fresh registrations; recorded cases fell from 76 in 2021 to 20 in 2022. The Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita 2023 removed 124A and introduced Section 152, covering acts endangering the sovereignty, unity and integrity of India.

**Strongest case for.** A colonial-era offence that had been used against 548 people between 2015 and 2020 with 12 convictions is off the statute book, and the government did what critics had demanded for decades. Section 152 is framed around sovereignty and territorial integrity rather than around disaffection towards the government, which is the narrowing critics had specifically asked for. Registrations fell sharply and immediately after the abeyance order.

**Strongest case against.** Section 152 covers a wider range of conduct than 124A on its face, including by subversive activities and encouraging separatist feelings, and carries a higher minimum sentence. More importantly, 124A had accumulated six decades of judicial narrowing since Kedar Nath Singh, all of which attaches to the repealed text and none of which automatically attaches to the new one, so the effective scope may be broader even if the words are narrower. Calling this a repeal is a description of the statute book rather than of the law in operation.

**Re-test when.** Effective scope of BNS 152 in operation becomes a buildable common measure once a prosecution record accumulates. Re-test against (c) then.

**Series this rests on.** sedition-cases-registered

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-51

**Sources** (3, T1×3):

- `T1` S.G. Vombatkere v. Union of India, Supreme Court of India, May 2022 — https://main.sci.gov.in/
- `T1` Bharatiya Nyaya Sanhita 2023, Section 152 — https://www.indiacode.nic.in/
- `T1` NCRB Crime in India — https://www.ncrb.gov.in/crime-in-india.html

### B.21 · L-0092 — The Ministry of Education's two framings of ASER 2024, six months apart

*Selected because: the contested spread — lowest id in one of the seven grounds.*

`contested` · T3 · governance/education · event · 2025-02-04–2025-07-28 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-02 · contested ground: vocabulary-residue

**History.** Created 2026-08-02. Never edited since.

**What this is.** On 4 February 2025 the Ministry of Education issued a factsheet on ASER 2024 headlined 'Leap in Rural School Enrollment' which reproduced ASER's findings near-verbatim while deleting every 2022 data point. On 28 July 2025 the same Ministry published a backgrounder attributing the learning recovery to its own policies - and reinstated the same 2022 troughs, because the recovery cannot be shown without them.

**What can be established since.** Three separate 2022 figures were removed from the February document. ASER: government enrolment among 6-14s 'was 65.6 per cent in 2018. The pandemic saw large increases in government school enrollments (72.9 per cent in 2022). But by 2024, the all-India figure declined to 66.8 per cent.' The factsheet: '...In 2018, 65.5 per cent... By 2024, the all-India figure increased to 66.8 per cent.' A 6.1-point decline from the 2022 peak was presented, under a headline claiming a leap, as an increase - by deleting the peak. Standard III government reading lost its 'fell to 16.3 per cent in 2022'; Standard III arithmetic lost its '25.9 per cent in 2022'; and 'dropped from 13.1 per cent in 2018 to 7.5 per cent in 2022, but stayed about the same at 7.9 per cent in 2024' became 'dropped sharply from 13.1 per cent in 2018 to 7.9 per cent in 2024'. In July, the backgrounder - sourced internally to the Department's own Ready Reckoner - wrote that Class III government-school reading reached 23.4 per cent in 2024, 'up from 16.3 per cent in 2022 and 20.9 per cent in 2018', and that arithmetic reached 27.6 per cent 'compared to 20.2 per cent in 2022'.

**Strongest case for.** A press factsheet is a summary, not a statistical publication, and summarising a six-year movement from 2018 to 2024 without narrating the intermediate trough is ordinary précis rather than misrepresentation. The underlying ASER report was public, in full, on the same day; nothing was concealed from anyone who read it. The figures reproduced are accurate as far as they go, the endpoints are correctly stated, and the enrolment claim is defensible on its own terms: rural government-school enrolment in 2024 IS higher than in 2018. And when the Ministry did make an attribution claim, in July, it stated the trough plainly and correctly.

**Strongest case against.** The February document is not a summary. It reproduces ASER's own sentences near-verbatim and then removes three numbers, each of which is the one that makes the sentence say the opposite of what the headline claims. The headline is 'Leap in Rural School Enrollment' and the underlying movement it describes is a 6.1-point fall from a peak - a leap manufactured by deleting the peak. The out-of-school figure is the clearest case: ASER's own sentence records that the fall stopped in 2022 and the number ticked back up, and the factsheet turns it into a sharp drop by removing the stop. And the July document settles the question of whether the deletions were incidental: the same Ministry, on the same data, reinstated exactly those figures six months later, because the recovery story needs the trough that the enrolment story needed gone. Same ministry, opposite framings, identical underlying data.

**Note on the verdict itself.** The assessment vocabulary is built for measures with stated objectives and this record is a presentational act. `contested` is used on its written definition - the evidence supports more than one defensible reading and the record does not choose - and the mechanism fits, since a précis reading and a misrepresentation reading are both available on the same documents. The value may change on review if a value for presentational findings is added.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** Whether the deletions were an editorial decision or a drafting artefact — No internal record of how the factsheet was drafted is public, and none would be expected to be. The record therefore states what the two documents say and does not attribute intent.

**Series this rests on.** aser-std3-reading-govt, aser-std3-arithmetic, aser-out-of-school-15-16, aser-private-share-rural

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-59

**Sources** (3, T1×2 T4×1):

- `T1` PIB Release PRID 2099725, Ministry of Education, 'Leap in Rural School Enrollment', 4 February 2025 — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleaseIframePage.aspx?PRID=2099725
- `T1` PIB backgrounder, 'Classrooms of Change: NEP 2020 and the New Era of Schooling', 28 July 2025 — https://static.pib.gov.in/WriteReadData/specificdocs/documents/2025/jul/doc2025729593801.pdf
- `T4` ASER 2024 National Findings, ASER Centre — https://asercentre.org/wp-content/uploads/2022/12/ASER-2024-National-findings.pdf

### B.22 · L-0094 — A national teacher-vacancy statistic, tabled for a decade and then withdrawn

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — statutory duty scored no-objective — the comparison set.*

`no-objective` · T1 · governance/federalism/education · episode · 2014-07-30–2026-07-20 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-03

**History.** Created 2026-08-02. 3 commit(s) touched it afterwards: 2026-08-02 (unmeasured) · 2026-08-03 (assessment) · 2026-08-03 (asOf, assessmentNote). **Verdict moved:** first filed contested ; contested→no-objective on 2026-08-03 — "Apply the rescore: 25 records to no-objective, 4 notes, 4 held red".

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** The "10 lakh vacancies" figure in circulation is not a figure for Indian school teaching posts at large. It covers government and Samagra Shiksha-funded aided schools; private unaided schools are wholly outside it, and the denominator is almost never stated when the number is quoted.

**What this is.** From 2014 the Ministry routinely tabled all-India totals for sanctioned, in-position and vacant school teaching posts in both Houses, drawn from Annual Work Plan and Budget and Project Approval Board returns. The last full national figure was given on 02.08.2023 (Rajya Sabha Unstarred 1460); the last vacancy-only national figure on 07.02.2024 (Rajya Sabha Unstarred 525). Since then no answer in either House has carried a sanctioned or a vacancy number at any level. The Ministry continues to compute it: it furnished 63,26,207 sanctioned and 9,59,148 vacant for 2023-24, and 69,85,760 and 9,82,662 for 2024-25, to the Standing Committee on 7 January 2025, printed in the 368th Report of 08.08.2025.

**What can be established since.** The refusal formulation - that education is in the Concurrent List and the data is maintained by States - appears in 1 of 46 retrieved answers from the 16th Lok Sabha and in 37 of about 76 from the 17th and 18th, and its spread maps onto the disappearance of the tables. The source migrated with it: AWP&B and PAB minutes (2015-2019), which carry sanctioned posts, then PRABANDH (2020-2024), then UDISE+ (2025-2026), which does not. A full-text search of the UDISE+ 2023-24 and 2024-25 national reports returns zero occurrences of any form of "vacancy" or "sanction"; UDISE+ holds no sanctioned-post denominator, so no vacancy is computable from it. On 17.12.2025 an MP put the Ministry’s own two Standing Committee totals to the Minister by number and asked him to confirm them (Rajya Sabha Starred 193); the reply neither confirmed nor denied and contained no figure. On 20.07.2026 a question asking for state-wise and level-wise sanctioned and vacant posts (Lok Sabha Unstarred 87) was answered with teachers in position only.

**Strongest case for.** The constitutional position is real and is not a pretext. Education is in the Concurrent List, recruitment and service conditions are state functions, and the Union directly administers about 3,000 of roughly 14.7 lakh schools - about 0.2 per cent. A sanctioned post is created by a state government under state rules, so there is no national definition of sanctioned strength and no national sanctioning authority. The aggregates that were tabled show exactly the defects that argument predicts: "vacant" is an arithmetic residual rather than an observation; the 2023-24 national total silently drops Tamil Nadu, which did not report on PRABANDH; and several annexures build a TOTAL row over 27, 28 or 30 of 36 jurisdictions without saying so. Migrating parliamentary answers to UDISE+ moves them onto a census-based instrument with a published methodology, and answering with teachers in position - a quantity actually collected - rather than a residual assembled from unreconciled state returns is defensible statistical practice.

**Strongest case against.** The Ministry has not stopped producing the number. It gave it to the Standing Committee in January 2025 and let it be printed in August 2025; when an MP quoted those same two figures back in December 2025, the Minister declined to confirm his own department’s numbers. A statistic furnished to a committee and withheld from the House has not ceased to exist - it has ceased to be answerable for. The Union also receives the data as a condition of releasing Samagra Shiksha money through the Project Approval Board, so the federal argument reaches the sanctioning question but not the collection question. And the substitution is not neutral: UDISE+ is the one instrument that lacks the denominator, so the switch removes the possibility of the question being answered rather than improving the answer. The Committee had already recorded, in its 363rd Report, that because the funds are granted by the Union it is imperative for the Department to seek the data and maintain it centrally.

**Different facts — flag is TRUE.** The Department is counting administrative competence - which government sanctions a post, which one recruits against it, and therefore which one holds the record - and the Committee is counting a fiscal relationship, that the Union transfers the money the posts are paid from and conditions the transfer on state returns it already receives. Both are correct and neither contradicts the other. What the record establishes is that the dispute is not about whether the Union can know the number: it produced the number for a decade and produced it again in January 2025.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Scored no-objective, and not reversed. No objective was stated at announcement for a disclosure practice, so there is nothing to score the withdrawal against. `measure` in the reversed definition means an intervention that acts on the world, and every other reversed record withdraws something that was doing work. Here recruitment, funding and the PRABANDH returns all continue; what stopped is an answer. A disclosure practice is not a measure. Not failed either - neither side of the pair describes a failure: caseFor defends the change as competent statistical practice, caseAgainst attacks it as evasion, and neither says the Ministry tried something and could not. The definition of reversed was amended on 2026-08-02 to state this, because this record was its third candidate mechanism in two weeks. VALUE-AND-NOTE RECONCILED 2026-08-03: this record was rescored contested -> no-objective in the pass that introduced no-objective to drain the contested sink, and the note was not rewritten with it, so the reasoning of record argued a value the field had abandoned. Nothing in the validator sees that - both halves are individually valid. The substantive reasoning below stood up and is preserved verbatim; only the value it names has been corrected.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** A national teacher-vacancy figure in answer to a parliamentary question, after February 2024 — Tabled routinely from 2014; last full national figure Rajya Sabha Unstarred 1460 of 02.08.2023, last vacancy-only figure Rajya Sabha Unstarred 525 of 07.02.2024. Since then every question is answered with the Concurrent List formulation and teachers in position from UDISE+. The quantity is still produced - furnished to the Standing Committee on 07.01.2025 and printed in the 368th Report - so it exists and is producible under compulsion. This is discontinuation of publication, not absence of collection. **What would close it:** A tabled answer carrying sanctioned, in-position and vacant posts, or restoration of the annexures now parked at education.gov.in/parl_ques

**Not measured — stated reason `never-defined`.** A defined national statistic, as against an aggregate assembled on request — No published document defines what "sanctioned" covers, which levels, or which school-management categories. The 368th Report’s entire method note is one sentence recording that the Department furnished the table at a meeting. Two of that Report’s own tables give contradictory 2023-24 totals a page apart - 55,76,000 sanctioned and 8,31,000 vacant against 63,26,207 and 9,59,148 - and it does not reconcile them.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The state-wise annexures to the answers that do carry data — Several answers - Lok Sabha Unstarred 4925 of 23.03.2026, Lok Sabha Starred 210 of 04.08.2025, Rajya Sabha Starred 193 of 17.12.2025 - park their state-wise annexures at education.gov.in/parl_ques rather than tabling them. The repository is live and indexed but was unreachable throughout retrieval, so the annexures exist and were not obtained. **What would close it:** Direct retrieval from education.gov.in/parl_ques, retried; if still unreachable, an RTI to the DoSEL Parliament Section naming Lok Sabha Unstarred 4925 of 23.03.2026, Lok Sabha Starred 210 of 04.08.2025 and Rajya Sabha Starred 193 of 17.12.2025, requesting the annexures as tabled. Documents tabled in Parliament are public records.

### B.23 · L-0095 — RTE section 26's ten per cent vacancy ceiling, breached in the Union's own schools

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — statutory duty scored failed.*

`failed` · T3 · governance/education · institutional · 2010-04-01–2025-12-31 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-02

**History.** Created 2026-08-02. 1 commit(s) touched it afterwards: 2026-08-05 (sources).

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** The Committee separately records 'overall 30 to 50 per cent vacancies in KVs and NVs', which could not be reconciled with the dated tables in the same reports giving 17.1 and 29.6 per cent. The tabulated figures are dated, internally consistent and preferred here; the discrepancy stands unresolved.

**What this is.** Section 26 of the Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act 2009 requires the appointing authority for any school established, owned, controlled or substantially financed by the appropriate Government to ensure that teacher vacancies do not exceed ten per cent of sanctioned strength. Measured national rates are 14 to 15 per cent. In Kendriya Vidyalayas, where the appropriate Government is the Union itself, the rate rose from 14.7 to 17.1 per cent over twelve months; in Navodaya Vidyalayas it rose from 23.6 to 29.6 per cent over the same twelve months - the statutory ceiling breached by a factor of three. Both readings are at 31 December. The same KVS reports give 13.2 per cent as on 1 August 2024, which is the lower base a seventeen-month comparison would use; it is not used here, because the year-end pair is like for like.

**What can be established since.** The provision was not invoked. None of the four Rajya Sabha Standing Committee reports retrieved - the 363rd, 368th, 373rd and 376th - cites section 26. The Committee argues from adequacy and from 'the spirit of RTE Act' and does not reach for the binding numeric provision its own subject-matter statute contains. The Department's own compliance answer invokes a different measure entirely: 'the overall pupil-teacher ratio of the country is 25:1 across all levels, that is within the norms laid down under the RTE Act' - a national mean offered against a school-by-school duty. Meanwhile the Union filled the gap with contract staff rather than posts: KVS contract engagements rose from 3,260 (2020-21) to 7,909 (2025-26), about 15.7 per cent of sanctioned teaching strength against 8,618 vacant posts, and NVS contract teaching appointments rose 43 per cent from 2021 to 2025 while its vacancy rate rose with them.

**Strongest case for.** Vacancy is a flow, not a stock, and a ten per cent ceiling measured on a single date is close to unmeetable in a system of this size: retirements, resignations, promotions and new sanctions create posts continuously, and a recruitment cycle running through advertisement, examination, result, litigation and joining takes longer than the vacancies take to arise. The central institutions have not left classrooms empty - they engage contract teachers precisely so that, in NVS's own words, 'to protect the academic interest of students, teachers are being appointed on short term contract basis till the vacancies are filled on regular basis', so the teaching position on the ground is materially better than the vacancy rate implies. And KVS and NVS are the most transparent part of the system: their sanctioned, in-position and vacant figures are precise, dated and reported annually, which is exactly why the breach is visible here and invisible for the 99.8 per cent of schools the states run.

**Strongest case against.** Section 26 is not a target, an aspiration or a norm to be balanced against operational reality. It is a statutory duty, expressed as a number, owed by the appointing authority - and for Kendriya and Navodaya Vidyalayas the appointing authority is the Union, which has undivided control, its own recruitment machinery, its own budget and no federal excuse of any kind. It is breached at 1.7 times the ceiling in one and 3.0 times in the other, and the trend is worsening in both, across the same twelve months. The contract-staff answer concedes the point rather than rebutting it: contract engagement is rising in step with vacancies rather than in place of a recruitment cycle, KVS itself calls it a stop-gap arrangement for which 'specifying a fixed contract period is not possible', and it has been the arrangement for at least five years. The Committee's directive to fill these posts with regular teachers 'not later than 31st March, 2026' was, on the Committee's own figures as at 31 December 2025, not going to be met. And in fifteen years no report, no reply and no committee has cited the section: a binding numeric provision has sat unenforced in the governing statute while everyone argued about ratios.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Assessed failed on the written definition - the measure did not achieve the objective stated at its enactment - with the objective being a number in the statute rather than a policy claim. The failure is of the duty, not of a programme, which is why the record is typed institutional.

**Series this rests on.** teacher-vacancy-rate-kvs, teacher-vacancy-rate-nvs, teacher-vacancy-rate-ssa, ptr-primary-udise

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-64

**Sources** (4, T1×4):

- `T1` The Right of Children to Free and Compulsory Education Act 2009 (Act 35 of 2009), section 26, India Code consolidated text — India Code item 123456789/12911: Act ID 200935, Act No. 35 of 2009, enacted 26 August 2009, Type: CENTRAL ACT (Ministry of Human Resource Development, Department of School Education and Literacy). Retrieved through a pinned resolver (www.indiacode.nic.in -> 94.202.207.51; a plain request returns HTTP 000 on this machine) and identified by confirming the item's section index carries Section 26, “Filling up vacancies of teachers”. TWO LIMITS STATED: the Act-level item carries the section INDEX, not the section text, so the section numbers and headings are verified here and the operative wording is NOT; and a same-titled item at handle 123456789/13682 is Type: STATE, Location: Delhi, so short title alone does not identify this Act. — https://www.indiacode.nic.in/handle/123456789/12911

- `T1` 373rd Report, p. 85 (the Department's pupil-teacher-ratio compliance answer) — https://bucketapi.rajyasabha.digital/public-bucket/committee_file/ReportFile/16/214/373_2026_3_12.pdf

### B.24 · L-0106 — Single-teacher schools and the ratio norm that permits them

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — statutory duty scored failed.*

`failed` · T1 · governance/education · institutional · 2013-03-31–2026-07 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-02

**History.** Created 2026-08-02. 2 commit(s) touched it afterwards: 2026-08-02 (unmeasured) · 2026-08-05 (sources).

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** The RTE Act does not prescribe 30:1 for primary. The Schedule prescribes a staffing slab whose binding norm for a small primary school is TWO teachers, and whose only explicit primary ratio is a ceiling of 40:1 above two hundred children. The 30:1 in universal circulation, including in the Standing Committee's own text, is an administrative gloss on the first slab.

**What this is.** RTE section 25 required the pupil-teacher ratio in the Act's Schedule to be maintained 'in each school' within three years of commencement, that is by 31 March 2013. Thirteen years later about one lakh schools have a single teacher - 104,125 in FY2024-25, 7.08 per cent of all schools, falling to about 100,843 in FY2025-26 - while the Department answers the school-level duty with a national mean of 25:1. Both are true at once because the norm is a ratio and not a coverage rule.

**What can be established since.** The Act does not contain the figure 30:1 that everyone cites, including the Standing Committee about its own governing statute. The Schedule prescribes a staffing SLAB for Classes I-V - two teachers up to sixty children, three up to ninety, rising to a ceiling of 40:1 above two hundred - and its only explicit ratio is 35:1 for Classes VI-VIII. On that reading the binding norm for a small primary school is two teachers, so every single-teacher school is in breach of the Schedule independently of any ratio, and no official document retrieved applies that test. The distributional indicator that would show compliance was published annually by the predecessor system through 2015-16 - one in four primary schools above the norm in its final year - and has never been published since, although the current system holds school-level enrolment and teacher counts for every school. The single computation of it since is a university centre's, from 2021-22 microdata: 24 per cent of all schools above 35:1, 81 per cent of them rural, with 70 per cent of government schools in Bihar and 50 per cent in Delhi carrying a teacher requirement.

**Strongest case for.** The count is falling and the direction has been sustained: single-teacher schools went from 118,190 (FY2022-23) to about 100,843 (FY2025-26), a 15 per cent reduction in three years, and enrolment in them fell faster still, from 47.4 lakh to 29.1 lakh. National pupil-teacher ratios are inside every norm at every level and improving, the total teaching workforce crossed one crore for the first time, and the deployment problem is genuinely a state function in a system where the Union runs 0.2 per cent of schools. Many single-teacher schools also exist for a defensible reason: they are small, remote and hilly, and the alternative to a one-teacher school in such a habitation is no school - which is why the access ratio for primary schooling stands at 97.8 per cent of habitations.

**Strongest case against.** A statutory duty owed school by school, with a deadline of 31 March 2013, is unmet in about a lakh schools thirteen years later, and the state answers it with a national average - a category error, since a mean of 25:1 is consistent with any distribution, including this one. The Committee states the mechanism plainly: a ratio norm 'took the focus away from the importance of having one teacher per grade', and is satisfiable by a small school with one teacher and thirty children spread across five grades. That is not a hypothetical: 91 per cent of single-teacher schools are primary schools, two-thirds of Standard I and II classrooms are multigrade, and the Committee notes that a single teacher's illness closes the school. The supply explanation does not hold everywhere either - the states with the highest shares include several with adequate supply of qualified teachers, which makes it a deployment failure rather than a shortage. And the improvement is partly not one: single-teacher schools fell by about 17,000 across three years while the total school stock fell by roughly 5,000 and zero-enrolment schools by 4,631, so part of the reduction is closure and consolidation - a split no source decomposes, while the Ministry attributes the whole of it to 'conscious and meaningful governmental interventions'. Finally the indicator itself did not exist in the national system before FY2022-23, so its first published value sits exactly on the reporting-base shift with a six-year hole behind it covering most of T1.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Assessed failed against the objective stated in the statute - the Schedule's staffing norm maintained in each school by 31 March 2013 - rather than against any programme claim. The direction of travel is positive and is carried in caseFor; the duty is nonetheless unmet.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The share of schools complying with the RTE staffing norm, since 2015-16 — Published annually by DISE/NUEPA through 2015-16 and never by its successor system, which holds school-level enrolment and teacher counts for every school in the country. The Department's only compliance-type statement is a national mean, which cannot discharge a school-level duty. This is the cleanest 'the state stopped measuring it' finding in the domain. **What would close it:** A UDISE+ table computing the norm school by school, from data already collected

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Schools with no teacher at all — UDISE+ reports zero-ENROLMENT schools and never zero-TEACHER schools; no such count appears in any edition from 2019-20 to 2024-25. **What would close it:** An RTI to DoSEL for the count of schools reporting zero teachers in UDISE+, or failing that the school-level teacher-count file. The field is collected; the count is derivable. RTI may decline to compile, which makes the file the stronger ask.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** The split between deployment improvement and school closure in the fall in single-teacher schools — Single-teacher schools fell by about 17,000 across three years while total schools fell by roughly 5,000 and zero-enrolment schools by 4,631. No source distinguishes a second teacher arriving from a school ceasing to exist, while the official gloss attributes the whole reduction to policy.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Any all-India single-teacher school figure for 2016-17 to 2021-22 — The predecessor series stopped after 2015-16 and the successor did not begin until 2022-23 - a six-year hole spanning most of T1 in the sharpest deployment indicator there is. That an outside body could compute 9.5 per cent from 2021-22 microdata establishes the data existed and was simply not published. **What would close it:** A research-access request to DoSEL for UDISE+ school-level files, 2016-17 to 2021-22. CETE's computation of 9.5 per cent from the 2021-22 microdata establishes both that the data exists and that access has precedent.

**Series this rests on.** single-teacher-schools-udise, single-teacher-schools-share-dise, schools-above-rte-ptr-primary-dise, ptr-primary-udise, ptr-elementary-dise, govt-schools-count

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-63, P-61

**Sources** (5, T1×4 T2×1):

Retrieved through a pinned resolver (www.indiacode.nic.in -> 94.202.207.51; a plain request returns HTTP 000 on this machine) and identified by confirming the item's section index carries Sections 19 “Norms and standards for school” and 25 “Pupil-Teacher Ratio”, and the Schedule. TWO LIMITS STATED: the Act-level item carries the section INDEX, not the section text, so the section numbers and headings are verified here and the operative wording is NOT; and a same-titled item at handle 123456789/13682 is Type: STATE, Location: Delhi, so short title alone does not identify this Act. — https://www.indiacode.nic.in/handle/123456789/12911
- `T1` UDISE+ Table 2.2, State wise highlights: single-teacher and zero-enrolment schools, editions 2022-23 to 2024-25 — https://web.archive.org/web/20250331172900id_/https://dsel.education.gov.in/sites/default/files/statistics/report_in_PDF/udise_report_existing_23_24.pdf
- `T1` Rajya Sabha Standing Committee on Education, 363rd Report paragraph 3.4.67 and 373rd Report p. 95 — https://bucketapi.rajyasabha.digital/public-bucket/committee_file/ReportFile/16/214/373_2026_3_12.pdf

### B.25 · L-0108 — The West Bengal teacher-recruitment panel annulled, and the anti-cheating law that does not reach it

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — statutory duty scored failed.*

`failed` · T1 · governance/federalism/education · event · 2016–2025-04-03 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-02

**History.** Created 2026-08-02. 1 commit(s) touched it afterwards: 2026-08-04 (whatHappened).

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** The widely quoted count of roughly 25,753 cancelled appointments does not appear in the judgment text and is not carried here. What is established is the scope: the entire 2016 panel across four categories.

**What this is.** On 3 April 2025 the Supreme Court upheld the Calcutta High Court's judgment of 22 April 2024 setting aside en bloc the entire 2016 selection process of the West Bengal Central School Service Commission for Assistant Teachers of Classes IX-X and XI-XII and for non-teaching Group C and Group D staff. Six weeks before the High Court ruled, Parliament had enacted the Public Examinations (Prevention of Unfair Means) Act 2024, whose Schedule does not cover any state school service commission, state public service commission, state teacher eligibility test or state teacher recruitment board.

**What can be established since.** The findings of fact the Court adopted are why segregating tainted from untainted candidates was unavailable: OMR sheets had been destroyed by the Commission's own executive decision of 22 July 2019 without scanned mirror images being preserved; the Commission had supplied purportedly scanned OMR sheets to information applicants from 2018 to 2023 although the investigating agency found none on its server; appointments exceeded declared vacancies in all four categories; appointments were given to persons not on the panel, to persons who submitted blank OMR sheets, and after the panel had expired; the merit list with candidates' marks was never published; the total beneficiaries of the illegalities remain unidentified; and the Commission had applied to create supernumerary posts to accommodate the illegal appointees. The Court's own line of authority requires every effort to segregate and holds en bloc cancellation incompatible with law where segregation is possible - so the remedy fell on candidates the Court did not find to have done anything wrong, and it directed that they may re-sit with age relaxation. The Act’s Schedule names six authorities: the Union Public Service Commission, the Staff Selection Commission, the Railway Recruitment Boards, the Institute of Banking Personnel Selection, Ministries or Departments of the Central Government for recruitment of their own staff, and the National Testing Agency. Coverage therefore follows the conducting authority, not the employer - so a teacher-recruitment examination conducted by NTA for Kendriya Vidyalayas falls inside the Act while the Central Teacher Eligibility Test, conducted by CBSE for the same schools, falls outside it. No state school service commission, no state public service commission, no state teacher eligibility test and no state teacher recruitment board is listed, and the Act’s own Statement of Objects and Reasons says it "shall serve as a model draft for States to adopt at their discretion". Of the teacher-examination failures in this period - West Bengal 2016, Bihar TRE-3, UPTET 2021, REET 2021, the Rajasthan Senior Teacher paper of December 2022, Maharashtra TET 2018 and 2020, Haryana HTET 2015, Gujarat TAT 2018 and Odisha OTET 2019 - the central Act would have reached none. The Union's other instrument against West Bengal in the same period was fiscal rather than legal: central release under the employment guarantee was stopped under section 27 of that Act from March 2022 and stayed stopped for more than four years (L-0168).

**Strongest case for.** The system worked, slowly and at cost, but it worked: a fraudulent recruitment was investigated, litigated through two courts, and annulled, with a fresh selection process ordered and relief for candidates who had done nothing wrong. That is the constitutional machinery functioning against a state government, and the Supreme Court modified the High Court's directions rather than rubber-stamping them. On the central law, the exclusion of state recruitment is not an oversight but the constitutional position stated openly in the Statement of Objects and Reasons: the Bill 'shall serve as a model draft for States to adopt at their discretion. This would aid States in preventing the criminal elements from disrupting conduct of their State level public examinations.' Public examinations conducted by state bodies are for states to police, and the Union legislated for the sphere it controls.

**Strongest case against.** An entire year's teacher recruitment for the secondary and higher-secondary schools of a large state was voided nine years after it was held, and the reason segregation was impossible is that the recruiting authority destroyed the evidence by its own executive decision - so the remedy necessarily punished the innocent along with the rest, and the posts returned to the vacancy pool that the same state's schools were already short against. The Union's answer to examination fraud, enacted six weeks before the High Court judgment and fourteen months before the Supreme Court's, does not reach it: the Schedule covers the Union Public Service Commission, the Staff Selection Commission, railway recruitment boards, banking personnel selection, central ministries recruiting their own staff, and the National Testing Agency - and no state body, not even the board that conducts the central teacher eligibility test. The Act therefore reaches teacher recruitment only where the Union is itself the recruiter, which is the 0.2 per cent of schools it runs. That is the same federal boundary that prevents the Union from counting teacher vacancies reappearing as a gap in enforcement: the largest teacher-recruitment fraud in Indian history was a state process, and the national law was written to leave state processes to state discretion.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Assessed failed against the recruitment process's own object - appointing teachers on merit - which the Court's adopted findings establish it did not achieve. The record also carries the coverage gap in the central law, which is a separate institutional finding tied to the same event.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** The number of appointments cancelled and the number of beneficiaries of the illegalities — The Court's adopted findings record that 'total beneficiaries of the illegalities are yet to be identified', and the judgment states no headline count of cancelled appointments. The primary evidence - the OMR sheets - was destroyed by the recruiting authority in 2019 and no mirror images were preserved, so the count is not recoverable from the original records.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** State teacher-recruitment examination integrity as a national statistic — The central Act’s Schedule names six conducting authorities and excludes every state recruitment and eligibility body, and CBSE with them, so no national reporting obligation attaches to state teacher recruitment examinations and no national series of cancellations, leaks or annulments exists. Three of the five state anti-paper-leak Acts - Haryana 2021, Uttarakhand 2023, Jharkhand 2023 - were drafted around the hiring examination and do not reach the licensing examination either; only Uttar Pradesh 2024, by writing "either qualifying or competitive" into its definition, and Gujarat 2023, by naming the State Examination Board in its Schedule, do. This is a coverage absence rather than a data absence, and it is recorded because it explains why the vacancy absence persists: the same federal boundary runs through both.

**Series this rests on.** teacher-vacancy-rate-ssa

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-64

**Sources** (3, T1×3):

- `T1` State of West Bengal v. Baishakhi Bhattacharyya (Chatterjee) and Others, 2025 INSC 437, 3 April 2025, per Sanjiv Khanna CJI, paragraphs 49 and 51 — https://www.sci.gov.in/
- `T1` The Public Examinations (Prevention of Unfair Means) Act 2024, Act No. 1 of 2024, the Schedule (see section 2(k)) and the Statement of Objects and Reasons, paragraph 4 — https://www.indiacode.nic.in/
- `T1` The Public Examinations (Prevention of Unfair Means) Act, 2024 (Act 1 of 2024), assented 12.02.2024, in force 21.06.2024 - the Schedule — https://www.indiacode.nic.in/bitstream/123456789/20100/1/A2024-01.pdf

### B.26 · L-0110 — Has militancy in Jammu and Kashmir declined since 2019, and on which measure

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — contested on a missing-evidence ground.*

`contested` · T2 · defence/kashmir · lens defence-sector · episode · 2019-08–2026-03 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-02 · contested ground: evidence-withheld

**History.** Created 2026-08-03. 2 commit(s) touched it afterwards: 2026-08-04 (lenses) · 2026-08-05 (sources).

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** MHA has published 614, 417 and 228 as the number of incidents in calendar 2018, and 594, 255 and 153 for 2019, with no methodology note accompanying either change. No comparison spanning the Annual Report 2021-22 and 2022-23 boundary compares like with like.

**What this is.** The government's central claim about Jammu and Kashmir after August 2019 is that militancy has collapsed. MHA's own table shows terrorist-initiated incidents falling from 228 (2018) to 28 (2024), security-force deaths from 91 to 31, militants killed from 257 to 67, and civilians killed from 55 to 14 in 2023 before rising to 26 in 2024. The counting instrument that produces those figures was rewritten twice by the ministry publishing them, in 2022 and 2023, with no methodology note in either report, and the two independent registers that could have produced a competing count - a legislature that could question police and public order, and a functioning local press - were removed over the same period. The record does not choose between the readings.

**What was claimed at announcement.** MHA Annual Report 2024-25 para 15.9, verbatim: 'The security situation of UT of J&K has improved significantly. Organized stone pelting and protests have become things of the past.' The stated approach, from RS Unstarred Q.384 of 6 December 2023: 'The Government has a policy of zero tolerance against terrorism. The approach of the Government is to dismantle the terror eco-system.'

**What can be established since.** Totals computed from the AR 2024-25 rows for 2018-2024: 835 terrorist-initiated incidents, 731 encounters and counter-terror operations, 369 security-force personnel killed, 249 civilians killed, 1,142 militants killed. Infiltration attempts fell from 419 (2017) to 53 (2022), after which MHA stopped publishing the table. Tourist visits are offered as the normalcy proxy at 2.11 crore (2023) and 2.36 crore (2024), and President's Rule was revoked by S.O.4484(E) dated 13 October 2024. Against that: MHA has published 614, 417 and 228 as the number of incidents in calendar 2018, and 594, 255 and 153 for 2019, with no note accompanying either change; the trend is not monotonic on its own series (2021 at 129 above 2020 at 126); and the Pahalgam attack of 22 April 2025 killed 26 civilians in one incident, more than MHA's entire recorded civilian toll for 2023. MHA Annual Report 2025-26 was not located, so the whole of calendar 2025 - including the government's own classification of Pahalgam - sits outside the primary series here.

**Strongest case for.** Every measure the state publishes moves the same way and the largest falls are in the measures hardest to manufacture. Terrorist-initiated incidents fell 87.7 per cent between 2018 and 2024. Militants killed fell from 257 to 67, which is what a shrinking adversary looks like, and you cannot redefine your way to fewer bodies. Infiltration attempts fell from 419 to 53 and net estimated infiltration from 136 to 14. Above all, security-force deaths fell from 91 to 31 - and this is the government's strongest number precisely because it is the series the state has least incentive and least ability to understate: the dead are named, pensioned and publicly honoured, and no critic located in this phase offers a competing count. The independent press-derived register agrees with MHA on this measure year after year, within about ten per cent (91/95 in 2018, 80/78 in 2019, 63/56 in 2020, 42/45 in 2021). The restructuring objection also has a limit that fairness requires stating: the Annual Report table from 2022-23 onward does include civilians killed in counter-terrorism operations, per its own footnote, so the charge that the frame excludes one side's harm holds against the parliamentary series and against most political usage but not against the current table. And the corroboration extends beyond violence data: 2.36 crore tourist visits in 2024, an Amarnath Yatra completed by 5.12 lakh pilgrims, an assembly election held and President's Rule revoked.

**Strongest case against.** The instrument was rewritten mid-series by the party being measured, and rewritten for this theatre alone. MHA restated the incident series for 2017, 2018 and 2019 in the Annual Report 2021-22 with no note, then split it into two columns in the Annual Report 2022-23 with no note, then revised the civilian figures upward for 2018-2020 with no note - while the Left Wing Extremism table, in the same reports, kept an unchanged header throughout. The fall from 228 to 28 is read in public against a series that used to report 614 for the same year. Neither instrument has ever counted civilians killed by security forces outside counter-terrorism operations at all, and neither separates the two causes it now merges. The count itself is generated at the point of the killing by the force that did the killing, and the body is then removed to a distant graveyard: Amshipora put three civilian labourers into the 2020 militants-killed figure for eleven weeks. The verification machinery is inert - 506 magisterial inquiries since 1990, one completed and that report never submitted, no convictions - and both instruments that could have produced an independent count were removed over the same period: the J&K assembly did not sit from 2018 to 2024 and cannot legislate on police and public order now, the Kashmir Press Club closed in January 2022, and at least seven named journalists were held under anti-terror law between 2018 and 2023. And the trend is not the clean line it is quoted as: targeted killings of minorities and migrant workers rose in 2021-22, and one attack at Pahalgam in April 2025 killed more civilians than the government's own count records for the whole of 2023, in the district and the sector used as the normalcy proxy.

**Different facts — flag is TRUE.** The government's case is a set of quantities. The critics' case is a claim about the instrument that produced them: that it was restated twice without notice by the ministry publishing it, restructured for this theatre alone, and that the two registers capable of contradicting it - a legislature competent over police and public order, and a local press - ceased to operate over the same window. No single measure exists, or could be constructed from what is published, that places 'terrorist-initiated incidents fell from 228 to 28' and 'the counting instrument was rewritten by the party being measured and the independent checks were removed' on one ledger; the second is not a quantity. Granting the government its strongest fact in full - the fall in security-force deaths, which the independent register corroborates - leaves the critics' case untouched, and granting the critics their strongest fact does not make any published number wrong. This is the phase's structural finding rather than a feature of this record alone: for six of the nine quantities examined, a two-sided account of the conflict is an assembly the researcher makes and no source makes.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Scored contested on the written definition - the evidence supports more than one defensible reading and the record does not choose. The split verdict below is deliberate and must not be collapsed: on the incident measures the two sides are working from different published numbers for the same object, while on tourism, turnout and Pahalgam they are weighting facts both accept.

**Re-test when.** MHA Annual Report 2025-26, which will carry the government's own classification of the Pahalgam attack of 22 April 2025 and the first full-year 2025 figures. It was not located in this phase and calendar 2025 is therefore outside every series here.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** An independent, non-state count of incidents in Jammu and Kashmir for 2020 onward — The one press-derived register that covers the period, SATP, publishes no methodology page, codebook or definitions page of any kind - its site structure was enumerated and none exists - and gave no HTTP response in this phase, so its figures arrive at two removes. ACLED can in principle carry the quantity but its India coverage begins only on 1 January 2016, it defaults fatalities to zero where the source is unclear, and an export was not obtained. **What would close it:** A registered ACLED API export for Jammu and Kashmir, read against the de-published ACLED methodology note on its 2016-2018 India coverage revision, which would establish whether the earliest years carry an undocumented within-series discontinuity.

**Series this rests on.** jk-terrorist-incidents-legacy, jk-terrorist-incidents-merged, jk-terrorist-initiated-incidents, jk-encounters-ct-ops, jk-security-forces-killed, jk-security-forces-killed-satp, jk-militants-killed, jk-civilians-killed-composite, jk-civilians-killed-satp, jk-infiltration-attempts, jk-net-estimated-infiltration

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-71, P-72, P-73, P-75, P-78, P-85, P-86

**Sources** (4, T1×3 T4×1):

- `T1` MHA Annual Report 2024-25 (AREnglish_24032026.pdf), paras 15.5-15.9, J&K violence table; and Annual Reports 2013-14 to 2023-24, retrieved as PDFs from the MHA annual-reports index — https://www.mha.gov.in/en/documents/annual-reports
- `T1` MHA, Rajya Sabha Unstarred Question No. 371, answered 20 July 2022 - 'Terrorist Incidents' 417/255/244/229 and the bounded civilian formula — https://www.mha.gov.in/MHA1/Par2017/pdfs/par2022-pdfs/RS20072022/371.pdf
- `T1` MHA, Rajya Sabha Unstarred Question No. 228, answered 24 July 2024, 'LAW AND ORDER IN J&K', (Source: UT of J&K) — https://www.mha.gov.in/MHA1/Par2017/pdfs/par2024-pdfs/RS24072024/228.pdf
- `T4` South Asia Terrorism Portal, Jammu and Kashmir fatalities datasheet - relayed through Internet Archive snapshots; satp.org gave no HTTP response in this phase DEEP-LINKED 2026-08-05 (correction cycle 8): satp.org now answers on a pinned resolver (www.satp.org -> 104.21.34.220) and the datasheet was retrieved, ending the no-HTTP-response condition recorded above. The page carries the column header verbatim as described: 'Year \| Incidents of Killing \| Civilians \| Security Forces \| Terrorists/Insurgents/Extremists \| Not Specified \| Total'. Checked at EVERY point the two SATP series carry (2014, 2016-2022): eight of eight security-force values and seven of eight civilian values match the live datasheet exactly. ONE DIVERGENCE, LEFT UNCHANGED: civilians 2022 is 28 in this corpus, taken from the Internet Archive snapshot, against 30 on the live page. NO VALUE IS ALTERED - the recorded figures remain the archived vintage, and substituting the live vintage would replace one dated reading with another without establishing which SATP intends. THE TIER IS UNCHANGED: retrieval improved, the source did not, and the figures still derive from the archive snapshot. — https://www.satp.org/datasheet-terrorist-attack/fatalities/india-jammukashmir

### B.27 · L-0114 — Pellet-firing shotguns in Kashmir: the deaths counted, the injuries refused

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — contested on a missing-evidence ground.*

`contested` · T1 · governance/kashmir · episode · 2016-07-08–2026-07-30 · confidence medium · last researched 2026-08-02 · contested ground: evidence-withheld

**History.** Created 2026-08-03. 2 commit(s) touched it afterwards: 2026-08-05 (summary, caseAgainst, differentFactsNote, caveat) · 2026-08-05 (caveat).

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** CORRECTED 2026-08-05 (adversarial triage 2): the summary previously read "the only pellet quantity any government has ever published". That was contradicted by this record's own next field, which reports the Jammu and Kashmir Chief Minister giving 6,221 injured by pellets among five quantities, and it dropped a hedge the preceding sentence had used correctly. Restated to what was located, in a Union publication. Two counterfactual claims - that nobody, and that no instrument of any provenance, has ever measured deaths avoided - are bounded to what this phase searched. No figure changed and the score is unchanged. Seventeen deaths over three calendar years is the only pellet quantity located in this phase in a Union government publication, and it was published in the same answer that refused the injury count. (This sentence carried the same universal claim as the summary and was MISSED by the first correction pass on 2026-08-05, whose search string required the word 'ever' that this sentence omits.) It is not a measure of the harm and must never render as one. The injury aggregates in circulation - 6,221, 1,726, about 4,000, 2,942 - are five different objects on five different windows, none of which states whether it counts persons, eyes, admissions or referrals.

**What this is.** On 7 February 2018 MHA answered a Rajya Sabha question that asked for the number of protesters injured or killed by pellet guns in Jammu and Kashmir. It supplied a month-by-month table of deaths for calendar 2015 to 2017 - seventeen protesters killed, and two security-force personnel killed in law-and-order protests - and refused everything else in one sentence: 'Further details in the matter cannot be disclosed in the interest of the national security.' That single table is the only official Indian document located in this phase that puts harm to civilians and harm to the state's own personnel side by side on one page. It is the only pellet quantity located in this phase in a UNION government publication. It is not the only pellet quantity any government has published, and this record reports the counter-example itself: the Jammu and Kashmir Chief Minister gave the Legislative Assembly bounded injury aggregates twenty-six days earlier, set out below. Those are a separate official publication on a different period and a different basis - not another reading of this series. The injuries, the munitions expended and the metallurgy of the pellets were all refused under the same exemption on the same afternoon.

**What can be established since.** The word 'pellet' occurs zero times in every MHA Annual Report from 2013-14 to 2024-25. Outside the flagship, figures exist and were produced under compulsion rather than published. The J&K Chief Minister told the Legislative Assembly, in a written reply reported on 12 January 2018 - twenty-six days before MHA's refusal - that between 8 July 2016 and 27 February 2017 in the Kashmir division 51 people were killed, 9,042 injured, 6,221 injured by pellets, 782 with eye injuries and 510 hospitalised. That route closed: the assembly was suspended in June 2018, dissolved in November 2018, and the assembly restored in October 2024 is barred by the Reorganisation Act from legislating on police and public order. In the High Court, on 11 May 2018, the Director of Health Services Kashmir filed a district-wise list of persons who had received pellet injuries, and the Medical Superintendents of SMHS and Bone & Joint Hospitals filed counts, natures of injury and procedures; the judgment of 11 March 2020 reproduces none of the numbers and dismissed the petition. Government Order No.61-Home of 2017 enumerated permanent and partial disablement over a 118-day window ending 2 November 2016 - a boundary the assembly's own figure crosses - and sanctioned Rs 0.75 lakh to 54 persons and Rs 0.50 lakh to 10. The CRPF told the same court it had fired 1.3 million pellets, 8,650 tear-smoke shells and 2,671 plastic pellets in the 32 days from 8 July 2016, so consumption returns demonstrably exist. The weapon was never withdrawn: MHA's own most recent authoritative statement describes an escalation sequence in which other munitions are used 'before the use of Pellet Guns', and on 30 July 2026 the Supreme Court declined a blanket ban on the ground that a Bureau of Police Research and Development advisory permits use in exceptional crowd-control circumstances.

**Strongest case for.** The choice was never between the pellet gun and nothing; it was between the pellet gun and a rifle, and the CRPF said so on affidavit to the High Court: 'In case this (pellet shotgun) is withdrawn, the CRPF would have no recourse in extreme situations but to open fire with rifles, which may cause more fatalities.' Before 2010 that was the recourse. The comparison is the state's own and it is stark: in roughly four months of unrest in 2010, policed largely without this weapon, around 112 civilians died; in the far larger and longer unrest of 2016-17, with 1.3 million pellets fired in the first 32 days alone, the Chief Minister told the Assembly that 51 were killed in the Kashmir division. More unrest, more force applied, fewer deaths - and the High Court found the same on the pleadings before it: 'because of use of Pellet Guns even though more protests become violent the loss of life is less'. The lethality figure is not an estimate but the government's own month-by-month series: seventeen deaths across three calendar years. Nor was the weapon adopted casually. It was ballistically tested at the Terminal Ballistics Research Laboratory on gelatin blocks selected to react as human tissue does; within eighteen days of the unrest beginning the Union government constituted a seven-member Expert Committee on alternatives, which reported within five weeks and whose recommendations MHA told Parliament had been taken into account; the result is a published escalation ladder placing PAVA, STUN-LAC and tear-smoke before the pellet gun. Two judicial forums have examined the demand for a ban on full argument and refused it, and the state paid ex-gratia for disablement without being ordered to.

**Strongest case against.** The weapon cannot be aimed. One cartridge releases between 360 and 600 metal pellets which scatter unpredictably, and the most a senior officer on the government's own Expert Committee would claim is that around 90 per cent strike below the waist - which concedes that roughly one in ten does not, at a rate of 1.3 million pellets in 32 days. The eyes are what is above the waist. The consequence has been measured, by government doctors, in peer-reviewed journals, using stated definitions, which is more than any government count offers: in one Srinagar hospital in four months of 2016, 777 patients were admitted with pellet-related ocular injuries, 76.3 per cent of eyes had open-globe injury, and final best-corrected visual acuity was counting fingers or worse in 82.4 per cent of eyes; in a prospective series at the same hospital 10.8 per cent of eyes had no perception of light at all; mean age in both is about 22. Amnesty International documented 88 people whose eyesight was damaged between 2014 and 2017, of whom 14 were women hit inside their own homes. And 'non-lethal' is the state's own word for a weapon that on the state's own count killed seventeen people. The trade-off argument requires knowing how many deaths were avoided, and no instrument located in this phase measures it. What has been measured is the harm - and the ministry that publishes the deaths refused to publish the harm on national-security grounds twenty-six days after its own state government had published the aggregate in a legislature, and four months before the same state's health department filed a district-wise injury list on affidavit. The two bodies that might have produced an independent count are gone: the State Human Rights Commission, which had produced the only severity taxonomy in existence, was extinguished on 31 October 2019, and the Assembly that extracted the only aggregate has been barred from the subject matter since. The one human-rights body that survives may not investigate the CRPF at all.

**Different facts — flag is TRUE.** The security case's load-bearing quantity is deaths avoided by substituting the shotgun for the rifle. No instrument located in this phase measures it, and the 2010-versus-2016 comparison that stands in for it compares different windows, different unrest intensities and different deployments, on two figures neither of which has an established basis. The critics' load-bearing quantity is persons injured and blinded, which was specifically requested of MHA and specifically refused, produced once to a court and never reproduced, and never defined by any state body on any threshold. So one side's central number is unmeasured and the other's was withheld. Three further factual disputes sit unresolved beneath that: whether the crowd-control SOP was followed, on which the Director General of Police and the CRPF told the same court incompatible things and no forum decided between them; who was hit, on which the district-wise list that would bear on it remains in a closed case file; and what the operative rule actually is, since the restriction the government relies on lives in an unpublished report. Classing this as different weightings would be wrong, and classing it as a factual dispute between two measured claims would also be wrong.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Contested on the written definition, and the contest is not the one usually described. It is not mainly a values disagreement about proportionality: the state holds the measurements for both sides of its own trade-off, publishes the term favourable to it and refuses the term unfavourable to it.

**Re-test when.** Production of the counter-affidavits in WP(C)(PIL) no. 14/2016, in particular the Director of Health Services Kashmir's district-wise injury list filed in answer to the order of 11 May 2018; or publication of the Expert Committee report of 30 August 2016, which would establish for the first time what the government's own rule on firing the weapon actually is.

**Not measured — stated reason `withheld` (stated reason DISPUTED, evidentiary).** Persons injured by pellet-firing shotguns in Jammu and Kashmir, year-wise — MHA was asked for the injured and the killed together by Prof. M. V. Rajeev Gowda in Rajya Sabha Unstarred Question No. 511, answered 7 February 2018, and answered, verbatim, that further details cannot be disclosed in the interest of national security. The stated ground is contradicted by the same government's conduct: the J&K Chief Minister gave the injury aggregate in the Legislative Assembly twenty-six days earlier, and the same government's Director of Health Services filed a district-wise injury list on affidavit four months later. A ministry with a month-by-month pipeline attributing deaths to pellets is working from the same case papers that carry the injuries. **What would close it:** An application to the High Court of Jammu and Kashmir at Srinagar for certified copies of the counter-affidavits in WP(C)(PIL) no. 14/2016 - the affidavit of the Director, Health Services, Kashmir filed in answer to the order of 11 May 2018, and the replies of the Medical Superintendents of SMHS Hospital and Bone & Joint Hospital, Barzulla. Secondarily, an RTI to the Director, Health Services, Kashmir for the district-wise number treated for pellet injuries year-wise, stating the definition and the unit of count. The registry addressee could not be verified in this phase because jkhighcourt.nic.in was unreachable.

**Not measured — stated reason `withheld`.** Pellet cartridges, pellets and PAVA or tear-smoke munitions expended by force and year — The same sentence in the reply of 7 February 2018 refused the munitions sub-question, part (c) of Prof. M. V. Rajeev Gowda’s Rajya Sabha Unstarred Question No. 511. The holder is established: the CRPF stated a 32-day consumption figure to the High Court in August 2016, and a force that can state a 32-day figure keeps consumption returns. **What would close it:** An RTI to the CPIO, Directorate General, Central Reserve Police Force for year-wise consumption of 12-bore pump-action shotgun cartridges and of PAVA and tear-smoke munitions in the Kashmir Zone. The odds are poor - the CRPF is reported to have refused such an application in January 2017 - but a refusal on the record would itself be informative.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** Persons blinded by pellets, on any stated definition — No state body has acknowledged the category. The nearest official vocabulary is 'permanent disablement' and 'partial disablement' under Government Order No.61-Home of 2017, which is neither pellet-specific nor sight-specific nor threshold-defined. If compelled tomorrow no state holder would have a blinding count to produce. **What would close it:** An RTI to the Principal, Government Medical College Srinagar and the Medical Superintendent, SMHS Hospital for the number of patients with pellet-related ocular trauma recorded at a final best-corrected visual acuity below a stated threshold, year-wise - a request that supplies the definition rather than assuming one. A request that merely asks for the number blinded cannot be answered.

**Not measured — stated reason `never-defined`.** An official threshold for blindness, permanent disablement or visual impairment — The government's own vocabulary carries no criterion, no examining authority and no acuity band anywhere it appears. Operable definitions exist in clinical work on this very population - no perception of light, best-corrected acuity of counting fingers or worse - and disagree with each other, producing impairment shares of 10.8, 59.3 and 82.4 per cent on overlapping populations at one hospital. On the narrow object of an official definition, none has ever existed.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The report of the Expert Committee on alternatives to pellet guns, constituted by MHA Memorandum dated 26 July 2016 and submitted 30 August 2016 — MHA has never given a reason. An interim version was placed before the Supreme Court in a sealed cover in 2017. MHA told Parliament on 7 February 2018 that the Committee's recommendations had been taken into account for appropriate implementation, so existence, receipt and reliance are all on the record. **What would close it:** An RTI to the Ministry of Home Affairs for the report of the Expert Committee constituted vide Memorandum dated 26 July 2016, and for the order or communication by which its recommendations were accepted. The Ministry has already pleaded the report's contents in aid of its own position in Parliament, so a refusal would have to explain how a document relied on publicly is undisclosable.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The district-wise pellet-injury list and the hospital-wise counts filed on affidavit in WP(C)(PIL) no. 14/2016 — Nobody asked for publication and none was needed - the documents were filed in answer to court orders and the judgment reproduces none of the numbers. Their authors are named and their contents are described by the Court. The dataset is not missing; it is in a case file that a court closed in March 2020. **What would close it:** Certified copies from the registry of the High Court of Jammu and Kashmir at Srinagar, as above.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The Divisional Commissioner Kashmir's list of persons identified as permanently or partially disabled, and the criteria applied — Sixty-four people were paid on the strength of this list by two named government orders of 5 January and 26 February 2018, so the list plainly exists. No reason for its non-publication has ever been stated. **What would close it:** An RTI to the Divisional Commissioner, Kashmir for the list submitted by communications dated 23 October 2017 and 25 January 2018 pursuant to Government Order No.61-Home of 2017, and for the criteria applied in classifying permanent and partial disablement. Every element of this route - office, document, date and authorising order - is named in a retrieved judgment.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** Injuries caused by crowd-control munitions other than pellets - tear-smoke, PAVA and plastic pellets — No body has acknowledged the category and no official series of any kind was located. Unlike pellets there is not even a refused parliamentary question specific to it: the 2018 question asked for PAVA consumption, not PAVA injuries.

**Series this rests on.** jk-pellet-deaths

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-79, P-80, P-81

**Sources** (5, T1×3 T3×1 T4×1):

- `T1` MHA, Rajya Sabha Unstarred Question No. 511, answered 7 February 2018, 'PROTESTERS KILLED BY PELLET GUNS IN JAMMU AND KASHMIR' - Annex-I month-wise deaths 2015-2017, and the national-security refusal — https://www.mha.gov.in/MHA1/Par2017/pdfs/par2018-pdfs/rs-07022018-ENG/511.pdf
- `T1` MHA, Rajya Sabha Unstarred Question No. 512, answered 7 February 2018, 'PEOPLE KILLED BY PELLETS IN J&K' - TBRL Chandigarh testing; metallurgy and procurement refused — https://www.mha.gov.in/MHA1/Par2017/pdfs/par2018-pdfs/rs-07022018-ENG/512.pdf
- `T1` J&K High Court Bar Association v. Union of India & ors., WP(C)(PIL) no. 14/2016, High Court of Jammu and Kashmir at Srinagar, judgment of 11 March 2020 (Magrey and Thakur JJ) - the affidavits, GO No.61-Home of 2017, the ex-gratia sanctions, and the elided DGP SOP of 25.10.2010 — https://images.assettype.com/barandbench/2020-03/3313c8f2-877c-4245-921d-1141b9c0a9b1/J_K_High_Court_Bar_Association_v_Union_of_India___Ors_pdf.pdf
- `T3` Wei X, Ang BCH, Nazir A, Shah FQ, Qureshi T, Jain A, Natarajan S, 'A series of 777 pellet gun ocular injuries over a 4-month period in Kashmir', Indian Journal of Ophthalmology 2022;70(10):3465-3469 — https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36190028/
- `T4` Kashmir Observer, 30 July 2026 - Supreme Court (CJI Surya Kant) declines a blanket ban while a BPRD advisory permits use in exceptional circumstances — https://kashmirobserver.net/2026/07/30/pellet-guns-cant-be-banned-while-advisory-permits-their-use-sc/
