### B.28 · L-0116 — Burial of encounter dead away from home districts, and who declares the identity of the killed

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — contested on a missing-evidence ground.*

`contested` · T2 · governance/defence/kashmir · lens defence-sector · episode · 2020-04–2026-06-13 · confidence medium · last researched 2026-08-02 · contested ground: evidence-unobservable

**History.** Created 2026-08-03. 1 commit(s) touched it afterwards: 2026-08-04 (lenses).

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** How many bodies have been buried under this practice is not established. Three official figures circulate - 158 and about 180, about 140, and at least 580 - all attributed to official sources, none reconcilable, and no published series exists. No figure should be authored.

**What this is.** Since April 2020 those killed in encounters in Jammu and Kashmir have been buried in remote graveyards rather than released to their families. No written order, circular or SOP establishing the practice has ever been published, and on the police's own account none exists: officials point to generic Disaster Management Act lockdown orders, a senior officer said in the same month that 'it's not a policy now but it should become a policy', and a lawyer acting for a family was told 'no written order has been given to us'. The stated reason moved from public health to prevention of glamorisation without the practice changing, and the practice was still operative in 2026 under an elected J&K government.

**What can be established since.** The practice first appears in the Keran sector operation in Kupwara, 1-6 April 2020, in which five militants were killed, neither police nor Army identified them, and all five were buried at an undisclosed location. The rationale predates COVID: a confidential J&K Crime Branch report of 2017 warned that funerals of local militants romanticise and glamorise them, and public funerals for foreign militants had been prohibited since October 2015. Three official counts of how many bodies have been buried under the practice circulate and do not reconcile - 158 for 2020 and about 180 for 2021 put before the Supreme Court in August 2022, about 140 for 2020 in one investigative account, and 'at least 580' since April 2020 as at February 2023 - and no published series exists. The bearing on counting is demonstrable rather than inferential. Amshipora put three civilians into the 2020 militants-killed figure for eleven weeks, and they were removed only because families 160 km away filed missing-person complaints. The Inspector General's own protocol makes identification conditional on a family learning of the death, obtaining a lockdown travel pass, reaching Srinagar in time and being believed by two District Commissioners, each of which is a filter that can fail without anyone recording that it failed. At Ganderbal in 2026 the police could identify the dead man only because identity papers happened to be on his body, and the family waited 75 days. The Supreme Court on 12 September 2022 set aside a High Court exhumation direction in the Aamir Magray matter while upholding compensation and grave access. The verification machinery behind contested killings is separately inert: the Hyderpora magisterial inquiry ordered on 18 November 2021 with a fifteen-day deadline has never been published, though the Lieutenant Governor sought two days to publish it on 7 December 2021, and a Ganderbal seven-day inquiry in 2026 had not appeared weeks later.

**Strongest case for.** Between 2018 and 2024 the security forces conducted 731 encounters and counter-terror operations and killed 1,142 militants while 369 personnel died in action. Against that volume the number of cases in which identity has been seriously contested and subsequently established as wrong is very small, and Amshipora is the case everyone names precisely because it is exceptional - exceptional in a second way too, because the state established it, the Army opened the inquiry itself and court-martialled the officer. Between 2016 and 2020 militant funerals were the single most effective recruiting instrument in the Valley, not private grief but staged public events with crowds mobilised across a wide radius; the Crime Branch said so in writing in 2017, and ending them is the most direct life-saving measure available. The safeguards are real and were stated with dated worked examples: religious washing and shrouding are performed, post-mortems and DNA are taken, families may identify and participate before a magistrate, and where identity is later established exhumation and handover follow, as at Amshipora in 2020 and Ganderbal in 2026. In Hyderpora the SIT reconstructed the sequence with physical evidence within six weeks and the two civilian bodies were exhumed and returned within three days without litigation.

**Strongest case against.** The base-rate argument assumes the denominator is knowable, and the state has arranged matters so that it is not. The dead are declared militants by the force that killed them, buried in a distant graveyard, unnamed and unphotographed; the check on that declaration is a family that hears in time, travels, is believed and gets DNA run. Amshipora is not a demonstration that the system self-corrects but of what correcting it takes - three simultaneous missing-person complaints, sustained press attention and eleven weeks. Ganderbal in 2026 shows the alternative: identification happened only because papers were in a pocket, and even then the family waited 75 days. Nor does the machinery for testing disputes function: 506 inquiries since 1990, one completed, that report never submitted, no convictions. Hyderpora is the model - an inquiry ordered within three days under public pressure, a fifteen-day deadline, a Lieutenant Governor who promised publication within two days, and no report ever; what was published instead was the police's own account of a police operation, clearing the forces. And a body is not a public-order problem: denying a family the right to bury its dead is a penalty imposed without charge, trial or finding, on people who have done nothing, by no published order that anyone can produce six years on. The COVID justification has outlived COVID by four years, and the police chief said so himself when he listed stopping glamorisation alongside stopping infection.

**Different facts — flag is TRUE.** The state's case rests on a base rate - a very small number of established misidentifications against 731 operations. The critics' case is that the base rate is unmeasurable because of the burial and identification practice itself, and that claim is supported by a retrieved mechanism rather than asserted: identity is declared at the encounter site by the force that conducted it, before independent identification, and the body is then removed to a location that makes contestation costly. So the error in the series is structurally one-directional - mistakes flow from civilian into terrorist and not the reverse, because a body wrongly recorded as a militant is buried far away and unphotographed while a body wrongly recorded as a civilian would be claimed immediately. No measure exists, or could be constructed from what is published, that places the operations count and a declared-unmeasurable misidentification rate on one ledger; the counterpart is not unbuilt but unbuildable while the practice stands. What both sides do agree on is the strongest single fact here: in the one case where identity was conclusively tested, the official record had been wrong, and wrong in the direction of classifying civilians as terrorists.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Contested on the written definition, and the mixture inside it must be preserved rather than averaged. On the burial practice the disagreement is a weighting of one agreed fact; on the safeguards and on Hyderpora the two sides assert incompatible facts that no forum has resolved; and on the general question of identity one side asserts that the other's key quantity cannot be measured at all.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected` (stated reason DISPUTED, normative).** Any written order, circular or SOP establishing the burial practice in force since April 2020 — On the responsible officials' own account no such instrument exists: the Inspector General of Police Kashmir pointed to generic Disaster Management Act lockdown orders that say nothing about the disposal of encounter dead, a senior officer said in the same month that it was not a policy, and a lawyer was told no written order had been given. What is contested is not whether the instrument exists but whether lockdown orders are sufficient authority for it - the administration treats them as sufficient and critics treat the absence of any instrument as the defect. Nobody disputes the facts. **What would close it:** An RTI to the J&K Home Department for the legal authority under which bodies of persons killed in encounters are buried without release to next of kin - framed as a request for the basis rather than for a document, since an RTI for a document officials say does not exist would return a nil reply that does not close the absence. The correct addressee could not be verified because jkhome.nic.in was unreachable.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The findings of magisterial inquiries into contested killings in Jammu and Kashmir — The Hyderpora inquiry was ordered by the Lieutenant Governor on 18 November 2021 with a fifteen-day deadline; families met him on 7 December 2021 and he sought two days to publish it; it has never appeared. Classified not-published rather than withheld on the written test, which turns on producibility under compulsion and not on whether anyone asked: no requester issued a request and no authority issued a refusal. What is recorded is a promise volunteered by the holder and not kept, which is non-release. For the general figure of 506 inquiries since 1990 the position is weaker still and no request-and-refusal is documented at all. Demoted from withheld on 2026-08-03 in the pre-merge verification of this phase, on the same test that demoted two education-phase absences. **What would close it:** An RTI to the District Magistrate, Srinagar for the report of the magisterial inquiry into the Hyderpora encounter of 15 November 2021 conducted by the Additional District Magistrate, and a parallel RTI to the District Magistrate, Ganderbal for the 2026 inquiry report.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The number of bodies buried away from home districts under the practice, year-wise — No body has published a series. Three counts attributed to official sources circulate and do not reconcile, and none has an established basis. **What would close it:** A parliamentary question to MHA for the year-wise number of persons killed in encounters in J&K whose bodies were buried at locations other than their home districts since 1 April 2020, and the number subsequently exhumed and handed over.

**Series this rests on.** jk-militants-killed, jk-civilians-killed-composite, jk-encounters-ct-ops

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-85

**Sources** (3, T1×1 T4×2):

- `T4` Reporting of the Inspector General of Police Kashmir's April 2020 statements, the 2017 J&K Crime Branch report, the counts put before the Supreme Court on 29 August 2022, and the Ganderbal exhumation of 13 June 2026. Relayed throughout: no J&K Police or Home Department document was retrievable in this phase and jkpolice.gov.in and jkhome.nic.in gave no HTTP response — https://www.jkpolice.gov.in/
- `T4` Supreme Court of India, Aamir Magray exhumation matter, order of 12 September 2022 (Surya Kant and Pardiwala JJ) setting aside the High Court exhumation direction while upholding compensation and grave access - judgment not retrieved; indiankanoon.org returned HTTP 403 — https://main.sci.gov.in/
- `T1` MHA Annual Report 2024-25 (AREnglish_24032026.pdf), para 15.6 J&K violence table - the 731 operations and 1,142 militants killed on which the base-rate argument rests — https://www.mha.gov.in/en/documents/annual-reports

### B.29 · L-0122 — AFSPA section 7: fifty sanction requests from J&K, none granted, and a decision rule nobody holds

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — statutory duty scored no-objective — the comparison set.*

`no-objective` · T1 · governance/kashmir · lens defence-sector · institutional · 2018-01-01–2020-06-05 · confidence medium · last researched 2026-08-03

**History.** Created 2026-08-03. 3 commit(s) touched it afterwards: 2026-08-03 (assessment) · 2026-08-03 (assessmentNote, asOf) · 2026-08-04 (lenses). **Verdict moved:** first filed contested ; contested→no-objective on 2026-08-03 — "Apply the rescore: 25 records to no-objective, 4 notes, 4 held red".

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** The 50/0/47/3 figures are strongly corroborated but not primary-retrieved: the parliamentary reply PDF could not be obtained and the annexure listing the 47 individual cases has never been seen.

**What this is.** Section 7 of the Armed Forces (Jammu and Kashmir) Special Powers Act 1990 requires prior Central Government sanction before a member of the armed forces can be prosecuted for anything done in the exercise of the Act's powers. Since 1990 the J&K government has sought sanction in 50 cases. Sanction was refused in 47 and three remained pending as at April 2018. None was granted. When an applicant asked under the Right to Information Act not for the case files but for the procedure, norms, criteria and standards used to decide, both the Ministry of Defence and Army Headquarters answered that they do not hold the information; the Central Information Commission then sealed the case files under the national-security exemption in June 2020.

**What can be established since.** The figures come from a Ministry of Defence reply to Rajya Sabha Unstarred Question No. 1463 of 1 January 2018, corroborated by OHCHR's June 2018 report and by an RTI application reproduced verbatim in a CIC second appeal; the reply PDF itself could not be retrieved and the annexure listing the 47 individual cases has never been seen. On the decision rule the CPIO of the Ministry of Defence answered on 22 February 2018 that the information is not under his custody and should be treated as nil; the Under Secretary (AG-I) that it is not maintained or available in his section; and the CPIO for the Indian Army on 16 April 2018 that it is not available or held with the concerned agency of Headquarters. Both ministries denied custody of the same records. The CIC's final order of 5 June 2020 permitted access to the refusal orders in the 47 cases but refused the case files under section 8(1)(a), reasoning that the facts required a 'dynamic attribution to the meaning of larger public interest' by associating non-disclosure with it, and that 'indisputably, the larger public interest of the State (vis-a-vis its susceptibility to unrest) prevails over that of the alleged victims'. Separately, MHA told the Lok Sabha on 20 March 2018 that there is no proposal to amend or withdraw the 1990 Act. The Act itself is never named in any MHA Annual Report retrieved, and no disturbed-area statement for J&K appears in any of them, while AFSPA 1958 in the North East is reported every year at district granularity as a measurable achievement.

**Strongest case for.** Prior sanction is an ordinary protection for personnel operating under statutory powers in an armed conflict, and it exists so that criminal process cannot be used to paralyse operations or to litigate battlefield judgement in a magistrate's court. It is not immunity from accountability: the armed forces retain their own disciplinary jurisdiction, which in the one case where a staged encounter was established resulted in a Court of Inquiry, a court martial, a murder conviction and dishonourable cashiering - a faster and more complete disposal than the parallel civil prosecution achieved. The CIC did not seal everything: it directed disclosure of the refusal orders themselves, which is the reasoned part of each decision, while declining to open the underlying operational files. And a grant rate of zero over three decades is consistent with a screening mechanism working as intended if the cases put forward did not disclose an offence outside the exercise of the Act's powers.

**Strongest case against.** Fifty requests, forty-seven decided, none granted, over three decades, is not a screening mechanism producing a distribution - it is a determinate answer. And the decision rule that produced it is held by nobody: two arms of the same government each told the statutory information authority that the other holds it and that they do not, which means no written criteria for the exercise of the power exist or can be produced. The appellate body then declined to open the files on a reasoning that puts the State's susceptibility to unrest above the interest of the alleged victims - deciding, on the record, that the public interest lies in non-disclosure. In the one case where the state's own institution established that an encounter was staged, whether a section 7 request was even forwarded could not be established. The result is a provision whose operation cannot be examined, whose criteria do not exist in documentary form, and whose statute is never mentioned in the ministry's own annual account of the theatre where it applies - while the same ministry reports the retreat of the parallel Act in the North East district by district, every year.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Scored no-objective as an institutional record rather than a measure - no objective was stated at announcement for the sanction regime, so the disposal record has no claim to be scored against. The value scores the disposal and the disclosure record, not the merits of any individual refusal, none of which has been seen. VALUE-AND-NOTE RECONCILED 2026-08-03: this record was rescored contested -> no-objective in the pass that introduced no-objective to drain the contested sink, and the note was not rewritten with it, so the reasoning of record argued a value the field had abandoned. Nothing in the validator sees that - both halves are individually valid. The substantive reasoning below stood up and is preserved verbatim; only the value it names has been corrected.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** The procedure, norms, criteria and standards applied in deciding an AFSPA section 7 sanction request — Both the Ministry of Defence and Army Headquarters stated on the record, in answer to a statutory information request, that they do not hold it, and the Commission required an affidavit to that effect. The holders have attested that it does not exist in documentary form, so there is nothing to release.

**Not measured — stated reason `withheld`.** The reasons for refusal in the 47 J&K sanction cases — Disclosure was specifically requested through the statutory route by Venkatesh Nayak, whose RTI and second appeal are on the record, and refused by the Central Information Commission by its order of 5 June 2020 under section 8(1)(a), with reasons on the record associating non-disclosure with the larger public interest. This is an identifiable refusal by an identifiable authority on a stated ground. **What would close it:** A writ petition challenging the CIC order of 5 June 2020, or a parliamentary question for the case-wise grounds of refusal. A fresh RTI would meet the same exemption.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** AFSPA section 7 sanction requests received, granted, refused and pending in respect of J&K since 1 January 2019 — The published figures end at April 2018 and no later statement was located from either ministry. **What would close it:** A parliamentary question to the Ministry of Defence and to MHA for sanction requests received, granted, refused and pending in respect of J&K since 1 January 2019.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The areas of J&K notified as disturbed under the AFSPA (J&K) Act 1990, and the dates of the notifications in force — The 1990 Act is never named in any MHA Annual Report retrieved and no disturbed-area statement for J&K appears in any of them, while the equivalent information for AFSPA 1958 in the North East is volunteered annually at district granularity. MHA administers the Act and the notifications are its own instruments. **What would close it:** A parliamentary question to MHA for the areas of J&K currently notified as disturbed under the AFSPA (J&K) Act 1990 with the dates of the notifications in force - framed on the asymmetry with what MHA publishes for the North East without being asked.

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-82

**Sources** (4, T1×2 T2×1 T4×1):

- `T2` UN OHCHR, Report on the Situation of Human Rights in Kashmir, June 2018, para 53 and footnotes 31, 38, 62, 271 - the 50/0/47/3 sanction figures as stated by MHA to Parliament in February 2018 — https://www.ohchr.org/
- `T4` Ministry of Defence and Indian Army CPIO replies of 22 and 26 February and 16 April 2018, reproduced verbatim in the appellant's second appeal to the Central Information Commission; and the CIC final order of 5 June 2020 refusing the case files under section 8(1)(a), whose extracts are known through press accounts - the order itself was not retrieved — https://cic.gov.in/
- `T1` MHA, Lok Sabha Unstarred Question No. 4063, answered 20 March 2018 - no proposal to amend or withdraw the AFSPA (J&K) Act 1990 — https://sansad.in/
- `T1` MHA Annual Reports 2013-14 through 2024-25 - zero occurrences of the AFSPA (J&K) Act 1990 and no J&K disturbed-area statement in any year, against annual district-level reporting of AFSPA 1958 in the North East (AR 2024-25 para 2.39) — https://www.mha.gov.in/en/documents/annual-reports

### B.30 · L-0123 — Two J&K security quantities lost their only legislative route in 2018, and it did not resume in 2024

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — heavily cited, non-evaluative, no source conflict.*

`no-objective` · T1 · governance/kashmir · institutional · 2018-06-20–2025-10-29 · confidence medium · last researched 2026-08-03

**History.** Created 2026-08-03. 3 commit(s) touched it afterwards: 2026-08-03 (assessment) · 2026-08-03 (whatHappened, assessmentNote, sources, asOf, caveat) · 2026-08-03 (assessmentNote). **Verdict moved:** first filed contested ; contested→no-objective on 2026-08-03 — "Apply the rescore: 25 records to no-objective, 4 notes, 4 held red".

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** No J&K Legislative Assembly proceedings record of any date was retrieved - jklegislativeassembly.nic.in gave no HTTP response - so every assembly answer and every refusal here is relayed through press. It is also not established that no security question has been admitted and answered since 2024: refusals were found, admissions were not searched for exhaustively, and the absence of a found admission is not evidence of none. Since that caveat was written a live J&K Legislative Assembly presence has been found on the National e-Vidhan platform at jkla.neva.gov.in, which resolves and returns HTTP 200 while jklegislativeassembly.nic.in remains NXDOMAIN on multiple resolvers - so the caveat is true as to the host it names. Whether the new host carries the PROCEEDINGS and question answers this record depends on is NOT established; it has been used only for a member directory. Finding the site is not finding the documents.

**What this is.** Two security quantities that MHA does not tabulate at all were on the public record only because a Member of the Legislative Assembly asked and a Chief Minister answered in writing: pellet injuries in the Kashmir division between July 2016 and February 2017, and year-wise local recruitment into militancy for 2015 to 2017. The J&K assembly was kept in suspended animation from 20 June 2018, dissolved on 21 November 2018, and did not sit again until October 2024. The assembly that returned is barred by section 32(1) of the Reorganisation Act from legislating on Police and Public Order, and the Lieutenant Governor may make rules prohibiting questions on matters affecting his own discretionary functions. This is not a gap in a series that resumes; it is the termination of an instrument, and it bit hardest on exactly the quantities the central instrument does not carry.

**What can be established since.** Three refusals in the restored assembly are documented, of unequal weight. In March 2025, at the first budget session in seven years, MLAs were barred from asking questions on terrorism, law and order, All India Services and related matters said to be in the domain of the Central Government, against roughly 1,300 questions submitted - how many were actually rejected, and on what individual grounds, is not established. In March 2025 the Speaker disallowed a discussion on the killing of three civilians at Billawar, Kathua, invoking section 32 and additionally a rule barring debate while a probe is pending. On 28-29 October 2025 the Speaker deleted a question asking the reasons for inordinate delay in ex-gratia and benefits under SRO-43 to the families of victims of violence, on the ground that the Home Department falls under the Lieutenant Governor's jurisdiction, and told legislators generally that they cannot ask questions pertaining to departments under the Lieutenant Governor's control. That third instance is a specifically requested figure, specifically refused, with an identifiable refuser, date, questioner and stated ground, on a question that sits squarely inside the security arc. Whether the Lieutenant Governor has in fact made rules under the proviso to section 46(1), or whether the Speaker's rulings rest on section 32 alone, could not be established, and the two are different things with different routes to challenge. A third quantity belongs with the two above and is better evidenced than either. PSA detention figures were answered on the floor of the same Assembly: in October 2010 the Chief Minister, who also held the Home portfolio, told the House that 724 people had been detained across 2009 and 2010, of which 322 between January and September 2010; and in March 2010 the Home Department, answering an opposition legislator, gave details of 334 persons booked under the PSA between 5 January and 14 February 2010 alone. Carry the contradiction with them - 334 in six weeks against 322 in nine months - because it is the state's own inconsistency showing in its own answers.

**Strongest case for.** The allocation is a straightforward consequence of the constitutional status of a Union Territory with a legislature, and it is the same allocation that applies elsewhere: where police and public order are Union subjects the legislature that does not hold them does not question about them, and the accountability route runs to Parliament, where the Ministry of Home Affairs answers and has in fact produced on request quantities that appear in no annual report - the organised stone-pelting series, the encounter counts, the injured columns. A Speaker's ruling that a department under the Lieutenant Governor is outside the House's questioning competence is an application of the statute, not a suppression of it, and one of the three instances was additionally justified on a pending-probe rule that would apply in any legislature. The Speaker himself has argued publicly for the narrower reading of the rule-making power, that it can only restrict matters requiring the Lieutenant Governor's discretionary action and only after consulting him.

**Strongest case against.** The two quantities that make this concrete are quantities the Union instrument does not carry. Pellet injuries and local recruitment into militancy appear nowhere in seventeen MHA Annual Reports; they entered the public record because an assembly could compel a written answer, and there is now no forum in J&K that can. Parliament is not an equivalent substitute, because MHA is simultaneously the answering ministry and the publisher of the figures - the producer and the consumer of the numbers became the same ministry on 31 October 2019 - and the one time MHA was asked directly for the pellet injury figure it refused on national-security grounds. The pattern of rulings since October 2024 runs toward the broad reading of the bar: three documented refusals in twelve months, one of them on compensation to families of victims of violence. And the underlying question of whether the bar arises from rules actually made or from the Speaker's own construction of competence has never been answered.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Scored no-objective, and confined to answerability as it bears on whether security quantities can be counted - no objective was stated for the allocation of legislative competence, so there is nothing to score it against. The assembly, the elections and the restoration of statehood are outside this record's subject and are not scored here. SCOPE, made precise rather than weakened: the termination is SUBJECT-MATTER-SPECIFIC, not chamber-wide. It holds for security because s.32(1) removes Police and Public Order. It does not hold for revenue subjects: domicile and land sit within the UT legislature's competence - MHA told the Rajya Sabha on 11 December 2019 that the powers to make laws relating to land and property are vested with the Legislative Assembly of the Union Territory - and the restored Assembly demonstrably worked the route twice in 2025, extracting 83,742 domicile certificates granted to non-state subjects in April 2025 and 631 non-resident land buyers over 386 kanal in October 2025. That makes this a finding about s.32(1) rather than about the Assembly. VALUE-AND-NOTE RECONCILED 2026-08-03: this record was rescored contested -> no-objective in the pass that introduced no-objective to drain the contested sink, and the note was not rewritten with it, so the reasoning of record argued a value the field had abandoned. Nothing in the validator sees that - both halves are individually valid. The substantive reasoning below stood up and is preserved verbatim; only the value it names has been corrected.

**Not measured — stated reason `withheld`.** The reasons for delay in ex-gratia and benefits under SRO-43 to families of victims of violence in J&K — A question tabled by Sheikh Khursheed, MLA for Langate (Awami Ittehad Party), was deleted by Speaker Abdul Rahim Rather on 28-29 October 2025 on the ground that the Home Department falls under the Lieutenant Governor's jurisdiction. That is a specifically requested figure, specifically refused, with an identifiable refuser, date, questioner and stated ground. **What would close it:** A parliamentary question to MHA, which is now the only legislative route to this subject matter: no absence on a J&K security quantity can be given the J&K assembly as its closing route, because that route does not exist for this subject.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Whether the Lieutenant Governor has made rules under the proviso to section 46(1) clause (c) of the Reorganisation Act — The reporting that raised the power in January 2025 was prospective, and the October 2025 refusal was justified by reference to departmental jurisdiction rather than to any rules made. A bar arising from made rules and a bar arising from the Speaker's own construction of legislative competence are different things with different routes to challenge, and no document establishing which is operating was located. **What would close it:** An RTI to the Secretary, J&K Legislative Assembly for any rules made under the proviso to section 46(1) of the J&K Reorganisation Act 2019 and the date of the Speaker's consultation. The addressee could not be verified in this phase.

**Sources** (6, T1×2 T4×4):

- `T1` J&K Reorganisation Act 2019, ss.32(1) and 46(1) proviso (c), verbatim from the Gazette of India Extraordinary No. 53 of 9 August 2019, MD5 0e16a53f5e362636f3d65294e562259f, independently re-verified. s.32(1) excludes entries 1 and 2 of the State List, 'Public Order' and 'Police'. The proviso reads that the Lieutenant Governor SHALL, after consultation with the Speaker, make rules prohibiting questions on matters affecting his functions - 'shall', not 'may', so the absence of such rules would itself be a fact worth having — https://egazette.gov.in/WriteReadData/2019/210407.pdf
- `T4` The Wire, 5 January 2025, Umer Maqbool - the text of the proviso to section 46(1) clause (c) of the J&K Reorganisation Act 2019 and the competing readings of its reach by a Rules Committee member and by the Speaker - the competing readings of the proviso's reach by a Rules Committee member and by the Speaker — https://m.thewire.in/article/government/how-lgs-powers-could-pose-a-challenge-to-the-scrutiny-of-human-rights-issues-in-jk-assembly
- `T4` The Tribune, 29 October 2025 - the Speaker deletes a question on delayed SRO-43 ex-gratia to families of victims of violence and tells MLAs they cannot question departments under the Lieutenant Governor — https://www.tribuneindia.com/news/j-k/speaker-asks-mlas-not-to-put-up-questions-related-to-departments-under-l-g
- `T4` Kashmir Dot Com, 2 March 2025 - MLAs barred from questions on terrorism, law and order and All India Services at the first budget session in seven years — https://kashmirdotcom.in/2025/03/02/jk-assembly-barred-from-home-ais-queries-mlas-to-prioritize-works-power-ic-rdd/
- `T4` Deccan Herald - the Speaker disallows a debate on the Billawar/Kathua civilian killings citing the Reorganisation Act. Direct fetch returned HTTP 403; content known through search extraction and graded accordingly — https://www.deccanherald.com/india/jammu-and-kashmir/jammu-and-kashmir-assembly-speaker-denies-debate-on-kathua-killings-citing-reorganisation-act-3439793
- `T1` MHA Annual Report 2024-25 (AREnglish_24032026.pdf), para 15.5 - revocation of President's Rule by S.O.4484(E) dated 13 October 2024, the only primary-retrieved date in this record — https://www.mha.gov.in/en/documents/annual-reports

### B.31 · L-0131 — The Public Safety Act was retained on the appointed day, adapted by executive order, and its Advisory Board transferred to executive selection

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — heavily cited, non-evaluative, no source conflict.*

`no-objective` · T2 · governance/kashmir · institutional · 2019-10-31–2026 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-03

**History.** Created 2026-08-03. Never edited since.

**What this is.** The Jammu and Kashmir Public Safety Act 1978 survived the reorganisation at Fifth Schedule Table-4 entry 123, in the table of State Acts that remain in force. It was then adapted from State to Union Territory by executive order S.O. 1229(E) of 31 March 2020 made under section 96 of the Reorganisation Act, which also omitted the proviso to section 10 barring the lodging of permanent residents outside the territory and replaced the Advisory Board appointment provision. Selection of the Board that reviews detention orders now sits with a committee of three executive officers chaired by the Chief Secretary.

**What can be established since.** The composition qualification for the Advisory Board is untouched - the Chairman is or has been a High Court judge - and what changed is who chooses the person. Under the 1978 text members were appointed by the Government 'in consultation with the Chief Justice of the High Court'. On 22 May 2018 the Jammu and Kashmir (Preventive Detention Laws) Order 2018 removed the requirement to consult the Chief Justice, a change recorded by OHCHR in July 2019. On 31 March 2020 S.O. 1229(E) entry 104 substituted a new section 14(3) under which the Chairman and Members are appointed by the Government on the recommendations of a Search-cum-Selection Committee of the Chief Secretary as Chairman, the Additional Secretary of the Home Department and the Administrative Secretary of the Law Department, with judicial consultation surviving only where a sitting judge is to be appointed. The Home Department is the parent department of the detaining authorities. The same order substituted 'the Union territory of Jammu and Kashmir' for 'the State' throughout, and replaced the Ranbir Penal Code and the Samvat 1989 Criminal Procedure Code references with their central equivalents. The administration told the High Court that the Act 'shall be deemed to have been passed by the Parliament under Article 22(7)' - a claim that the Fifth Schedule converted a 1978 State enactment into parliamentary legislation, which matters because Article 22(7) is the clause under which Parliament, and not a State legislature, may prescribe detention beyond three months without an Advisory Board opinion. In May 2026 the High Court of Jammu and Kashmir and Ladakh upheld the adaptation in Tanveer Ahmad Mir v. Union Territory of J&K, 2026:JKLHC-SR:1587, holding that it fell within the power Parliament itself delegated by section 96, and dismissed the petition. Three press accounts date that judgment to 15, 16 and 18 May 2026 and the judgment itself was not retrieved, so no date for it is asserted here.

**Strongest case for.** Parliament made a deliberate legislative choice about a territory for which it had just assumed direct responsibility, and preventive detention is not an aberration in Indian constitutional law: Article 22 of the Constitution itself contemplates detention without trial and prescribes the safeguards that attend it. The theatre in which the Act operates is not hypothetical - 2008, 2010 and above all 2016 are documented instances of mass mobilisation converting rapidly into lethal violence - and the state's answer to the charge that it detains people who have committed no offence is that this is the entire point of preventive detention: to act on the organiser before the mobilisation, because a criminal prosecution requires a completed offence and the alternative instrument once the mobilisation happens is the bullet. The 2020 appointment reform replaced an unstructured 'consultation' with a written search-and-selection procedure of the kind used across Indian statutory tribunals, making the process repeatable and recorded where it had been neither. And the adaptation was tested in court by a detenu and upheld, on the ground that the parent Act itself conferred the power: once Parliament delegates by section 96, action taken under it cannot be ultra vires.

**Strongest case against.** The same Fifth Schedule that kept this Act repealed the State Human Rights Commission, the Accountability Commission, the Vigilance Commission and the Women's Commission. The bodies that received a citizen's complaint against the state went; the statute by which the state takes a citizen's liberty without charge stayed. What the 2020 order then did to the Advisory Board is the sharper half: a tribunal whose function is to review the executive's own detention orders now has its members selected by a committee chaired by the head of that executive and seating the Home Department, which is the parent department of every detaining authority in the territory. The form of the body is judicial and the production of its membership is executive. The same order omitted the proviso that had confined detenus to prisons inside the territory, and did so by executive notification rather than by any legislature. And the constitutional footing the administration now asserts - that the Act is deemed to have been passed by Parliament under Article 22(7) - claims for a 1978 State enactment a status it did not have before 31 October 2019, on the strength of its inclusion in a schedule.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Filed no-objective because no objective was stated at announcement against which any of these three acts could be scored. Neither the Fifth Schedule's retention of the Act, nor the Order of 22 May 2018, nor entry 104 of S.O. 1229(E) was accompanied by any statement of what it was meant to achieve, and none has a claim at launch anywhere in the retrieved record. The value asserts nothing about the quality of the finding: the mechanism is established from the official consolidated text and from the Gazette.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** Rules or Standard Operating Procedures governing how a detaining authority in J&K satisfies itself under section 8 of the Public Safety Act — The holder has spoken, and says there are none. The J&K Home Department confirmed under the Right to Information Act that the State Government has not made any Rules or Standard Operating Procedures under the 1978 Act during its four decades of existence. So an order that can deprive a person of liberty for up to two years is made under no written criteria at all. This is the same shape as the AFSPA section 7 sanction decision rule, which both the Ministry of Defence and Army Headquarters attested they do not hold: two of the three coercive powers operating in Jammu and Kashmir have, on their own holders' record, no written decision rule.

**Sources** (5, T1×2 T2×1 T4×2):

- `T1` The Jammu and Kashmir Public Safety Act 1978, official consolidated text with amendment footnotes - section 14(3) in its Search-cum-Selection Committee form, section 10 with the proviso omitted by S.O. 1229(E), and the complete list of amending instruments. Retrieved as a PDF and read — https://www.indiacode.nic.in/bitstream/123456789/16496/1/public_safety_act,_1978.pdf
- `T1` Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act 2019, Fifth Schedule Table-4 entry 123 (Public Safety Act retained) against Table-3 entries 1, 23, 117 and 164 (the four commissions repealed), read from the Gazette — https://egazette.gov.in/WriteReadData/2019/210407.pdf
- `T4` The Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation (Adaptation of State Laws) Order 2020, S.O. 1229(E) of 31 March 2020, entry 104 - reproduced on a private legal portal, retrieved and read. This is a reproduction and not the Gazette instrument. The reproduction reads 'Administrative Secretary, Home Department' where the official Act text reads 'Additional Secretary' — https://www.legitquest.com/act/jammu-and-kashmir-reorganisation-adaptation-of-state-laws-order-2020/CC70
- `T2` UN OHCHR, Kashmir update of 8 July 2019, paragraph 89 and footnote 112 - the Jammu and Kashmir (Preventive Detention Laws) Order 2018 of 22 May 2018 removing the requirement to consult the High Court Chief Justice when constituting the Advisory Board. The OHCHR document was retrieved and read; the 2018 Order is relayed through it — https://www.ohchr.org/sites/default/files/Documents/Countries/IN/KashmirUpdateReport_8July2019.pdf
- `T4` Press accounts of the administration's submission to the High Court that the Act 'shall be deemed to have been passed by the Parliament under Article 22(7)', and of Tanveer Ahmad Mir v. Union Territory of J&K, 2026:JKLHC-SR:1587, upholding the adaptation. Three accounts give three different dates in May 2026 for the same judgment and the judgment was not retrieved — https://m.thewire.in/article/government/jk-public-safety-act-shall-be-deemed-to-be-an-act-passed-by-parliament-admin-tells-hc

### B.32 · L-0136 — The August 2019 communications blackout: four defensible durations, and orders the state refused to produce

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — contested on a missing-evidence ground.*

`contested` · T2 · governance/kashmir · episode · 2019-08-04–2021-02-05 · confidence medium · last researched 2026-08-03 · contested ground: evidence-unobservable

**History.** Created 2026-08-03. 1 commit(s) touched it afterwards: 2026-08-03 (sources).

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** Confidence is not uniform across this record's span. For 4 August 2019 to roughly mid-January 2020 there is no measurement of the subject independent of either a government that did not publish its orders or a press that could not file. Everything from January 2020 is far better evidenced.

**What this is.** Mobile networks, internet and landline connectivity in the Kashmir Valley were all discontinued on 4 August 2019, the day before the constitutional orders. Restoration was staged over eighteen months and the duration therefore has at least four defensible values, from roughly 163 days to roughly 551, depending on which restoration is counted as the end. The orders that imposed the restriction were never published, and when the Supreme Court directed their production on 16 October 2019 the Union claimed privilege and then supplied only samples.

**What can be established since.** Every date below is from a retrieved primary document. 4 August 2019: 'mobile phone networks, internet services, landline connectivity were all discontinued in the valley'. 5 August 2019: District Magistrates imposed section 144 CrPC restrictions on movement and public gatherings; the Kashmir Times Srinagar edition could not be distributed that day and could not be published from 6 August. 16 October 2019: the Court records the Solicitor General's submission that mobile and landline had been partly relaxed, without dates. 10 January 2020: judgment. 14 January 2020: fixed line to institutions managing essential services, 2G postpaid in five Jammu districts. 24 January 2020: 2G restored in all districts, whitelisted sites only. 31 January, 7 February and 24 February 2020: the whitelist runs 329, then 481, then 1,674 sites. 4 March 2020: the whitelist is abolished. 15 April 2020: fixed line without speed restriction. 5 February 2021: the speed restriction revoked, on the advice of a Special Committee then in its sixth meeting. The four durations, each with both endpoints retrieved: total blackout to first fixed-line restoration, about 163 days; to any mobile internet in the Valley, about 173; to an open non-whitelisted internet, about 213; to unrestricted-speed mobile internet, about 551. The 551-day figure is the one in general circulation and requires the most care, because from March 2020 there was an open 2G internet on postpaid and verified prepaid connections and an unthrottled fixed line from April. The two counters that publish the figure differ by a day - 551 against 552 - on whether the start is 4 or 5 August and whether endpoints are inclusive. On the orders themselves: not one document in the J&K Home Department's numbered suspension-order series is dated 2019, the series begins in January 2020 after the judgment, and the Department was publishing other orders on the same website throughout 2019, so the mechanism existed and the communications orders were kept off it.

**Strongest case for.** A shutdown is a preventive instrument against rumour-driven mass mobilisation, and the theatre has a documented history of exactly that in 2008, 2010 and 2016. The Supreme Court, in the judgment most often cited against the state, accepted the premise in terms: modern terrorism relies heavily on the internet, which is used to raise money, recruit and spread propaganda, and which 'provides an easy inroad to young impressionable minds'. The period following August 2019 did not produce the mass-casualty street unrest of those earlier years. Restoration was not indefinite and was not a single switch: it was staged, documented, and each stage was tied to an assessment, and every one of the dates above sits on a published order that was retrieved for this record. Asked in May 2020 to lift the restriction outright, the Court declined and constituted a review body instead - which is not what a court that thought the restriction indefensible would have done. The state is under no obligation to publish operational security orders in real time while an operation is running, and from January 2020 the J&K Home Department has published its numbered suspension orders continuously, with publication written into the order's own distribution list alongside the press and the Gazette.

**Strongest case against.** The longest shutdown recorded anywhere, imposed on a population of more than twelve million, and the orders that imposed it were never published - so proportionality review was impossible by design rather than by oversight. That is not a characterisation but the record: when the Court directed production on 16 October 2019 the Union first claimed privilege, then dropped it and produced samples, and the Court held that difficulty 'is not a valid ground to refuse production of orders'. The published series begins after the judgment, and the Department's website was carrying its other orders throughout 2019, so the absence is not the absence of a mechanism. The deepest point is about knowledge rather than about rights: the blackout disabled the instruments that would have measured its own effects. Every tracker of Indian shutdowns is built, by its own written admission, from newspaper reports, and the newspapers could not publish - so the single most consequential shutdown in Indian history is recorded in the most-cited tracker as one row citing a news website, with no end date and a status of 'Active' seven years later. On the official side, the Ministry told the Lok Sabha in September 2020 that 2G was 'not an impediment' to health, education and justice in Jammu and Kashmir, and fourteen months later told a parliamentary committee that no impact assessment is available with it at all, and that neither it nor the telecommunications department has ever conducted any study linking shutdowns to the disorder they are imposed to prevent.

**Different facts — flag is TRUE.** The security case rests on incident counts the UT administration produces and the Union relays, on the absence of the mass-casualty unrest of 2008, 2010 and 2016, and on the stagedness of restoration. The rights case rests on a duration, a population, and a stated absence - unpublished orders, and therefore no possible proportionality review. Both are arithmetically correct and neither contradicts the other: grant every incident figure the State placed before the Court and the rights case is untouched, because it is not a claim that violence rose; grant the 551 days and the unpublished orders and the security case is untouched, because it is not a claim that the orders were published. They cannot be netted, and not because the netting is merely unbuilt. It would require two terms that do not exist: the violence that would have occurred without the shutdown, which has no control territory and no counterfactual and which the Government has told a parliamentary committee it has never assessed; and the proportionality record, which is precisely what was not published and was refused when the Supreme Court asked for it. Building the common ledger would require the very thing whose absence is the rights case's central claim. That is the schema's own admitted case - a quantity against a stated absence, with the counterpart declared unmeasurable - and not the unbuilt-comparison case it excludes.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Scored contested rather than partly or failed because the two sides rest on different facts, neither of which is in doubt, and no measure exists on which the record could choose between them. The schema's contested definition fits on its own text: the evidence supports more than one defensible reading and this record does not choose. The scoring is not against a launch claim - no objective was announced for the suspension - but against the Supreme Court's directions of 10 January 2020, which is the same construction L-0081 uses and declares.

**Not measured — stated reason `withheld` (stated reason DISPUTED, normative).** The orders that imposed the communications restriction from 4 August 2019 — Specifically requested, and refused, on the record of the Supreme Court. Named requester: the petitioners in Writ Petitions (Civil) 1031 and 1164 of 2019, and the Supreme Court of India itself. Specific request: the prayer for a mandamus directing production of 'all orders by way of which communication has been blocked in State of Jammu and Kashmir', and the Court's own order directing production. Date: 16 October 2019. Refusal: the Solicitor General claimed privilege over the orders at that hearing; the claim was subsequently dropped and only sample orders were produced, on a plea of difficulty, which the Court held 'is not a valid ground to refuse production of orders before the Court'. The stated reason moved from privilege to administrative difficulty, and those are different claims about different facts; what is contested is not whether the orders existed - samples were produced - but whether difficulty justifies non-production, which the Court answered. **What would close it:** Direction (a) of the judgment of 10 January 2020 itself, which requires publication of all orders in force and any future orders for suspension of telecom services. This is one of the few routes in the corpus that is enforceable rather than requestable, and unlike the all-India case it has a specific refusal behind it rather than a general non-practice.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** A count of orders under section 144 CrPC, now section 163 of the Bharatiya Nagarik Suraksha Sanhita, restricting movement and assembly — No count exists at any level, official or unofficial. The trackers count internet shutdowns; nobody counts movement orders. The orders are issued by individual magistrates and there is no central register, so if a holder were compelled tomorrow no single body could produce the series. The August 2019 restriction had two legs - the communications cut and the movement restrictions imposed by District Magistrates on 5 August - and the corpus records only one of them. The judgment's direction to publish covers both.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** A count of suspensions of voice calling and SMS, as distinct from internet — The Suspension Rules cover telecom services, which include telephony, and a Valley-wide suspension of voice calling and SMS was ordered for Republic Day 2020 - sixteen days after the judgment - and confirmed by a published Home Department order that was retrieved and read. Every tracker of Indian shutdowns counts internet only. So a suspension of the oldest and most basic telecom service is counted nowhere, by anyone, official or unofficial, and the instruments that exist are defined so as to exclude it. This is not that nobody has published the count but that the quantity has never been gathered.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** Any assessment of the effect of the suspension, or of whether suspension achieves what it is imposed for — Both responsible Union bodies told a parliamentary committee they hold none, and each named the other. The telecommunications department said it is not assessing whether the objectives have been achieved and that responsibility lies completely with the Ministry of Home Affairs or the State Government; the Committee recorded that no assessment is available with the Ministry of Home Affairs; and both told the Committee they have not conducted any study to establish the link between internet shutdown and communal riots. Neither has studied the rules adopted in other democracies. A measure defended by its effects has never had its effects measured, and the Government said so to Parliament.

**Series this rests on.** jk-published-suspension-orders

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-95, P-96, P-95, P-54

**Sources** (5, T1×5):

- `T1` Anuradha Bhasin v. Union of India, Supreme Court of India, 10 January 2020 - paragraphs 3, 4, 8, 11, 12, 18, 39 and the directions at paragraph 152, including the direction to publish all orders in force and any future orders under section 144 CrPC and for suspension of telecom services. Retrieved as a PDF from the Court's own document host and read in full — https://api.sci.gov.in/supremecourt/2019/29796/29796_2019_1_1501_49019_Judgement_11-Dec-2023.pdf
- `T1` Foundation for Media Professionals v. Union Territory of Jammu and Kashmir, Supreme Court of India, 11 May 2020 - the table of thirteen orders passed since 10 January 2020 giving the staged restoration dates and whitelist sizes, and paragraph 23 constituting the Special Committee headed by the Union Home Secretary. Retrieved and read — https://api.sci.gov.in/
- `T1` Government of Jammu and Kashmir, Home Department, Government Order No. Home-07 (TSTS) of 2021 dated 5 February 2021, revoking the restrictions on mobile data and fixed line placed by Home-02 (TSTS) of 2021, and recording the Special Committee's sixth meeting of 4 February 2021. Retrieved from an Internet Archive capture of the Department's own file and read. Cited as the reproducible archive INVENTORY from which these orders were enumerated, not as a wildcard listing: a /web/*/ URL is the archive's search interface and retrieves no document. Each order named here is recoverable from this inventory as a /web/<timestamp>id_/ snapshot — https://web.archive.org/cdx/search/cdx?url=jkhome.nic.in&matchType=domain&output=text&fl=original,timestamp&collapse=urlkey&limit=20000
- `T1` Standing Committee on Communications and Information Technology (2021-22), Seventeenth Lok Sabha, 'Suspension of Telecom Services/Internet and its Impact', Twenty-Sixth Report, December 2021 - the Committee's record that no impact assessment study has been done by the Department, that no assessment is available with MHA, and that neither has conducted any study to establish a link between internet shutdown and communal riots. Retrieved in full as the Lok Sabha Secretariat's own PDF from an Internet Archive capture of the official host, contemporaneous with publication, and read — http://web.archive.org/web/20211202141356if_/http://164.100.47.193/lsscommittee/Communications%20and%20Information%20Technology/17_Communications_and_Information_Technology_26.pdf
- `T1` Ministry of Home Affairs, Lok Sabha Unstarred Question No. 1440, answered 20 September 2020, 'INTERNET BLOCKADE IN KASHMIR', in which the Ministry states that 2G speed is not an impediment to health, education and justice in Jammu and Kashmir. Retrieved as a PDF from MHA's own Q&A archive — https://www.mha.gov.in/MHA1/Par2017/PArQueAnsPage-new.html

### B.33 · L-0137 — The Review Committee for J&K: findings refused as exempt, ordered released, then said not to exist

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — contested on a missing-evidence ground.*

`contested` · T3 · governance/kashmir · institutional · 2023-06-22–2025 · confidence medium · last researched 2026-08-03 · contested ground: evidence-withheld

**History.** Created 2026-08-03. 1 commit(s) touched it afterwards: 2026-08-03 (sources).

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** The RTI chain is known only through the applicant organisation's own published posts. The applicant is a party and not an observer, no appellate order was retrieved, and no J&K host was reachable to check any of it against the department's own records.

**What this is.** The Review Committee is the only safeguard the Suspension Rules provide against misuse of the power, and the Rules require it to record findings. Asked twice under the Right to Information Act for the findings on J&K suspension orders, the Home Department refused them as exempt without naming a clause, was twice directed to release them by its own First Appellate Authority, did not comply, and then said the information was not available in its records. The Review Committee orders that have since surfaced are bare confirmations containing no findings at all, and one of them confirms orders passed 178 days earlier.

**What can be established since.** The chain, from the applicant's own published account. June 2023: an RTI to the J&K Home Department for the number of suspension orders issued since 10 January 2020, whether a Review Committee had been constituted, and its findings; everything disclosed except the findings, refused as exempt under section 8 without specifying which clause; first appeal 29 July 2023; the First Appellate Authority ordered release on 17 August 2023, recording that the Supreme Court had made no direction permitting a Public Information Officer to withhold Review Committee orders. May 2024: application IFF/RTI/2024/112 of 15 May 2024, same subject; refused 6 June 2024 on the same unparticularised ground; first appeal 6 July 2024; the Appellate Authority again ordered release on 5 August 2024; the officer did not comply; a compliance letter of 15 October 2024 produced the reply of 29 October 2024 that the information is 'not available in the records' of the department. A further appeal on 30 November 2024 produced a direction limited to orders issued after 23 February 2024, and a second appeal was filed. Separately, two Review Committee orders were retrieved from archive captures of the department's own files and read: Government Order No. 2128-JK(GAD) of 23 December 2024 and No. 249-JK(GAD) of 18 February 2025. Three things follow from them. They are published by the General Administration Department and not the Home Department, and sit in a GAD file, which supplies a candidate innocent explanation for the Home officer's answer - though under section 6(3) of the Right to Information Act a public authority that does not hold information must transfer the application rather than reject it. Each states only that the action 'has been confirmed by the Review Committee', with no finding of any kind, where both the 2017 and 2024 Rules require the Committee to record its findings as to whether the order accords with the statute. And the timing does not meet the five-day rule on the face of the published record: the December 2024 order confirms orders of 27 and 28 June 2024, 178 and 179 days earlier, while the February 2025 order confirms an order twelve days earlier; neither gives a meeting date, so whether the Committee met in time is not determinable from the document that exists to record its work.

**Strongest case for.** A Review Committee for Jammu and Kashmir demonstrably exists, meets, and has its confirmations published - established here from primary documents rather than from any applicant's account. Its composition matches what the Rules prescribe and the distribution of its orders is consistent with that composition. The department's two answers are not necessarily inconsistent: the review record sits with the General Administration Department and the applications were addressed to the Home Department, so an officer answering that the material is not available in his department's records may be departmentally accurate. The improvement across the statutory changeover is real and visible - a 178-day lag on the June 2024 orders against a twelve-day lag on the February 2025 one. And the Supreme Court's own position, as reported, is that the Committee's deliberations need not be published; what must be published is the order passed in the review, and those orders are published.

**Strongest case against.** What is published is a bare confirmation with no findings and no reasons, when the rule that constitutes the Committee requires it to record its findings as to whether the direction accords with the statute. If the order passed in review must be published so that it can be reviewed, an order containing nothing but the word 'confirmed' defeats the purpose exactly. On the two documents that exist, either the review was months late or the record of a timely review was published months late, and the published document cannot distinguish them - which is itself the finding, since a document whose function is to evidence timeliness omits the only date that would. And the department's own answers cannot both be true: in June it withheld the findings as exempt under section 8, which asserts they exist and are held, and in October it said they were not available in the records, which asserts they are not. If the second answer is the true one, then the Review Committee findings for January 2020 to February 2024 do not exist, and the only statutory oversight mechanism was not producing the record the rule requires - which is a materially worse finding than non-disclosure. Neither reading can be eliminated on the evidence held.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Filed contested on the written definition, and the two defensible readings are named rather than chosen: either the findings exist and were withheld, or they do not exist and the safeguard was not operating. The distinction matters and cannot be resolved from the documents retrieved, and this record does not choose between them.

**Not measured — stated reason `withheld` (stated reason DISPUTED, evidentiary).** The Review Committee's findings on J&K suspension orders between January 2020 and February 2024 — Specifically requested and refused, twice, in defiance of two appellate directions. Named requester: the Internet Freedom Foundation. Specific request: RTI application IFF/RTI/2024/112 to the Home Department, Government of Jammu and Kashmir, for the findings of the Review Committee on internet suspension orders in the UT since 10 January 2020. Date: 15 May 2024; refused 6 June 2024 as exempt under section 8 without naming a clause; ordered released by the First Appellate Authority on 5 August 2024; refused again on 29 October 2024 as 'not available in the records'. The stated reason is contradicted by the same holder's own earlier reason in the same file five months apart: exempt under section 8 asserts the material exists and is held, not available in the records asserts it does not. Only one can be true. **What would close it:** The second appeal already filed before the Jammu and Kashmir State Information Commission. Failing that, a request addressed to the General Administration Department, which is where the review record demonstrably sits - the published confirmations of December 2024 and February 2025 are GAD orders and carry a GAD file number. A route naming the Home Department's website is not a route: that domain no longer resolves.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** Whether Review Committees have been constituted in every State and Union Territory, and how often any of them has met — The Union department that made the Rules told a parliamentary committee in December 2021 that the status of formation of Review Committees is not monitored by it and that this is the State Governments' responsibility, and the Ministry of Home Affairs replied that the question has to be answered by that department. Each Union body named the other and neither held it. In at least one jurisdiction the Committee had not been constituted at all. The Committee found it strange that the Department did not know, and recorded that there is no mechanism to ascertain it. Nothing in the report states how many times any Review Committee has met, or that any finding has ever been published. **What would close it:** The Action Taken Report on the Twenty-Sixth Report, which would record whether the Committee's recommendation that the data be obtained and maintained with periodic monitoring was accepted and acted on. It was not retrieved in this phase.

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-95

**Sources** (3, T1×2 T4×1):

- `T4` Internet Freedom Foundation, published accounts of RTI applications of 22 June 2023 and IFF/RTI/2024/112 of 15 May 2024 to the Home Department, Government of Jammu and Kashmir, and of the First Appellate Authority orders of 17 August 2023 and 5 August 2024 and the subsequent refusals. The applicant's own posts; no appellate order retrieved — https://internetfreedom.in/
- `T1` Government of Jammu and Kashmir, General Administration Department, Government Order No. 2128-JK(GAD) of 2024 dated 23 December 2024 and No. 249-JK(GAD) of 2025 dated 18 February 2025 - published Review Committee confirmations of Home Department suspension orders, signed by the Commissioner/Secretary GAD and copied to the Principal Secretary Home, the Principal Secretary Finance, the Secretary Law and Justice and the Chief Secretary. Retrieved from Internet Archive captures of the department's own files, rendered and read. Cited as the specific timestamped snapshot rather than a wildcard listing: a /web/*/ URL is the archive's SEARCH interface and retrieves no document — https://web.archive.org/web/20250212111631id_/https://jkhome.nic.in/pdf/TSTS2128GADof2024.pdf
- `T1` Standing Committee on Communications and Information Technology, Twenty-Sixth Report, December 2021, paragraphs 50 and 51 and the Committee's finding - that constitution of Review Committees is not monitored by the Department of Telecommunications, that the Ministry of Home Affairs replied that this has to be replied by DoT, that no Review Committee had been constituted in Delhi, and that there is no mechanism to ascertain whether Review Committees have been constituted in all States and UTs. Retrieved in full and read — http://web.archive.org/web/20211202141356if_/http://164.100.47.193/lsscommittee/Communications%20and%20Information%20Technology/17_Communications_and_Information_Technology_26.pdf

### B.34 · L-0144 — Permanent resident replaced by domicile: a definitional change, and the sub-count the Union did not publish for five years

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — contested on a missing-evidence ground.*

`contested` · T2 · governance/kashmir · reform · 2020-04-03–2026 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-03 · contested ground: evidence-withheld

**History.** Created 2026-08-03. Never edited since.

**What this is.** The category of permanent resident, which had defined who could hold immovable property, take State employment and claim State scholarships in Jammu and Kashmir, was replaced by a domicile category defined by executive order in 2020. The Union published a total of certificates issued and, once, the number issued to people who already held or depended on a permanent-resident certificate. The complement - certificates to people who did not - was never published by the Union, and appeared only when a restored J&K Assembly asked for it in April 2025.

**What can be established since.** The Union's published figures, every one prefaced 'As per the information provided by the Government of Jammu & Kashmir'. Around September 2020, with no as-on date stated: 2,113,879 applications received, 1,679,520 certificates issued, 121,630 rejected. As on 31 December 2020, given twice in identical terms in March 2021: 3,544,938 applications, 3,231,353 issued, 215,438 rejected, and 3,108,682 issued to dependents or holders of erstwhile Permanent Resident Certificates. The residual, 3,231,353 minus 3,108,682, is 122,671 - and it is not a count of newcomers. It necessarily mixes persons qualifying on the migrant limb, children of central-government officials, children of parents who qualify, and persons who were entitled to a permanent-resident certificate but never held one in their own name, because the published denominator is certificate-holding rather than entitlement. Treating it as a count of outsiders granted domicile would be exactly the laundering this instrument exists to prevent. A further 98,147 applications on the same date were neither issued nor rejected; that subtraction is derived here and is stated in no reply. Then the Union series stops. A sweep of every parliamentary answer published on the Ministry's own day-pages for 2019 to 2026 - 3,674 files - finds the word domicile in no answer after 24 March 2021. Five and a half years of sessions have produced no update. The quantity the Union did not disaggregate was produced within months of the J&K Assembly resuming: in April 2025 the Revenue Minister told a member that 83,742 domicile certificates had been granted to persons described as non-state subjects in the preceding two years, out of roughly 3.512 million issued in that period. That figure is relayed and carries three cautions: the assembly reply itself could not be reached; 'non-state subject' is the reply's own pre-2019 term and is not obviously the complement of the Union's 'dependents or holders of erstwhile Permanent Resident Certificates'; and its period is roughly 2023 to 2025, so it must not be added to or subtracted from the 2020 residual, and the gap between them remains unpublished.

**Strongest case for.** The permanent-resident category was itself an exclusion, and the people it excluded are identifiable and were excluded for generations: West Pakistan refugees, Valmikis and Gorkhas resident in the territory for decades could not hold immovable property, could not take State employment, and could not claim State scholarships, and a woman who married a person from outside lost her status. Replacing a hereditary status with a residence-and-study test extends to those populations rights every other Indian citizen already held, and it does so on conditions - long residence, or schooling in the territory - that are ordinary in Indian domicile law. The scale of the exercise is also evidence against the fear it was designed to serve: 3.1 million of the 3.2 million certificates issued to the end of 2020 went to people who already held or depended on a permanent-resident certificate, which is a conversion of existing status rather than an influx.

**Strongest case against.** The definition of who belongs to a territory was replaced by executive order, under a power that expired within a year of the appointed day, at a time when the legislature of that territory did not exist and could not be consulted. And the one quantity that would let anyone test what the change did - certificates issued to persons who were not previously permanent residents - was not published by the Union in five and a half years, while the Union's own answers show the underlying split was separable from the start, since it published the complement in 2020. That the figure existed all along is now established rather than inferred: a restored Assembly asked and got 83,742 within months. The Union's own series then simply stops in March 2021, so there is no published Union figure for anything after 31 December 2020, and a reader looking at the published record cannot tell whether that is because nothing happened or because nobody asked.

**Different facts — flag is TRUE.** The two sides do not share a measure, and the failure is structural rather than clerical. The case for rests on legal-status facts - that named populations resident for generations could not hold property, take State employment or claim scholarships, and that a woman marrying a non-resident lost status. Those are facts about who was excluded from a category, and the excluded population was never enumerated by any instrument: there is no stock count of permanent residents anywhere, and the object 'person who held permanent-resident status and did not convert' has no agreed definition at all, because the two statuses have different qualifying conditions and neither population is enumerated as a stock. The case against rests on process facts - that the definition was replaced by executive order under a power that expired within a year, when the affected legislature did not exist. Granting the government's 3,108,682 in full leaves the process objection untouched; granting the process objection in full leaves the rights-extension case untouched. The one quantity that would join them, certificates by year and by qualifying limb, is not published, and the only figure that exists is on a different period under a different category name. That is criterion (c) in its stated form: a constitutional process against a stated absence, crossing a category boundary.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Filed contested on the written definition: the evidence supports more than one defensible reading and this record does not choose between them. It is not partly or failed, because no objective was stated at announcement for the domicile order that could be scored, and it is not no-objective, because both sides are making claims about effects that the record has to weigh rather than merely reporting an unclaimed finding.

**Re-test when.** Publication of a domicile-certificate series disaggregated by year and by qualifying limb of the domicile rule. If that appears, a common denominator exists, criterion (c) fails, and the differentFacts flag on this record should flip to false.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Domicile certificates issued to persons who were not previously permanent residents, as a Union-published series — Not not-collected: the holder demonstrably has it. The J&K Revenue Department produced 83,742 to the J&K Assembly in April 2025, and the Union's own answer of March 2021 shows the permanent-resident-certificate sub-total was already separable in 2020. Not withheld: no named requester made a specific refused request to the Union for the disaggregation - members asked for the permanent-resident-holder total and were given it, and nobody asked for the complement and was refused. The Union simply stopped publishing anything on the subject after March 2021. **What would close it:** A parliamentary question to the Ministry of Home Affairs for domicile certificates issued, by year and by qualifying limb of the domicile provision - fifteen years' residence, seven years' study, the migrant limb, children of central officials, children of qualifying parents, spouse. The limbs exist in the rule, so the split is constructible from the application forms themselves.

**Not measured — stated reason `never-defined`.** The number of persons who held permanent-resident status and did not obtain, or were refused, a domicile certificate — The one item in this subject that meets the strict test. The two statuses have different qualifying conditions and no instrument enumerates the permanent-resident population as a stock, so the intersection is not definable, let alone countable. The published rejection figures do not answer it: they count applications rejected for missing documents, not persons whose prior status failed to convert. This is not that nobody has studied it - there is no agreed object to study. No route exists and none is invented, because closing it would require first defining and enumerating a stock that no instrument has ever defined.

**Series this rests on.** jk-domicile-certificates-issued

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-89

**Sources** (4, T1×3 T4×1):
