### B.35 · L-0148 — The J&K human rights commission was abolished, and the body said to replace it cannot investigate the respondents its caseload named

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — contested on a missing-evidence ground.*

`contested` · T2 · governance/kashmir · institutional · 2019-10-31–2026 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-03 · contested ground: evidence-withheld

**History.** Created 2026-08-03. Never edited since.

**What this is.** The Jammu and Kashmir State Human Rights Commission was extinguished on the appointed day with more than 630 complaints pending, and the National Human Rights Commission acquired ordinary jurisdiction over the territory for the first time. Complaints registered with the national body from J&K have risen from 210 in the last full pre-break year to 982 in 2023-24. Referrals from the national body to a state commission, which ran at 38 and 51 in the two preceding years, have been zero in every year since. And section 19 of the national Act, untouched by the reorganisation, bars the national Commission from investigating the armed forces - the class of respondent the abolished Commission's caseload most often named.

**What can be established since.** The Fifth Schedule did two things to the human rights architecture on one day. Table-1 entry 86 omitted the proviso that had kept the central Protection of Human Rights Act out of the erstwhile State for matters on the State List, so the national Commission gained ordinary jurisdiction. Table-3 entry 117 repealed the J&K Protection of Human Rights Act 1997, extinguishing the State Commission. The measured record is in the national Commission's own annual reports, retrieved from its own server. J&K cases registered ran 194 in 2017-18, 218 in 2018-19, 160 in 2019-20, 282 in 2020-21, 465 in 2021-22, 379 in 2022-23 and 995 in 2023-24, with a Ladakh row appearing from 2019-20. The abolition is visible inside the same reports' arithmetic: the column recording J&K cases transferred to State Human Rights Commissions ran 38 in 2017-18 and 51 in 2018-19, fell to 10 in 2019-20 and has been zero in every year since - so complainants in every other State have two bodies and complainants in J&K have one. One column decides the substitution question and it is empty for a different reason: the annual reports carry a 'Defence/Para-Military Custodial Deaths and Rapes' intimation column, and the J&K row reads zero in every year from 2017-18 to 2022-23, against all-India values of 1, 2, 0, 1, 1 and 6 - a column that records intimations received rather than complaints made. What the Commission does not publish is the fraction of J&K complaints whose named respondent is the Army, the Rashtriya Rifles or a central armed police force, and section 19 of its own Act means that for that class it may only seek a report from the Central Government and then drop the matter or recommend: it cannot summon, cannot compel and cannot establish a fact of its own. Section 2(1)(a) defines armed forces to include any other armed forces of the Union, which brings in the central armed police forces.

**Strongest case for.** The pre-2019 architecture was a second state-level commission sitting under a national one, and on the hardest cases in the territory it could do nothing at all: the United Nations human rights office recorded in June 2018 that the State Commission did not send a notice to the Indian Army because it had no jurisdiction over forces controlled by the central government operating in Kashmir. A body that cannot notice the principal respondent in the theatre is not an accountability mechanism for that theatre, and its recommendations were non-binding in any event - a limitation the national Commission itself has asked the Central Government to remove for all state commissions. Against that, the reorganisation deleted the proviso that had kept the national Act out of the territory, so for the first time a better-resourced body with a national investigation division and statutory reporting to Parliament has ordinary jurisdiction there; the same schedule extended the central Right to Information Act with its penalty provisions, the National Commission for Women Act, the child rights commissions Act and the Prevention of Corruption Act. And the record shows the national body working the territory: registrations rose from 210 in the last full pre-break year to 982 in 2023-24, monetary relief has been recommended in J&K cases in several years, and the Supreme Court has pressed for a mechanism letting people file from the territory itself. A duplicate and partly ornamental layer was removed and a stronger one extended, and the rise in registrations is what substitution looks like when it works.

**Strongest case against.** A commission actively examining more than 630 complaints alleging murder, enforced disappearance and rape by security forces was extinguished by a line in a schedule, with no successor named and no arrangement for its files, and its records have sat in a locked room at its former premises ever since. No effort was made to transfer them to the national Commission. The body said to have replaced it cannot investigate the respondents those complaints name: section 19 was not touched by the reorganisation, and for any complaint against the armed forces - a class the Act defines to include any other armed forces of the Union, and so the central armed police forces - the Commission may only seek a report and then drop the matter or recommend. In the one territory where central forces do most of the holding, the substitute is statutorily blind to the principal respondent, and the twenty-four-hour custodial-death intimation duty does not run to those forces either. Nor is the substitution visible in the instruments that would show it. Referrals of J&K cases to a state commission ran at 38 and 51 in the two full years before the abolition and have been zero in every year since. No camp sitting or open hearing was held in the territory in any year for which a report has been published. The one dimension that ever identified armed-forces and paramilitary complaints as such was all-India only, was never crossed with the state dimension, and was discontinued after the 2021-22 report. And the arithmetic that would settle whether any of this amounts to substitution has never been published, because nobody publishes what fraction of J&K complaints concern forces the national Commission may not investigate.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** Flagged false and noted, because the two cases look as though they cross a category boundary - a rising complaint count against a statutory bar - and they do not. Precondition (b) holds: granting the case for its strongest fact in full, that the State Commission had no jurisdiction over centrally controlled forces, leaves the case against untouched, because 630 complaints still abated and section 19 still bars the national Commission; and granting the case against in full leaves the extension of the national Act intact. But criterion (c) fails, and it fails for the ordinary reason the criterion names. The common measure exists in principle: it is the fraction of J&K complaints registered with the national Commission whose named respondent is the Army, the Rashtriya Rifles or a central armed police force. The Commission assigns an incident code to every case and records the state of origin of every case, and it prints both facts separately in the same document - a body that can print two columns can produce their intersection. The comparison is unbuilt, not unbuildable, and an unbuilt comparison fails (c) expressly. That the one number which would decide the argument is the one nobody has printed is the finding, and it is a gap in the instrument rather than a property of the argument.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The fraction of J&K complaints to the National Human Rights Commission whose named respondent is the Army, the Rashtriya Rifles or a central armed police force — This is the number that decides the substitution question and it is the one nobody prints. The Commission registers every complaint against a named respondent and publishes the two dimensions separately - state-wise in one annexure, and all-India by incident code including dedicated code ranges for Defence Forces and Para-Military Forces in another - so the cross-tabulation exists in its case-management system and has never been printed. The classification is the one most at risk of being got wrong here: it would be easy to call it not-collected on the ground that the cross-tabulation has never been compiled, and that is the wrong test. The schema's test is whether the data exists, not whether the table has been built. **What would close it:** A request under the Right to Information Act to the National Human Rights Commission, which is a public authority under that Act, for cases registered from Jammu and Kashmir since 1 April 2017 broken down by incident code; or a parliamentary question to the Ministry of Home Affairs for the same breakdown.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** All-India complaints to the National Human Rights Commission by respondent category, after 2021-22 — The series was published for years and then dropped. The Commission plainly still holds the underlying detail, because it codes every case by incident, and its own annual report structure shows the aggregation being performed. The absence is of publication, not of the record. **What would close it:** The same request as above, extended to the all-India series and framed on the discontinuation after the 2021-22 report.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** Whether any J&K complaint to the National Human Rights Commission has been investigated by the Commission's own investigation division rather than referred for a report — The published disposal categories are dismissal in limine, disposal with directions, transfer to State Commissions, and conclusion after receipt of reports. There is no published category for investigation by the Commission's own division broken down by state, so the quantity has never been gathered in a form anyone could produce. For the armed-forces class the answer is knowable a priori without any data at all, because section 19 forecloses investigation as a matter of law.

**Series this rests on.** jk-nhrc-complaints, jk-nhrc-cases-transferred-to-shrc

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-97

**Sources** (4, T1×3 T2×1):

- `T1` National Human Rights Commission Annual Reports 2016-17 through 2023-24, Annexure-1 (state-wise cases registered, with the Defence and Para-Military custodial intimation column) and Annexure-2 (disposal, including the Transferred to SHRCs column). Retrieved from the Commission's own server, with the 2016-17 volume additionally retrieved from the Ministry of Home Affairs' server — https://nhrc.nic.in/publications/annual-reports
- `T1` Jammu and Kashmir Reorganisation Act 2019, Fifth Schedule Table-1 entry 86 omitting the proviso to section 1(2) of the central Protection of Human Rights Act, and Table-3 entry 117 repealing the J&K Protection of Human Rights Act 1997. Read from the Gazette — https://egazette.gov.in/WriteReadData/2019/210407.pdf
- `T1` Protection of Human Rights Act 1993, sections 2(1)(a) and 19 - the definition of armed forces as including any other armed forces of the Union, and the provision confining the Commission to seeking a report from the Central Government in respect of them. From the copy hosted by the Commission itself — https://nhrc.nic.in/
- `T2` UN OHCHR, Report on the Situation of Human Rights in Kashmir, June 2018, paragraph 95, recording that the State Commission did not send a notice to the Indian Army as it does not have jurisdiction over forces controlled by the central government operating in Kashmir — https://www.ohchr.org/

### B.36 · L-0151 — The Fourteenth Finance Commission raises the states' share of the divisible pool from 32 to 42 per cent

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — worked on one or two government citations.*

`worked` · T1 · federalism/macro · reform · 2014-12-15–2015-04-01 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-04

**History.** Created 2026-08-04. Never edited since.

**What this is.** The Fourteenth Finance Commission, chaired by Y.V. Reddy, reported on 15 December 2014 and recommended that the states' share of the divisible pool rise from 32 to 42 per cent with effect from FY2015-16 — the largest single-step increase in the history of the institution and the first departure from the incremental 1.5-point moves of the Eleventh, Twelfth and Thirteenth Commissions. The Union accepted it in full.

**What was claimed at announcement.** FC-XIV para 8.13, verbatim: "increasing the share of tax devolution to 42 per cent of the divisible pool", on four stated considerations — "(i) States not being entitled to the growing share of cess and surcharges in the revenues of the Union Government; (ii) the importance of increasing the share of tax devolution in total transfers; (iii) an aggregate view of the revenue expenditure needs of States without Plan and non-Plan distinction; and (iv) the space available with the Union Government." The same paragraph states the intended character of the move: "a compositional shift in transfers from grants to tax devolution... it does not impose an additional fiscal burden on the Union Government."

**What can be established since.** The share moved to 42 per cent and stayed there for the whole award. Devolution rose from 27.1 per cent of the Union's gross tax revenue in FY2014-15 to an FC-XIV award average of 34.0 per cent, and in rupees from ₹3,37,835 crore to ₹5,06,191 crore in the first year alone. Finance Commission grants as a share of gross tax revenue moved 5.1 to 5.8 in the same year and averaged 5.4 across the award against 4.6 under FC-XIII, so the promised compositional shift toward devolution happened without the grant channel collapsing. What also happened over the same five years is that the pool the 42 per cent applied to fell from 89.1 to 80.9 per cent of gross tax revenue.

**Strongest case for.** Neither the number nor the acceptance is in dispute. A Commission constituted under Article 280 recommended the largest increase ever made, the Union accepted it without modification and paid it for five years, and the states' share of gross tax revenue rose by about seven percentage points. The Commission expressly rejected a minimum guaranteed devolution at para 8.14, so what the states received was a share of whatever the pool turned out to be, and they received it untied — money a state legislature appropriates rather than money a Union ministry conditions. On the Union's own framing the shift was fiscally neutral to it, achieved by converting grants into devolution, which is a transfer of discretion to the states at no cost to them.

**Strongest case against.** The Commission's own first reason for the increase was that the states were not entitled to the growing share of cesses and surcharges — so the headline gain was, on the umpire's own account, partly compensation for a loss the states were already sustaining, and the loss continued afterwards. Against a rise of ten points in the share, the pool fell about eight points over the same award and a further seven by FY2020-21. A share of a shrinking base is not the same instrument as a share of a stable one, and the Commission did not fix the base. The compositional shift also carried a cost the paragraph does not mention: converting grants to devolution moved money out of formulae that could be targeted at need and into a formula fixed for five years, which is why the Commission's successors first rebuilt the grant channel and then dismantled it again.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** Both sides accept the recommendation, the acceptance, the rupee figures and the pool arithmetic. The disagreement is about which denominator the 42 per cent should be read against, and that is a weighting of one agreed ledger, not two ledgers.

**Note on the verdict itself.** The value scores the recommendation against the objective stated at announcement, which was to raise the states' share of the divisible pool to 42 per cent. That was recommended, accepted and paid. It does NOT score the net position of the states, which depends on the size of the pool and belongs to L-0150. Reading this record alone as "devolution rose sharply" is the error the pairing of the two records exists to prevent.

**Series this rests on.** fc-vertical-devolution-share, fc-devolution-share-of-gtr, fc-grants-share-of-gtr, fc-devolution-rupees, divisible-pool-share-gtr

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-103, P-104

**Sources** (4, T1×4):

- `T1` Fourteenth Finance Commission, Report (2015-20), Chapter 8 paras 8.10-8.14 and Tables 8.1 and 8.2 — https://fincomindia.nic.in/asset/doc/commission-reports/14th-FC/14thFCReport.pdf
- `T1` Thirteenth Finance Commission, Report 2010-2015, Volume I, para 8.17 and Table 8.1 — https://fincomindia.nic.in/asset/doc/commission-reports/13th-FC/english/13fcreng.pdf
- `T1` Sixteenth Finance Commission, Volume I — Main Report, Table 7.5 and paras 7.8-7.16 — https://fincomindia.nic.in/asset/doc/commission-reports/16th-FC/reports/Vol1-Main-Report.pdf
- `T1` Sixteenth Finance Commission, Volume II — Annexures, Annexure 7.1 — https://fincomindia.nic.in/asset/doc/commission-reports/16th-FC/reports/Vol2-Annexures.pdf

### B.37 · L-0154 — The Union's five action verbs under Article 281 have no definitions, and "due consideration" produced nil of ₹49,599 crore

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — statutory duty scored no-objective — the comparison set.*

`no-objective` · T2 · federalism/governance · institutional · 2021-02-01–2025-08-18 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-04

**History.** Created 2026-08-04. Never edited since.

**What this is.** Article 281 requires the President to lay every Finance Commission recommendation before Parliament with an explanatory memorandum on the action taken. The Union's memoranda use a graded scale of verbs — accepted, accepted in-principle, due consideration will be given, takes note, will examine — and nothing anywhere defines any of them. On the Fifteenth Finance Commission's final report the Union accepted the vertical share, the revenue deficit grants, the local body grants and the disaster grants, and gave "due consideration" to ₹1,29,987 crore of sector-specific and ₹49,599 crore of state-specific grants. On 18 August 2025 the Ministry of Finance told the Lok Sabha, in terms, that "No State-specific grants were specifically accepted in the Explanatory Memorandum... Accordingly, no state-specific grants under 15th FC recommendations are being released."

**What can be established since.** The construction of one verb is now on the parliamentary record: "due consideration will be given" was construed by the Union, four and a half years later, as non-acceptance. Nil of ₹49,599 crore was released. The Sixteenth Finance Commission states as a finding of fact that the Union "largely discontinued releasing" sector- and state-specific grants after FC-XIV, and narrowed its own inquiry because of it. The scale continues: on FC-XVI's recommendations the Union in February 2026 accepted four blocks, accepted the borrowing ceilings in-principle, took note of the state-finance assessment, and said it would examine centrally sponsored scheme rationalisation, the power sector, subsidies and public-enterprise reform.

**Strongest case for.** Article 281 requires the Union to lay an explanatory memorandum, not to accept anything. The Finance Commission is advisory on Article 275 grants and the Constitution deliberately leaves the Union free to decline; a memorandum saying "rejected" in plain terms would be constitutionally identical and politically worse, and the Union's answer to Parliament shows the position was stated when asked. The sector-specific grants were, on the Union's own account, folded into existing and new centrally sponsored and central sector schemes — the memorandum says so — so the money question and the label question are different questions. A body that reserves its position on twelve blocks while accepting nine is engaging with the report, not evading it.

**Strongest case against.** A vocabulary of five terms with no definitions is not a scale but a discretion described in the language of agreement. The states, the Commission and Parliament all read "due consideration will be given" in February 2021 as something other than refusal, and the Union's own later answer confirms it meant refusal from the start — a construction that could not be tested for four and a half years because nothing defined the term. The consequence is measurable: nil of ₹49,599 crore. The Sixteenth Finance Commission's response was to narrow its own inquiry, which is the clearest evidence that the practice changes what the constitutional umpire is willing to recommend. And the Union's own aggregate for FC-XV grants-in-aid differs from the Commission's by about ₹1.88 lakh crore — close to the ₹1,79,586 crore it declined — so the declined money was silently removed from the denominator of the Union's own performance statistic.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** Every fact is agreed: the verbs, the amounts, the nil release and the parliamentary answer. The disagreement is whether an undefined vocabulary in a constitutionally mandated document is a defect or a permitted flexibility.

**Note on the verdict itself.** The record finds something real and firmly established — a graded vocabulary with no definitions, one term of which has now been construed on the record as refusal — and no objective was stated at announcement against which it could be scored. The Union never claimed the verbs meant anything in particular; that is the finding. Not contested: no competing reading of the evidence exists, only a competing view of whether the practice is a defect.

**Not measured — stated reason `never-defined`.** Definitions of the Union's five action verbs under Article 281 — No statute, rule, manual or memorandum defines "accepted", "accepted in-principle", "due consideration will be given", "takes note" or "will examine", or states what follows from each. The scale is inferred from usage, and one term has been construed only retrospectively, in answer to a parliamentary question. **What would close it:** A Ministry of Finance instruction, or a parliamentary answer stating the consequences of each verb.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Whether any of the ₹1,29,987 crore of unaccepted sector-specific grants was in fact released — The Union states the sectors would be given due consideration "while formulating and implementing existing and new Centrally Sponsored and Central Sector Schemes", which makes the money untraceable as Finance Commission grant. The state-specific half is resolved at nil; the sector-specific half is not. **What would close it:** The Demands for Grants for Transfers to States with scheme-level Actuals, or a parliamentary answer on the sector-specific head.

**Series this rests on.** fc-grants-share-of-gtr

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-105

**Sources** (5, T1×5):

- `T1` Explanatory Memorandum on the Action Taken on the Recommendations of the Fifteenth Finance Commission in its Final Report, Ministry of Finance, 1 February 2021 — https://fincomindia.nic.in/asset/doc/commission-reports/atr-summary-annex-III-english.pdf
- `T1` Explanatory Memorandum on the Action Taken on the Recommendations of the Sixteenth Finance Commission, Ministry of Finance, 1 February 2026 — https://www.indiabudget.gov.in/doc/16fc.pdf
- `T1` Lok Sabha Unstarred Question No. 4056, answered 18 August 2025, Ministry of Finance (Department of Economic Affairs), "Recommendations of the 15th Finance Commission" — https://sansad.in/getFile/lsapps/loksabhaquestions/annex/185/AU4056_1225Gn.pdf?source=lsapps
- `T1` Lok Sabha Unstarred Question No. 139, answered 22 July 2024, Ministry of Finance (Department of Expenditure), "15th Finance Commission" — https://sansad.in/getFile/lsapps/loksabhaquestions/annex/182/AU139_A79t7F.pdf?source=lsapps
- `T1` Sixteenth Finance Commission, Volume I — Main Report, Chapter 9 paras 9.7-9.9, 9.17-9.18 and 9.48-9.50 and Table 10.1 — https://fincomindia.nic.in/asset/doc/commission-reports/16th-FC/reports/Vol1-Main-Report.pdf

### B.38 · L-0162 — The GST Council's constitutional dispute-adjudication mechanism has never been established

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — statutory duty scored failed.*

`failed` · T3 · federalism/governance · institutional · 2016-09-12–2026-08-03 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-04

**History.** Created 2026-08-04. Never edited since.

**What this is.** Article 279A(11) provides that the GST Council "shall establish a mechanism to adjudicate any dispute" between the Union and one or more states, or between states, arising out of the Council's recommendations or their implementation. Ten years after the Council was constituted, no such mechanism exists. Activation has been formally demanded in Council at least four times by named members on named dates, and the Council's own committees index lists no adjudicating body.

**What was claimed at announcement.** The constitutional text itself: to adjudicate disputes arising out of the Council's recommendations or their implementation.

**What can be established since.** Punjab raised it at the 37th meeting on 20 September 2019, as recalled in the 39th minutes; a member raised "activating the dispute resolution mechanism" at the 38th meeting on 18 December 2019; Punjab at the 39th meeting on 14 March 2020 said "the time has become ripe to activate the dispute resolution mechanism envisaged in the Constitution under Article 279A and requested that this Agenda may be brought in the next meeting of the Council"; and Punjab at the 42nd meeting on 12 October 2020 "requested that in a time bound manner, the dispute resolution mechanism may be set up in 7 days... Alternatively, he suggested that the possibility of formation of GoM on the issue may also be explored." The Council's committees index lists the Fitment Committee, the Law Committee, two Groups of Officers, the GST Implementation Committee, a Grievances Redressal Committee, a State Coordination Committee, an All India Coordination Committee and an IT Grievance Redressal Committee. None adjudicates a Centre-state dispute: the Grievances Redressal Committee is a taxpayer body and the GST Appellate Tribunal hears taxpayer appeals under Chapter XVIII of the CGST Act.

**Strongest case for.** Article 279A(11) says the Council shall establish a mechanism and leaves the form, timing and trigger entirely to the Council, a body of thirty-one governments deciding by three-quarters weighted majority — so a mechanism can exist only if the states themselves, who hold two-thirds of the weight, carry it, and they have never put it to a vote. In practice the Council has divided once in nine years and worked by consensus in fifty-four other meetings, which is what a dispute mechanism would have been for. Disputes that could not be settled in Council have gone to the Supreme Court under Articles 131 and 32 and been heard there, so no state has been left without a forum. And building an adjudicating body inside a body whose Chairperson is a party would have raised its own objection.

**Strongest case against.** The Constitution uses the mandatory "shall", and the duty sits on a Council the Union chairs and whose agenda the Union controls; four recorded demands by a named member across three years produced no agenda item. The consequence is precise and visible in the other records of this phase: when the compensation dispute of 2020 turned on whether the Compensation Act contains a causal test and whether an unlegislated Council decision survives, there was no forum inside the design to answer it, and there still is none. A body in which the Centre alone holds a blocking third, in which no group of states can reach the 75 per cent threshold over the Chairperson's objection, and in which the only counterweight the Constitution provides has not been built, is not a shared sovereignty in the respect that matters most — what happens when the sharing fails.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** Both sides accept the text of Article 279A(11), the four demands, their dates and the contents of the committees index. The disagreement is about whether a duty on a collective body can be breached by the party that chairs it, which is an argument about responsibility for an agreed fact.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Failed against the objective stated in the constitutional text that created the duty. The mechanism is non-establishment over ten years despite four recorded demands — not a court striking anything down, and not a withdrawal by the enacting authority. The word "shall" in Article 279A(11) is the objective; the empty committees index is the outcome.

**Re-test when.** Re-test if the GST Council's committees index adds any body with an adjudicating remit over Union-state or state-state disputes, or if any Council minute records an agenda item on Article 279A(11).

**Sources** (5, T1×5):

- `T1` The Constitution (One Hundred and First Amendment) Act, 2016 — Article 279A(11) — https://gstcouncil.gov.in/sites/default/files/2024-02/consti-amend-act.pdf
- `T1` GST Council, minutes of the 38th meeting, 18 December 2019 — https://gstcouncil.gov.in/sites/default/files/Minutes/signed_minutes_38th_gst_council-meeting.pdf
- `T1` GST Council, minutes of the 39th meeting, 14 March 2020 — https://gstcouncil.gov.in/sites/default/files/Minutes/signed-minutes-39th_gstcm.pdf
- `T1` GST Council, minutes of the 42nd meeting, 12 October 2020 — https://gstcouncil.gov.in/sites/default/files/Minutes/42nd-gstc-minutes-signed_0.pdf
- `T1` GST Council, committees index, swept 3 August 2026 with a positive control on the Fitment and Law Committees — https://gstcouncil.gov.in/all-committees

### B.39 · L-0163 — Ten Tamil Nadu bills deemed assented by the Supreme Court, and the doctrine that assented them withdrawn seven months later

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — contested with a pending outside adjudication.*

`contested` · T3 · federalism/governance · episode · 2020-01-13–2025-11-20 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-04 · contested ground: interpretation

**History.** Created 2026-08-04. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** The range of 199 to 1,400 days is a subtraction from the two date columns of the Court's own table. The Supreme Court did not quantify the delay; its own characterisation is "an unduly long period of time". The counts ten and twelve are both correct for different questions and both appear in the same judgment: the petition and every operative conclusion speak of ten bills, while the factual table has twelve rows, the extra two being bills reserved in the first round and never re-enacted.

**What this is.** Twelve bills passed by the Tamil Nadu Legislative Assembly between 13 January 2020 and 28 April 2023 sat with the Governor for between 199 and 1,400 days. He acted on all twelve on 13 November 2023, three days after the Supreme Court issued notice on the State's writ petition, withholding assent to ten without any message for reconsideration and reserving two for the President. The Assembly repassed the ten in a special session on 18 November 2023; the Governor reserved them for the President ten days later. On 8 April 2025 the Supreme Court held the second-round reservation illegal, set aside the President's consequent actions and, under Article 142, declared the ten bills deemed assented as of 18 November 2023 — the only instance in Indian constitutional history in which assent has been deemed. On 13 May 2025 the President referred fourteen questions under Article 143. On 20 November 2025 a Constitution Bench held that the Governor is not bound by ministerial advice under Article 200, that the function is not justiciable on its merits, that courts may not prescribe timelines, and that the Constitution, "Article 142 even, does not allow for the concept of 'deemed assent' of Bills" — while expressly declining to disturb the relief already granted to Tamil Nadu.

**What can be established since.** The ten Acts stand. The doctrine that produced them does not, and no state can use it again. What survives on the states' side is a limited mandamus where inaction is "prolonged, unexplained, and indefinite" — a standard the Court did not define — and the holding that Article 361 immunises the person of the Governor but not the office.

**Strongest case for.** Article 200 confers a constitutional function on the Governor with no time limit written into it, and a Constitution Bench has now held the absence deliberate: the framers wrote deeming clauses into Articles 107, 108, 109(4), 109(5), 196, 197, 198(4), 198(5), 213 and 239B and wrote none into 200 or 201. "In the absence of constitutionally prescribed time limits, and the manner of exercise of power by the Governor, it would not be appropriate for this Court to judicially prescribe timelines." A two-judge Bench had read into Article 200 a timetable the Constitution does not contain and then enforced it with a remedy — deemed assent — that the larger Bench characterised as "virtually a takeover, and substitution, of the executive functions by the Judiciary". The bills at issue transferred the appointment of Vice-Chancellors from the Governor as Chancellor to the State Government, a subject on which Entry 66 of List I gives the Union a genuine interest, and reserving such bills for the President is the constitutional route for resolving exactly that question. The five-judge opinion also left Tamil Nadu's relief intact, which is the opposite of vindictiveness.

**Strongest case against.** The measured facts are not in dispute and were supplied by the Governor himself: 199 to 1,400 days of silence, ended three days after the Supreme Court issued notice, and a second-round reservation completed in ten days. Whatever Article 200 permits, a function performed in ten days when a court is watching and not at all for nearly four years when it is not is not a considered exercise of discretion. The bills were passed by an elected legislature, repassed without material change after the first refusal, and every one of them concerns universities the State funds. After 20 November 2025 the position is that this can recur with no timeline, no merits review, no duty to give reasons, no duty even to announce the decision, and no remedy beyond an undefined mandamus. Tamil Nadu's Acts survive; the mechanism that produced them does not.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** The clearest weighting case in the phase and the one most likely to be misread as a different-facts case. Nobody disputes that Article 200 has no time limit; nobody disputes that ten bills sat for over a year; nobody disputes the dates of the second-round reservation. The two sides differ on the weight to give the framers' silence, not on what the framers wrote or on what the Governor did.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Contested because the two sides do not disagree about a single fact. Every date is admitted, and most of them were supplied by the Governor. The dispute is entirely about what Article 200 permits, and the Supreme Court gave two answers to that in seven months. Not awaiting-adjudication: the adjudication has happened, twice, and the record carries both.

**Not measured — stated reason `never-defined`.** The Governor's reasons for the 199 to 1,400 days — Neither judgment records any reason, because Article 200 imposes no duty to give one. After the November 2025 opinion there is no timeline to breach and no merits review, so no instrument will ever produce this quantity.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** The Presidential disposal of Tamil Nadu Bills 25/2022 and 26/2022 — The Supreme Court's own factual table records the President's action on these two bills as "Status not on record" — seventeen months after they were reserved. Positive control: the same table gives the President's decision date to the day for eight of the other ten bills, so the column exists and is populated; the two gaps are gaps in what was placed before the Court. This is a court declaring an absence in the Union's own return. **What would close it:** Disclosure by the Union of the disposal of the two reserved bills.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** The Presidential reasons for withholding assent to seven Tamil Nadu bills — Article 201 obliges the President to declare assent or withholding and requires no statement of reasons, and the Constitution Bench has held the Article 201 function non-justiciable. Nothing therefore obliges the reasons to be recorded at all, so there is no held document a compulsion could reach - which is what distinguishes not-collected from not-published on the schema's own test. The dates are in the Court's table; the reasons are in neither judgment. Not graded withheld: no named requester, specific request or date of refusal was identified. Re-graded from not-published on 2026-08-04, because the record's own text already argued this and the value contradicted it.

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-110

**Sources** (2, T1×2):

- `T1` Supreme Court of India, The State of Tamil Nadu v. The Governor of Tamil Nadu, W.P.(C) No. 1239 of 2023, 2025 INSC 481, 8 April 2025 — paras 3-13 (the twelve-bill table), 250, 391, 434-437 and 435(c) — https://api.sci.gov.in/supremecourt/2023/45314/45314_2023_11_1501_60770_Judgement_08-Apr-2025.pdf
- `T1` Supreme Court of India, In re: Assent, Withholding or Reservation of Bills by the Governor and the President of India, Special Reference No. 1 of 2025, 2025 INSC 1333, 20 November 2025 — paras 26-28, 111-128 and 165.1-165.14 — https://api.sci.gov.in/supremecourt/2025/39157/39157_2025_1_1501_66169_Judgement_20-Nov-2025.pdf

### B.40 · L-0164 — The Constitution sets a deadline for the elected legislature and none for the Union's appointee, and both sides argue from the same silence

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — statutory duty scored no-objective — the comparison set; put in question by the first external review — heavily cited, non-evaluative, no source conflict.*

`no-objective` · T3 · federalism/governance · institutional · 1950-01-26–2025-11-20 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-04

**History.** Created 2026-08-04. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** The pendency of a bill becomes measurable only at the moment it stops being pending, and in every retrieved case that moment was caused by the litigation — Punjab's Money Bills recommended "after the institution of the Petition", Kerala's eight bills disposed of eight days after notice, Tamil Nadu's twelve acted on three days after the Punjab judgment. The observation instrument is the writ petition, which is a selection effect on the entire evidence base and is not correctable from within the record.

**What this is.** Article 200 gives the Governor three options on a bill presented to him — assent, withhold assent, or reserve it for the President — and attaches the words "as soon as possible" only to returning a bill for reconsideration. Article 201 gives the President two options and no period at all. The single fixed period in the whole scheme is six months, and it binds the State legislature to reconsider a returned bill. There is no deeming provision, no lapse and no default anywhere in the scheme. After 20 November 2025 there is also no judicially enforceable clock, no merits review, no duty to give reasons and no duty to announce the decision.

**What can be established since.** The Punjab judgment records at paragraph 8 that "the Governor did not 'declare' in any public notification that he is withholding his assent to the Bills" — so the deciding officer need not announce the decision, and no register follows from the Constitution. The measurable consequence is that no national count of bills pending with Governors exists: neither the April 2025 judgment nor the November 2025 Constitution Bench opinion cites any aggregate figure, and a Constitution Bench decided the legal effect of a quantity without either party placing a national figure for it before the Court. What exists instead is bill-by-bill, state-by-state, only for litigated states, and only at the moment of litigation: twelve rows for Tamil Nadu, eight named bills for Kerala with a pendency range of seven to twenty-three months, four bills plus three Money Bills for Punjab.

**Strongest case for.** The framers wrote deeming clauses into ten other Articles and none into 200 or 201, and a five-judge Bench found that decisive. The office is a constitutional check, not a postbox: the second proviso to Article 200 makes reservation mandatory in a defined case, which shows the framers contemplated the Governor stopping a bill. The elasticity is deliberate — "The text of Articles 200 and 201, has been framed in such a manner, so as to provide a sense of elasticity" — and the considerations the function serves are, in the Union's own words in the Presidential Reference, "polycentric considerations, inter alia being federalism, uniformity of laws, integrity and security of the nation, doctrine of separation of powers". A deadline on a discretion is a different constitution from the one that was written, and amending it is Parliament's work, not a court's.

**Strongest case against.** "Shall declare" admits of no inaction, and a power with no stated period must be exercised in reasonable time. The concrete consequence of the contrary reading was stated by the Court itself in 2023: an unelected appointee "would be in a position to virtually veto the functioning of the legislative domain by a duly elected legislature by simply declaring that assent is withheld without any further recourse". The measured facts support the concern — 1,400 days in Tamil Nadu, seven to twenty-three months in Kerala — and the six-month clock the Constitution does contain runs against the elected House, not against the appointee. Three official commissions have recommended fixing the gap and none has been implemented.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** Every fact in both cases is agreed: the text, the absence of deeming clauses, the dates in each litigated state, and the holdings of both 2025 benches. The two sides differ on the weight to give the framers' silence. This is the structural statement that distinguishes the assent dispute from the fiscal disputes in this phase, where the parties genuinely disagree about a subtraction.

**Note on the verdict itself.** No objective was stated at announcement because the subject is a constitutional design, not a measure. The record finds something real and firmly established — an asymmetry in the text, and the absence of any register following from it — and there is no claim to score. The value asserts nothing about the finding's quality.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** A national count of bills pending with Governors — Nobody is shown to collect it. There is no reporting obligation anywhere in Articles 200 or 201, a Governor need not notify anyone that a bill is with him, and the Punjab judgment records that no public declaration was made. Neither 2025 Supreme Court record cites an aggregate figure, and none of the retrieved instruments carries a series. The quantity becomes measurable only when it stops being pending, and in every retrieved case that moment was caused by the litigation. **What would close it:** A Ministry of Home Affairs or Inter-State Council return requiring Governors to report bills received and pending.

**Not measured — stated reason `never-defined`.** How long a bill sits between the legislature passing it and Raj Bhavan receiving it — Every dated record retrieved gives a date of forwarding or presentation and a date of gubernatorial action, and none distinguishes passage, transmission and receipt. The Kerala order gives no per-bill presentation date at all, only the Court's range. The clock's start is never independently evidenced.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The Ministry of Home Affairs D.O. letter No. 23/33/1992-Judl of 22 September 1992 conveying the Cabinet-approved guidelines for disposal of State legislative proposals — Cited by name inside a Ministry memorandum that the Supreme Court reproduced in full, and entirely unretrieved. The two memoranda of 4 February 2016 that rely on it are public only because a State sued and the Court chose to print them; no independent government publication of either was found. Not graded withheld: no named requester, specific request or date of refusal. **What would close it:** Publication by the Ministry of Home Affairs of the 1992 guidelines and the 2016 memoranda.

**Sources** (5, T1×5):

- `T1` The Constitution of India, Legislative Department, as on 1 May 2024 — Articles 200, 201, 153-159 — https://cdnbbsr.s3waas.gov.in/s380537a945c7aaa788ccfcdf1b99b5d8f/uploads/2024/07/20240716890312078.pdf
- `T1` Supreme Court of India, State of Punjab v. Principal Secretary to the Governor of Punjab, 2023 INSC 1017, 10 November 2023 — paras 4, 5, 8, 9, 10, 25, 26 — https://api.sci.gov.in/supremecourt/2023/44896/44896_2023_1_19_48220_Judgement_10-Nov-2023.pdf
- `T1` Supreme Court of India, State of Kerala v. Governor of Kerala, W.P.(C) 1264/2023, order of 29 November 2023 — paras 2, 4, 6(v), 11 — https://api.sci.gov.in/supremecourt/2023/45758/45758_2023_1_3_48678_Order_29-Nov-2023.pdf
- `T1` Supreme Court of India, 2025 INSC 1333, 20 November 2025 — paras 113, 115, 117 and 165.1-165.14 — https://api.sci.gov.in/supremecourt/2025/39157/39157_2025_1_1501_66169_Judgement_20-Nov-2025.pdf
- `T1` Supreme Court of India, 2025 INSC 481, 8 April 2025 — paras 103, 137-142, 385 and 387 (the two MHA memoranda of 4 February 2016 reproduced in full) — https://api.sci.gov.in/supremecourt/2023/45314/45314_2023_11_1501_60770_Judgement_08-Apr-2025.pdf

### B.41 · L-0165 — Delhi: a Constitution Bench holding reversed by statute in eight days, under an express constitutional licence to do so

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — contested with a pending outside adjudication.*

`contested` · T2 · federalism/governance · reform · 2023-05-11–2023-08-07 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-04 · contested ground: criterion

**History.** Created 2026-08-04. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** "Lieutenant Governor" is not one office. Delhi's holds under Article 239AA and the GNCTD Act 1991; Puducherry's officer is the "Administrator" throughout the Government of Union Territories Act 1963, holding under Article 239A and designated Lieutenant Governor by notification. Their statutory powers differ, and both differ from a Governor: on a re-passed bill both the Delhi Lieutenant Governor and the Puducherry Administrator may by statute reserve it for the President, where Article 200's first proviso says a Governor "shall not withhold assent therefrom". Any cross-territory count of second-round reservations compares a statutory power with an alleged illegality. This record is bounded against the phase-12 records that own the Jammu and Kashmir Lieutenant Governor.

**What this is.** On 11 May 2023 a Constitution Bench held that the Government of the National Capital Territory of Delhi has legislative and executive power over Services and that the Lieutenant Governor is bound by the Council of Ministers on them. On 19 May 2023 — eight days later — Part IV-A of the Government of National Capital Territory of Delhi Act took effect, creating a National Capital Civil Service Authority, making its subject matter one on which the Lieutenant Governor acts "in his sole discretion", and providing that on a difference of opinion "the decision of the Lieutenant Governor shall be final". It was carried into permanent law by Act 19 of 2023.

**What was claimed at announcement.** The Part's own heading is the only objective statement retrieved: "PROVISIONS RELATING TO THE MAINTENANCE OF THE DEMOCRATIC AND ADMINISTRATIVE BALANCE IN THE GOVERNANCE OF NATIONAL CAPITAL TERRITORY OF DELHI".

**Strongest case for.** Article 239AA(7)(b) expressly authorises exactly this: a law made under 239AA(7)(a) "shall not be deemed to be an amendment of this Constitution for the purposes of article 368 notwithstanding that it contains any provision which amends or has the effect of amending, this Constitution." The framers of that clause contemplated Parliament legislating around the constitutional arrangement for Delhi, and Delhi is the national capital: the Union's stake in the administration of the seat of government is not a pretext. The 2023 Bench itself described Delhi as sui generis — a category Parliament may legislate for — and the Authority is chaired by the Chief Minister, not by the Lieutenant Governor.

**Strongest case against.** The 2023 Bench held the executive power of the National Capital Territory "co-extensive with its legislative power", and the statute that followed eight days later made the very subject of that holding one on which the Lieutenant Governor acts in his sole discretion. A three-member Authority chaired by the Chief Minister but including a Chief Secretary appointed by the Central Government, deciding by majority, can outvote the elected head two to one on his own officers — and even its recommendation is subject to the Lieutenant Governor's final say. An express licence to legislate around a judgment is not an argument for using it the week after one. And Delhi votes as a State in the GST Council, so the Union treats the same government as a state for fiscal purposes and as a subordinate administration for its own civil service.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** Unusually clean as a weighting case: both sides accept the text of Article 239AA(7)(b), the dates, the composition of the Authority and the terms of the 2023 holding. The disagreement is entirely about whether an express constitutional licence should be used eight days after a judgment.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Contested because both readings rest on the same admitted text and the same dates. The measure was not struck down and was not withdrawn, so neither failed nor reversed applies; and the constitutional question it raises has been answered — Article 239AA(7)(b) authorises it — so nothing is awaiting adjudication.

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-110

**Sources** (3, T1×3):

- `T1` Supreme Court of India, Government of NCT of Delhi v. Union of India, 2023, judgment of 11 May 2023 — paras 162 and 164(i) — https://api.sci.gov.in/supremecourt/2016/29357/29357_2016_1_1501_44512_Judgement_11-May-2023.pdf
- `T1` Government of National Capital Territory of Delhi Act, 1991, consolidated text, Legislative Department — Part IV-A, ss. 41, 45A(d), 45E(3), 45H(3) and s. 24 — https://www.indiacode.nic.in/bitstream/123456789/1923/1/a1992-01.pdf
- `T1` The Constitution of India, Legislative Department, as on 1 May 2024 — Article 239AA, including 239AA(7)(a)-(b), and Article 366(26B) — https://cdnbbsr.s3waas.gov.in/s380537a945c7aaa788ccfcdf1b99b5d8f/uploads/2024/07/20240716890312078.pdf

### B.42 · L-0166 — Article 356 since 2014: four proclamations outside Jammu and Kashmir, and no official list of any of them

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — heavily cited, non-evaluative, no source conflict.*

`no-objective` · T3 · federalism/governance · episode · 2016-01-26–2026-02-04 · confidence medium · last researched 2026-08-04

**History.** Created 2026-08-04. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** Only the Uttarakhand and Maharashtra rows rest on a T1 instrument. Arunachal Pradesh 2016 and Manipur 2025 are T4 in this drop and are flagged as such. The list excludes Jammu and Kashmir by phase boundary and is therefore not comparable with any published national total. Puducherry 2021 is section 51 of the Government of Union Territories Act 1963 and is not an Article 356 proclamation.

**What this is.** Excluding Jammu and Kashmir, which the phase-12 records own, Article 356 has been invoked four times since May 2014: Arunachal Pradesh from 26 January 2016 for about twenty-four days, Uttarakhand from 27 March 2016 for about forty-five days, Maharashtra from 12 November 2019 for about eleven days, and Manipur from 13 February 2025 to 4 February 2026 — about 357 days. Puducherry in 2021 is not in this list: it was section 51 of the Government of Union Territories Act 1963, a different statute. The Ministry of Home Affairs, whose own Annual Report describes "imposition of President's Rule" as part of its Centre-State Division's mandate, has never published a list of Article 356 proclamations in any Annual Report checked, and its Centre-State Division page carries none.

**What can be established since.** For Uttarakhand and Maharashtra the instrument is the Rajya Sabha laying record under Article 356(3), which names the Proclamation's G.S.R. number, the accompanying Order, and separately lays "the Report of the Governor... recommending the issue of the Proclamation". That laying record is a genuine one-ledger instrument: the Union's proclamation and the Governor's report go before the House together. For Arunachal Pradesh 2016 and Manipur 2025 no such record was obtained, and no Gazette notification was read for any of the four. The last official cumulative figure of any kind is the National Commission to Review the Working of the Constitution's count of 111 invocations as at 2002 — which expressly bundles thirteen applications of section 51 of the Government of Union Territories Act with Article 356 proper, and subtracts ten applied "technically due to the mechanics of the Constitution", leaving 54 with "apparent circumstances to warrant invocation", 13 of "possible misuse" and 18 of "clear misuse".

**Strongest case for.** Every proclamation is laid before both Houses within two months under Article 356(3) and must be approved by resolution of Parliament, so Parliament is the register and a ministry list would be duplicative. Four invocations in twelve years, three of them lasting between eleven and forty-five days, is a marked restraint against the historical record the NCRWC counted; on that count the power was used 111 times in the first fifty-two years. The one long proclamation, Manipur, followed sustained civil conflict, which is the circumstance Article 356 was written for.

**Strongest case against.** A laying record is a scatter of individual entries across decades of debates, findable only if you already know the date — which is exactly what a series is for and exactly what is missing. The nodal ministry names the subject in its own mandate and publishes no list, so the count in this record had to be assembled from two Rajya Sabha debate volumes and two rows that have no T1 instrument at all. The only cumulative figure that exists is twenty-four years old and mixes two statutes, so no published number separates Article 356 from section 51 of the Government of Union Territories Act, and any modern count that adds Puducherry 2021 to Uttarakhand 2016 is doing the same bundling without saying so.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** Both sides accept the four instances, the laying requirement and the absence of a ministry list. The disagreement is whether parliamentary laying discharges the duty of record, which is a weighting of an agreed record.

**Note on the verdict itself.** No objective was stated at announcement: a proclamation is an exercise of a constitutional power, not a measure with a claimed outcome, and no Union document states what any of the four was to achieve. The record's finding is the absence of an official register, which is firmly established by a sweep of the nodal ministry's own Annual Reports and division page.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** An official list of Article 356 proclamations — Not published by the Ministry of Home Affairs in any of four Annual Reports checked, and absent from its Centre-State Division page. The only cumulative figure of any kind is the NCRWC's 111 as at 2002, which bundles Article 356 with section 51 of the Government of Union Territories Act 1963 and is not updated. **What would close it:** A statement in the Ministry of Home Affairs Annual Report listing proclamations, their dates, durations and revocations.

**Sources** (5, T1×5):

- `T1` Rajya Sabha, Official Debates, Vol. 239 No. 1, 25 April 2016, p. 323 — the Article 356(3) laying record for Uttarakhand, G.S.R. 341(E) and 342(E), with the Governor's report — https://cms.rajyasabha.nic.in/UploadedFiles/Debates/OfficialDebatesDatewise/Floor/239/F25.04.2016.pdf
- `T1` Rajya Sabha, Official Debates, Vol. 250 No. 3, 20 November 2019, p. 1 — the Article 356(3) laying record for Maharashtra, G.S.R. 837(E) and 838(E), with the Governor's report — https://cms.rajyasabha.nic.in/UploadedFiles/Debates/OfficialDebatesDatewise/Floor/250/F20.11.2019.pdf
- `T1` National Commission to Review the Working of the Constitution, Final Report Volume I, Chapter 8, para 8.16 — the 111 invocations and their breakdown — https://web.archive.org/web/20030225215302id_/http://lawmin.nic.in:80/ncrwc/finalreport/v1ch8.htm
- `T1` Ministry of Home Affairs, Annual Report 2024-25, Chapter 17 (Centre-State relations) — https://www.mha.gov.in/sites/default/files/AREnglish_24032026.pdf
- `T1` Ministry of Home Affairs, Centre-State Division page — https://www.mha.gov.in/en/divisionofmha/centre-state-division

### B.43 · L-0167 — Three commissions recommended consulting the Chief Minister on the appointment of Governors; the Inter-State Council has met eleven times in thirty-six years and not once in the last ten

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — statutory duty scored no-objective — the comparison set; put in question by the first external review — heavily cited, non-evaluative, no source conflict.*

`no-objective` · T3 · federalism/governance · institutional · 2002-03-31–2026-03-24 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-04

**History.** Created 2026-08-04. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** A number degraded in transmission through two official documents and then printed by the Supreme Court: 2025 INSC 481 para 141, reciting Punchhi reciting the NCRWC, gives the recommended Governor time limit as "a period of four months"; the NCRWC report's own text at para 8.14.4, read directly, says six months. The three-month figure for the President is consistent across both. Flagged, not reconciled. Two Ministry of Home Affairs Annual Reports also give the Punchhi report's submission date one day apart, 30 and 31 March 2010.

**What this is.** The National Commission to Review the Working of the Constitution recommended in 2002 that a Governor be appointed "after consultation with the Chief Minister of that State", that removal or transfer follow a similar procedure, that the Sarkaria criteria be applied, and that assent be subject to time limits. Sarkaria (1988) had held that the Governor "must abide by the advice of his Council of Ministers" under Article 200 save in rare and exceptional cases of patent unconstitutionality. Punchhi (2010) held that "concrete timelines are sine qua non". None has been implemented. Punchhi's 273 recommendations went to the eleventh meeting of the Inter-State Council on 16 July 2016, which referred them to its Standing Committee; the Standing Committee met on 9 April 2017, 25 November 2017 and 25 May 2018. The Ministry of Home Affairs Annual Report 2024-25 then states: "It has been decided to obtain updated comments of the State Governments/UT Administrations on the recommendations of the Standing Committee. In view of this, a meeting of the ISC to discuss the Punchhi Commission recommendations could not be held."

**Strongest case for.** Article 155 confers the appointment power on the President without qualification, and adding a consultation requirement is a constitutional amendment, not an administrative decision — Punchhi itself advocated implementation "by way of a Constitutional Amendment". The Inter-State Council is a consultative body of the States themselves, and it was the Chief Ministers who asked in 2016 that the Standing Committee examine the recommendations first, so the delay is, on the Union's own record, a delay the States asked for. The recommendations are also not costless: consultation on the appointment of a constitutional functionary whose function includes reserving a State's bills for the President would give the government whose bills are at stake a say in choosing the officer who decides.

**Strongest case against.** The forum has met eleven times in thirty-six years and not once in the last ten. A body that does not meet cannot decide, and a recommendation referred to a committee of a body that does not meet is not under consideration in any meaningful sense. The stated reason for the latest non-meeting is that updated comments are being obtained — a process that has run for eight years since the Standing Committee last met. And the two documents most cited in every Governor debate in India, the Sarkaria and Punchhi reports, could not be read at all in this run: interstatecouncil.gov.in resolves but its port 443 is closed and no Wayback capture of any chapter returns 200, so both are known here only through what the NCRWC report and the Supreme Court quote of them.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** Both sides would accept every date, every recommendation and the ministry's own account of why the Council has not met. The disagreement is about whether an unfinished process that has been unfinished for eight years is consideration or its absence.

**Note on the verdict itself.** No objective was stated at announcement. The Annual Report states a status, not an objective, and no Union document has ever claimed that the commissions' recommendations would be implemented by a date or at all. The finding — a constitutional consultative forum that has met eleven times in thirty-six years and not once in the last ten — is firmly established from the ministry's own report.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The reports of the Sarkaria (1988) and Punchhi (2010) Commissions on Centre-State Relations, as documents — interstatecouncil.gov.in resolves on six independent resolvers to one address with port 443 closed and port 80 redirecting to the closed port; interstatecouncil.nic.in returns NXDOMAIN; and no Internet Archive capture of any chapter PDF returns 200. All three retrieval modes were tried. This is recorded as an availability failure resting on unreachability from this environment, not as proof of non-publication. **What would close it:** Republication of either report on any reachable official host.

**Sources** (4, T1×4):

- `T1` National Commission to Review the Working of the Constitution, Final Report Volume I, Chapter 8, paras 8.14.4 and 8.16 — https://web.archive.org/web/20030225215302id_/http://lawmin.nic.in:80/ncrwc/finalreport/v1ch8.htm
- `T1` Ministry of Home Affairs, Annual Report 2024-25, paras 17.5 and 17.7 — the eleven Inter-State Council meetings and the Punchhi status — https://www.mha.gov.in/sites/default/files/AREnglish_24032026.pdf
- `T1` Ministry of Home Affairs, Annual Report 2016-17, paras 4.7-4.8 — the eleventh Inter-State Council meeting and the reference to the Standing Committee — https://www.mha.gov.in/sites/default/files/AnnualReport_16_17.pdf
- `T1` Supreme Court of India, 2025 INSC 481, 8 April 2025 — paras 103 (Sarkaria 5.6.13), 137-142 (Punchhi) — https://api.sci.gov.in/supremecourt/2023/45314/45314_2023_11_1501_60770_Judgement_08-Apr-2025.pdf

### B.44 · L-0168 — West Bengal's MGNREGA money stopped under section 27 on 9 March 2022, and restored by a court rather than by the Ministry

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — contested with a pending outside adjudication.*

`contested` · T3 · federalism/welfare/employment/governance · episode · 2022-03-09–2026-06-01 · confidence medium · last researched 2026-08-04 · contested ground: evidence-withheld

**History.** Created 2026-08-04. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** Two dates circulate for this stoppage and they are two different events: 26 December 2021 is the last wage instalment (T4) and 9 March 2022 is the Union's own date for the stoppage of release under section 27 (T1). The earlier date must not be propagated as the date of the stoppage. The Calcutta High Court order of 18 June 2025 and the Supreme Court order of 27 October 2025 are held at T4 only: the court portals that would supply them are CAPTCHA-gated, completing a CAPTCHA is out of bounds, and a reproduction on a private portal is not retrieval. The pending-liability figures the Union has given are three different quantities and must not be summed or compared.

**What this is.** Section 27 of the Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Act 2005 allowed the Central Government, on a complaint about the issue or improper utilisation of funds and if "prima facie satisfied that there is a case", to "order stoppage of release of funds to the Scheme". Central release to West Bengal stopped on 9 March 2022 and did not resume for more than four years. The Union's own by-state table shows releases of ₹11,454.05 crore in FY2020-21, ₹7,507.80 crore in FY2021-22 and ₹0.00 in FY2022-23. Restoration came from the Calcutta High Court, not from the Ministry: an order of 18 June 2025, departmental orders of 6 December 2025, and resumption with effect from 1 June 2026.

**What can be established since.** The Union's stated ground is inspection findings of financial misappropriation, execution of non-permissible activities, splitting of works and lack of transparency, and non-compliance with Central Government directives. The antecedent step was an unapproved upward revision of the State's labour budget for FY2021-22. New job cards issued in West Bengal fell from 16.82 lakh in FY2020-21 to 8.25 lakh and then 2.15 lakh — but Bihar, against which no stoppage was ordered, fell from 26.68 lakh to 11.37 lakh over the same two years, so the series cannot separate the stoppage from whatever else was moving. The power has been used once, against one state, and the Union has told the Lok Sabha in terms that no direction under section 27 was issued to Tamil Nadu.

**Strongest case for.** Section 27 is a statute passed by Parliament, and Parliament wrote the power into a scheme that spends Union money in a state the Union does not administer. The threshold is a complaint plus prima facie satisfaction, and the Union has stated the ground on the record and repeatedly: central team inspections in nineteen districts finding financial misappropriation, non-permissible works and splitting of works. The power has been used once in twenty years, which is not a pattern. The stoppage runs to the Scheme, not to the State, and every other central scheme continued; the Union's own account is that more than ₹1.10 lakh crore has gone to West Bengal for rural development since FY2014-15. And the State's own conduct is in the record too: the Union complains that a centrally funded housing scheme was renamed, and an unapproved labour-budget revision preceded the stoppage.

**Strongest case against.** The power runs, on its own words, to "the Scheme", and there is no notice, no hearing, no time limit, no ceiling and no appeal in the section — "prima facie satisfied" is the entire threshold. It was used for fifty-one months against one state, and released only when a High Court ordered it. A statutory entitlement to a hundred days' work was suspended for the population of a state on the strength of findings in nineteen districts, and no instrument states why the whole state rather than the nineteen districts. Wages are a Union liability under the Act and the pending liability the Union itself acknowledges includes a wage component. And the order itself has never been published: the Union describes it in Parliament and does not produce it, so the reasons cannot be tested by anyone.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** The dates, the statutory provision, the release figures and the inspection findings are all the Union's own, and the State does not contest the release figures. The disagreement is about proportionality and about who bore the cost — which is a weighting of one agreed record, not two ledgers. The one thing that would settle it, the section 27 order with its reasons, is unpublished, and its absence bears on both sides equally.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Contested rather than failed: the two sides weight the same facts differently and no instrument in the record adjudicates between a proportionate enforcement action and a disproportionate one. The section 27 order itself has never been published, so the reasons that would settle it are not on any record.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The section 27 order or letter to the Government of West Bengal — No Ministry of Rural Development order invoking section 27 was located on nrega.nic.in or rural.gov.in. The Union describes the order in Parliament and does not publish it. This is not-published and NOT withheld: no named requester, no specific request and no date of refusal is established, and without all three the definition is not met. **What would close it:** Publication of the order by the Ministry of Rural Development, or its production in the pending litigation.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Any official statement of centrally sponsored scheme funds withheld, by state and by year — Asked in Parliament for funds released and pending by state, the Department of Expenditure answered with releases only; the pending half was not answered and no withheld column exists in any retrieved Union instrument. Positive control: the same corpus answers on adjacent subjects. **What would close it:** A Union Budget statement or parliamentary answer carrying a withheld or pending column by state.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** West Bengal's count of active MGNREGA workers at the date of the stoppage — The MGNREGA management information system holds active-worker counts and no dated figure for 8 or 9 March 2022 was retrieved in this run. Recorded as a retrieval gap in this drop rather than as an absence in the world.

**Series this rests on.** wb-mgnrega-funds-released, bihar-mgnrega-funds-released, wb-mgnrega-new-jobcards, bihar-mgnrega-new-jobcards

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-107, P-114

**Sources** (4, T1×4):

- `T1` PIB / Ministry of Rural Development, "Status of MGNREGS Jobcards and Funds Due for Allocation in West Bengal", 5 December 2023 — carrying the text and both Annexures of Lok Sabha Unstarred Question No. 254 of 5 December 2023: the 9 March 2022 section 27 sentence, Annexure-I (new job cards by state) and Annexure-II (funds released by state, ₹ lakh) — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=1982799
- `T1` PIB / Ministry of Rural Development, 4 August 2025 — the nineteen-district investigation with three named categories of irregularity, the section 27 sentence, and scheme-wise cumulative figures for West Bengal since FY2014-15 — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2152391
- `T1` PIB / Ministry of Rural Development, "Central Government rejects allegations of West Bengal Government on discrimination in rural development schemes", 2 October 2023 — the Union's advocacy case in full, including the renaming complaint and the action-taken-report sequence — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=1963198
- `T1` The Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Act, 2005 (Act 42 of 2005), India Code — section 27(2) and section 22(1)(a) — https://www.indiacode.nic.in/handle/123456789/4649
