### B.45 · L-0178 — Centrally sponsored scheme releases to West Bengal fell 67 per cent in five years while the national total fell 6, and the Union states four general conditions and no state-specific reason

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — contested on a missing-evidence ground.*

`contested` · T2 · federalism/welfare/governance · episode · 2020-04-01–2025-03-31 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-04 · contested ground: evidence-withheld

**History.** Created 2026-08-04. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** Releases under centrally sponsored schemes only. The same answer's second annexure shows West Bengal's Central Sector releases rising 57.5 per cent over the identical years, and that annexure is NOT a geographic allocation — Delhi carries 59 per cent of its national total because releases to Delhi-headquartered central agencies are booked against Delhi. Neither annexure may be quoted without the other, and the second may not be quoted as a state-wise distribution at all.

**What this is.** Answering the Lok Sabha on 1 December 2025 from the Public Financial Management System, the Department of Expenditure published centrally sponsored scheme releases by state for FY2020-21 to FY2024-25. West Bengal falls from 27,406.79 to 9,081.21 crore, a fall of 66.9 per cent, monotonic in every one of the four transitions with no reversal. Over the same five years the all-India total falls 5.8 per cent, Bihar rises 21.4 per cent and Tamil Nadu falls 10.9 per cent from a peak in the fourth year. West Bengal goes from the second-largest recipient in the table to smaller than Punjab, Himachal Pradesh or Tripura.

**What can be established since.** As a share of the all-India total West Bengal falls from 7.10 to 2.50 per cent. The question asked whether the Government was aware that delayed release had adversely affected states; the reply neither confirms nor denies, and instead restates the release conditions in full: funds are released "as per the scheme guidelines, demand from State, submission of Utilization Certificates of the funds released earlier and contribution of commensurate State Share". Those four conditions are the Union's stated levers. The answer names them without saying which, if any, applied to any state, and offers no explanation for any state's trajectory. The same answer's second annexure cuts the other way and must be carried with the first: West Bengal's CENTRAL SECTOR scheme releases ROSE 57.5 per cent over the identical five years, from 15,898.10 to 25,035.43 crore. That annexure also contains an artefact that disqualifies it as a geography: Delhi appears at 5.56 lakh crore in FY2024-25, 59 per cent of the national Central Sector total, because releases to central ministries and implementing agencies headquartered in Delhi are booked against Delhi, and the document's own footnote warns that "The figures include release to State Treasury as well as Implementing Agencies."

**Strongest case for.** Centrally sponsored schemes are conditional transfers by design and the conditions are published, uniform and old: guidelines, demand, utilisation certificates for money already released, and a matching state share. A state that does not demand, does not certify, or does not match receives less, and that is the scheme working rather than failing. The Union releases to twenty-eight states on one system and publishes the whole table, including the rows that embarrass it. On the same table Bihar rose and Tamil Nadu peaked in the fourth year, so no national squeeze is visible. And the second annexure of the same answer shows West Bengal's Central Sector releases rising by more than half over the same period — money going to the same state through a different channel, which is difficult to reconcile with a policy of squeezing a state.

**Strongest case against.** The fall is monotonic across four transitions, which no ordinary conditionality produces: utilisation certificates and matching shares vary year to year, and a state that failed them would recover in some year. It did not, in any year. The Union holds the reason — it decides each release against four conditions it names — and when asked in Parliament whether delay had harmed states, answered with the conditions rather than with the facts of any state. The largest single component of the decline is MGNREGA, where a statutory power to stop release was exercised against this state and no other, and restored by a court rather than by the Ministry. And the Central Sector rise is not an answer: those releases go substantially to implementing agencies rather than to the state government, on the same document's own footnote, so the two annexures do not measure the same relationship.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** Both sides read the same two annexures of the same answer and neither disputes a figure in either. The disagreement is about which annexure measures the relationship in question and about what a monotonic decline implies in the absence of a stated reason — a weighting of one agreed record.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Contested rather than failed: the same table supports a reading of conditions unmet and a reading of a state squeezed, and no instrument in the record adjudicates between them. What would settle it — a statement of which condition was unmet, by which state, in which year, on which scheme — is not published by anyone, and the answer that was asked for it gave the conditions in the abstract instead.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Which of the four stated release conditions was unmet, by which state, in which year — Asked in Parliament whether delayed release had adversely affected states, the Department of Expenditure restated the four conditions and applied none of them to any state. No Union instrument publishes a condition-by-state or a withheld-by-state column; the published table has a released column and nothing beside it. **What would close it:** A parliamentary answer or Department of Expenditure statement giving, by state and scheme, the amount due, the amount released and the condition on which any balance was held.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Centrally sponsored scheme money DUE to each state against money released — The Union publishes releases only. Without an entitlement column a fall cannot be separated from a smaller claim, and the answer that was asked for the pending half did not supply it. **What would close it:** A Union Budget statement or parliamentary answer carrying a pending or withheld column by state.

**Series this rests on.** wb-css-releases, bihar-css-releases, tn-css-releases, css-releases-all-states, wb-mgnrega-funds-released, bihar-mgnrega-funds-released

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-106, P-107, P-108, P-114

**Sources** (3, T1×3):

- `T1` Lok Sabha Unstarred Question No. 142, "Release of funds under various Centrally Sponsored Schemes", answered 1 December 2025, Ministry of Finance (Department of Expenditure) — Annexure-I (centrally sponsored scheme releases by state, FY2020-21 to FY2024-25), Annexure-II (Central Sector scheme releases by state, with the implementing-agency footnote), and the reply stating the four release conditions. Source stated in the document: Public Financial Management System — https://sansad.in/getFile/lsapps/loksabhaquestions/annex/186/AU142_6FgksB.pdf?source=lsapps
- `T1` PIB / Ministry of Rural Development, 5 December 2023, carrying Annexure-II to Lok Sabha Unstarred Question No. 254 of 5 December 2023 — MGNREGA funds released by state, the largest single component of the decline — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=1982799
- `T1` Rajya Sabha Starred Question No. *18, answered 21 July 2026 — the Union's counter-argument that cesses "are primarily utilized to finance Centrally Sponsored Schemes (CSS), which are implemented by State Governments", which makes this table the test of that argument — https://sansad.in/getFile/annex/271/AS18_7lp2Vc.pdf?source=pqars

### B.46 · L-0179 — Samagra Shiksha: two states received nil, one had it paid back in arrears and one did not, and the only state-specific reason the Union gives is an unsigned memorandum for a different scheme

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — contested on a missing-evidence ground.*

`contested` · T2 · education/federalism/governance · episode · 2021-04-01–2026-03-31 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-04 · contested ground: evidence-withheld

**History.** Created 2026-08-04. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** The FY2025-26 Tamil Nadu release is a composite of two years' money on the Union's own footnote and must not be read as that year's release. This record generalises the finding held at L-0101 in the live instrument from one scheme to a by-state table across three states and five years; the two must be read together and the generalisation is proposed, not applied, because this drop does not edit the live data.

**What this is.** Answering the Lok Sabha on 30 March 2026, the Department of School Education and Literacy published central allocation against central share released under Samagra Shiksha, by state, for five years. West Bengal was paid 97.8 per cent of allocation in FY2021-22 and 85.8 per cent in FY2022-23, then 17.6 per cent in FY2023-24, then nil in FY2024-25 and nil again in FY2025-26 — 3,744.19 crore allocated across the two nil years and nothing released. Tamil Nadu was also nil in FY2024-25. Bihar, on the same table in the same years, was paid between 70.8 and 93.8 per cent throughout.

**What can be established since.** The asymmetry is the finding and it is in the Union's own footnote. Tamil Nadu's FY2025-26 release of 626.20 crore is marked as including 362.81 crore "for FY 2024-25" — so Tamil Nadu's zero was part-paid in arrears, at 16.9 per cent of the allocation it had lost. No such note appears against West Bengal, which has received nothing for either year. A delay and a stoppage look identical in a single-year view and are distinguished here only because the table runs five years and carries the asterisk. The zeros are also not a national shortfall: the all-India total released stayed between 29,996.83 and 34,458.21 crore across all five years, and Bihar was paid 93.8 per cent in the same year West Bengal received nil. Asked directly whether funds had been withheld from states not implementing PM SHRI, the reply does not say yes and does not say no, and then supplies the linkage itself: states "are required to sign a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU)... for implementation of PM SHRI scheme. However, West Bengal has not signed the PM SHRI MoU", followed by "repeated reminders at the level of Secretary and Union Minister". The two schemes are legally distinct. The answer offers no other state-specific reason for any state, and closes the distinction outright: "implementation of Samagra Shiksha Scheme is implementation of NEP 2020 and PM SHRI Schools are exemplar schools of NEP 2020." The general conditions it also cites are the Ministry of Finance ones — pace of expenditure, receipt of commensurate state share, audited accounts, outstanding advances, expenditure statements.

**Strongest case for.** Samagra Shiksha is a shared scheme with published release conditions set by the Ministry of Finance, and they are the same conditions for every state: pace of expenditure, commensurate state share, audited accounts, statements of outstanding advances. Bihar met them and was paid; Tamil Nadu's shortfall was made good in arrears, which shows the process is a payment cycle rather than a bar. Education is on the Concurrent List, the answer says so at the outset, and a Union that funds a scheme may condition its money on the scheme being implemented as designed. The Government also states that it followed up the outstanding memorandum with requests and reminders at Secretary and Ministerial level and received no response, which is a description of a stalled administrative process rather than a sanction.

**Strongest case against.** Asked for the reasons for not releasing money under one scheme, the Government answered with the non-signature of a memorandum under another, and named nothing else specific to the state. The two schemes are separate in law and separately funded, and the answer's own closing sentence collapses them. The allocation continued through both nil years and rose 14.5 per cent in the second, so the Union did not treat West Bengal as ineligible; it allocated and did not release. And the arrears asterisk is the control: the same Government, in the same table, records a payment made late to one state and records nothing for the other, so the difference between the two zeros is on the face of the document. What is at stake is not a scheme's budget but a statutory entitlement to schooling in a state of ninety million people, and the answer supplies no test by which the release could be regained.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** Both sides read the same annexure and the same reply and dispute no figure and no quotation in either. The disagreement is whether an unsigned memorandum for a second scheme, offered as the only state-specific fact in a reply about the first, is an explanation or an implication — a weighting of one agreed record.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Contested: the record supports a reading in which release conditions were unmet and a reading in which participation in a separate scheme was made a condition of funding an existing one, and the answer that would choose between them declines to. The Union does not say the funds were stopped for the MoU; it says the MoU is unsigned, in reply to a question about why the funds were not released, and gives nothing else state-specific.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Which release condition West Bengal failed under Samagra Shiksha, in which year — Asked in terms for "the reasons for not releasing the said funds to the State of West Bengal", the Government listed the general Ministry of Finance conditions and then stated one state-specific fact, the unsigned PM SHRI memorandum. No condition is applied to the state in the answer, and no other instrument publishes a condition-by-state finding. **What would close it:** A Project Approval Board minute or Department of School Education and Literacy communication stating the condition unmet and the year, or a parliamentary answer doing so.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Whether the arrears paid to Tamil Nadu were paid to any other state that recorded a nil year — The Union prints one asterisk against one state and states no policy. Whether the arrears reflect a rule applied to all states, a case-by-case decision, or a single instance cannot be told from a table that carries one footnote. **What would close it:** A statement of arrears releases by state and year under Samagra Shiksha.

**Series this rests on.** wb-samagra-shiksha-allocation, wb-samagra-shiksha-released, tn-samagra-shiksha-allocation, tn-samagra-shiksha-released, bihar-samagra-shiksha-allocation, bihar-samagra-shiksha-released, wb-css-releases, wb-mgnrega-funds-released

**Measurement disputes this record declares.** P-106, P-107, P-114

**Sources** (3, T1×3):

- `T1` Lok Sabha Unstarred Question No. 5783, "Funds under Samagra Shiksha Scheme", answered 30 March 2026, Ministry of Education (Department of School Education and Literacy) — the Annexure of central allocation against central share released by state for FY2021-22 to FY2025-26 with the Tamil Nadu arrears asterisk, the PM SHRI memorandum passage, the general release conditions, and the source note "PAB Minutes / PFMS / PRABANDH Portal, as on 27.03.2026" — https://sansad.in/getFile/lsapps/loksabhaquestions/annex/187/AU5783_XBHvUl.pdf?source=lsapps
- `T1` Lok Sabha Unstarred Question No. 142, answered 1 December 2025, Ministry of Finance (Department of Expenditure) — centrally sponsored scheme releases by state, the wider frame within which the Samagra Shiksha figures sit — https://sansad.in/getFile/lsapps/loksabhaquestions/annex/186/AU142_6FgksB.pdf?source=lsapps
- `T1` Rajya Sabha Starred Question No. *18, answered 21 July 2026 — the Union's statement that Samagra Shiksha is among the schemes "funded through the proceeds of the Health and Education Cess", which ties this record to the cess argument — https://sansad.in/getFile/annex/271/AS18_7lp2Vc.pdf?source=pqars

### B.47 · L-0184 — The fifty per cent wall: a reciprocal tariff and a Russian-oil tranche, built under two orders and one emergency about Ukraine

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — no-objective on a record that states a target.*

`no-objective` · T3 · foreign/macro · lens united-states/russia · shock · 2025-04-02–2026-02-20 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-04

**History.** Created 2026-08-04. Never edited since.

**What this is.** Executive Order 14257 of 2 April 2025 imposed a duty on all imports from all trading partners of at least 10 per cent, with higher rates for dozens of countries. Executive Order 14329 of 6 August 2025 added a further 25 per cent ad valorem on articles of India alone, effective 21 days later — 27 August 2025 — on the finding that "the Government of India is currently directly or indirectly importing Russian Federation oil". The second order was made under the national emergency declared in EO 14066 about Russia's actions in Ukraine, not under any trade emergency, and India is the only country it names. Both were terminated on 20 February 2026.

**What was claimed at announcement.** EO 14329 states its own objective in its own terms: the additional duty on India is "necessary and appropriate" to deal with the national emergency described in EO 14066 — the threat to United States national security and foreign policy posed by Russia's actions against Ukraine — and that imposing it "will more effectively deal with" that emergency.

**What can be established since.** The 25 per cent India tranche took effect 27 August 2025 and stacked on the reciprocal rate, reaching 50 per cent. It ran until Executive Order 14389 of 20 February 2026 provided that the additional ad valorem duties imposed under IEEPA in EO 14257 and EO 14329, among seven other orders, "shall no longer be in effect and, as soon as practicable, shall no longer be collected". The national emergencies themselves were expressly left standing; only the duties went.

**Strongest case for.** The instrument was aimed and it was withdrawn, and both facts belong in the record. The rate applied for under six months, not for a term, and the emergency it was made under is one India is not party to — so a reader treating 50 per cent as a settled condition of the external sector would be reading a peak as a level. The orders also did what tariff orders are supposed to do in a negotiation: a framework for an interim agreement followed on 6 February 2026, before the orders were terminated.

**Strongest case against.** For the period it ran this was the highest rate applied to any United States trading partner, and the mechanism is the part that does not wash out with the rate. The second tranche was not a finding about Indian trade practice: it names India's purchases from a third country, under an emergency declared about that third country, and no other purchaser of Russian crude was named. That makes the exposure political rather than commercial, and it is India-specific in a way no other shock in this instrument is — every other one is shared with the peer panel and can be netted against it. This one confounds every external-sector reading for FY2026 and FY2027 whatever rate finally settles.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Scored no-objective from India's side, which is the side this instrument measures. EO 14329 has an objective and states it, but the objective is a change in Russian conduct toward Ukraine pursued through a duty on Indian goods, so nothing about India's condition is what it set out to move. There is no Indian measure here to have worked or failed. What the record holds is a shock and its dates.

**Re-test when.** Re-test if any duty is reimposed on India on a Russian-oil finding under an authority other than IEEPA. The emergencies under EO 14066 and EO 14024 were expressly preserved by EO 14389, so the finding that India imports Russian oil survives the instrument that rested on it.

**Confounding shocks.** India-specific in severity and in mechanism. Cannot be netted out by peer comparison and must be carried as a standalone confound for FY2026 and FY2027 external-sector assessments.

**Series this rests on.** exports-gdp

**Sources** (3, T1×3):

- `T1` Executive Order 14329 of August 6, 2025, Addressing Threats to the United States by the Government of the Russian Federation, 90 FR 38701, sections 1 and 2 — https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2025-08-11/html/2025-15267.htm
- `T1` Executive Order 14389 of February 20, 2026, Ending Certain Tariff Actions, 91 FR 9437, section 1 — https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-02-25/html/2026-03832.htm
- `T1` Learning Resources, Inc. v. Trump, 607 U.S. ___ (2026), No. 24-1287, syllabus, describing EO 14257's imposition of a duty on all imports from all trading partners of at least 10 per cent — https://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/25pdf/24-1287_4gcj.pdf

### B.48 · L-0187 — India adopts a forced-labour import prohibition and is placed in the lower tier of the tariff that replaced the surcharge

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — no-objective on a record that states a target.*

`no-objective` · T3 · foreign/governance · lens united-states · event · 2026-07-23–2026-07-24 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-04

**History.** Created 2026-08-04. Never edited since.

**What this is.** The United States Trade Representative determined action in section 301 investigations of 60 economies over the failure to impose and effectively enforce a prohibition on importing goods made with forced labour. India was investigated among the 60 and was found, with 53 others, to have failed both limbs. It then adopted a forced-labour import prohibition after the 5 June 2026 proposed action was published, and on that basis was placed in the ten per cent tier rather than the 12.5 per cent tier. The duties took effect at 12:01 a.m. eastern time on 24 July 2026 — the moment the section 122 surcharge expired.

**What was claimed at announcement.** The determination states the criterion for the lower tier in its own terms: an economy that "imposes a forced labor import prohibition, has committed to impose and enforce such a prohibition through an Agreement on Reciprocal Trade (ART), or has imposed a partial regime with the effect of preventing the importation of certain forced labor goods" receives 10 per cent rather than 12.5 per cent. India's entry reads that the determination was made "including India's adoption of a forced labor import prohibition subsequent to the publication of the June 5, 2026 FRN".

**What can be established since.** Sixteen other economies join India in the ten per cent tier. The action carries no statutory expiry, unlike the section 122 surcharge it displaced, and exempts articles already subject to section 232 duties along with a list of raw materials and, after comment, chemical products in their pharmaceutical applications only.

**Strongest case for.** Whatever it was adopted for, the prohibition is the reason India pays 10 rather than 12.5 per cent, and it is a rule against importing goods made with forced labour — a thing worth having on its own terms and one India had not previously had. The mechanism is also more durable than what it replaced: a section 301 action rests on a finding about conduct, so conduct can end it, where the section 122 surcharge could only be waited out.

**Strongest case against.** The instrument that produced the prohibition was a tariff threat from a third country, and the finding against India on both limbs stands unchanged — the tier moved, the determination that India had failed to impose and enforce did not. A prohibition adopted between a proposed action and a final one, and evidenced here only by the foreign determination citing it, is a commitment whose enforcement nobody has yet measured. And the replacement carries no sunset: India exchanged a duty that was going to expire in any case for one that will not.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Two measures meet here and neither is scorable as an Indian objective. The section 301 action is a United States measure with a United States objective. India's adoption of the prohibition is an Indian measure, but the record cannot establish what it was adopted FOR: no Indian announcement of it has been retrieved in this run, and the only document that describes it is the American determination that rewarded it. Scoring an Indian measure on a foreign agency's account of it would be inferring an objective, which is the thing the value exists to refuse.

**Re-test when.** Re-test when the Indian instrument establishing the forced-labour import prohibition is located, or when USTR publishes a review of the section 301 action. Either would convert the unmeasured item above into a series or close it.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** What India's forced-labour import prohibition requires, when it commenced, and what has been done under it — The only document retrieved that establishes the prohibition exists is the United States Trade Representative's determination of 23 July 2026, which cites its adoption without describing its terms. No Indian instrument, notification or announcement of it was retrieved in this run, so the record can state that a foreign agency treated it as adopted and cannot state what it says. **What would close it:** The Indian notification or order establishing the prohibition — most likely a DGFT notification amending the import policy, or a CBIC instruction — and any enforcement figures published under it.

**Sources** (2, T1×2):

- `T1` Notice of Actions in Section 301 Investigations of Acts, Policies, and Practices of Various Economies Related to the Failure of Each Economy To Impose and Effectively Enforce a Prohibition on the Importation of Goods Produced With Forced Labor, 91 FR 47318, USTR-2026-0265 and USTR-2026-0266 — DATES; determination 24, India; the 10 per cent tier list; exemptions — https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-07-28/html/2026-15181.htm
- `T1` Memorandum of July 23, 2026, Actions by the United States in the Investigations Under Section 301 of the Trade Act of 1974 of the Acts, Policies, and Practices of 60 Economies Related to the Failure of Each Economy To Impose and Effectively Enforce a Prohibition on the Importation of Goods Produced With Forced Labor, 91 FR 47717 — India listed at 24 of 60 — https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-07-28/html/2026-15274.htm

### B.49 · L-0188 — The 6 February framework: an eighteen per cent rate promised under an order struck down fourteen days later, and an interim agreement not concluded since

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — too-early on a measure not in force.*

`too-early` · T3 · foreign/macro · lens united-states · reform · 2026-02-06 · confidence medium · last researched 2026-08-04

**History.** Created 2026-08-04. Never edited since.

**What this is.** On 6 February 2026 the United States and India announced a framework for an Interim Agreement. Its central rate term is specific about its instrument: the United States "will apply a reciprocal tariff rate of 18 percent under Executive Order 14257 of April 2, 2025 ... as amended, on originating goods of India". Removal of the reciprocal tariff on generic pharmaceuticals, gems and diamonds and aircraft parts was made subject to "the successful conclusion of the Interim Agreement". EO 14257's duties were terminated on 20 February 2026, fourteen days later.

**What was claimed at announcement.** That the framework would be promptly implemented and the Interim Agreement finalised, en route to a broader Bilateral Trade Agreement launched on 13 February 2025. India's side of the framework is tariff elimination or reduction on all United States industrial goods and a listed range of food and agricultural products, action on named non-tariff barriers, and an intent to "purchase $500 billion of U.S. energy products, aircraft and aircraft parts, precious metals, technology products, and coking coal over the next 5 years".

**What can be established since.** Three commitment states, kept separate because they resolve differently. (a) NOT YET DUE: the $500 billion purchase intent runs five years from February 2026, so its trigger is February 2031 and nothing about it is testable now. The determination on standards acceptability is due within six months of entry into force, which has not begun to run. (b) DUE AND UNDELIVERED: the Interim Agreement itself. Both sides targeted July 2026 and the section 122 surcharge's 24 July expiry was the working deadline; USTR's own 2026 press-release index, retrieved 4 August 2026, records no announcement of a concluded agreement with India, while recording concluded or early-harvest agreements with other partners in the same window. (c) The 18 per cent rate is neither: it was applied, and then its authority was extinguished by a court ruling about a different case. India's rate has since been set by section 122 and then by section 301, at 10 per cent under each.

**Strongest case for.** The framework was announced at 50 per cent and India now pays 10, and every step of that path runs through a negotiation this framework opened. The pharmaceutical, gems and aircraft-parts carve-outs are still live commitments rather than lapsed ones, and the BTA track it reaffirms has been running since February 2025. A framework is by construction a statement of intent, and holding it to a delivery date it did not set would be scoring it against an objective it did not claim.

**Strongest case against.** The central term was drafted against a specific instrument by number, and that instrument was void within a fortnight — which means the promise as written could not be performed, and nothing retrieved shows it renegotiated onto a surviving authority. The valuable terms are all conditional on a conclusion that has not happened: pharmaceuticals, gems and aircraft parts turn on "successful conclusion", and the generic-pharmaceutical outcome is additionally contingent on the findings of a section 232 investigation that is a United States process India does not control. Against that, India's own side of the framework — tariff elimination on all US industrial goods, named non-tariff barriers, a $500 billion purchase intent — is written without conditions.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** Both readings accept the text, the dates and the termination. The disagreement is whether a framework whose named instrument was struck down is a live commitment or a dead letter, and that is a judgement about what a framework is, not a dispute about what happened.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Too-early rather than failed, and the distinction is the obstacle. What is missing is a concluded Interim Agreement, and the evidence that would settle whether the framework produced one is evidence time accumulates — further negotiating rounds, or their absence past a point where absence means something. Not awaiting-adjudication: nothing is pending before any body outside the two governments. Not failed: the framework's own headline term is a rate India is currently beating, so a reader told this failed would draw the wrong inference about the direction of the outcome. What can be said now is that the deliverable is late and the instrument it named no longer exists.

**Re-test when.** Re-test on the earlier of: an announcement that the Interim Agreement has been concluded, or 6 February 2027, one year from the framework. If neither has happened by then the (b) commitment state should be reconsidered against `failed`, since a year of accumulated absence is evidence rather than the lack of it.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Whether the 18 per cent reciprocal rate was ever re-based onto a surviving statutory authority after EO 14257's duties were terminated — The framework names EO 14257 explicitly and nothing retrieved in this run amends, replaces or re-bases that term. The rate India has actually paid since 24 February 2026 has been 10 per cent, first under section 122 and then under section 301, neither of which is the reciprocal tariff the framework describes. Whether the 18 per cent term is regarded by either government as subsisting is not established by any document retrieved. **What would close it:** A joint statement, USTR notice or Federal Register document addressing the status of the framework's rate term after 20 February 2026.

**Sources** (3, T1×3):

- `T1` United States-India Joint Statement, The White House, 6 February 2026 — key terms of the Interim Agreement, including the 18 per cent rate under EO 14257, the conditional removals, and the $500 billion five-year purchase intent — https://www.whitehouse.gov/briefings-statements/2026/02/united-states-india-joint-statement
- `T1` Executive Order 14389 of February 20, 2026, Ending Certain Tariff Actions, 91 FR 9437 — terminating the additional ad valorem duties imposed under IEEPA in EO 14257 — https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-02-25/html/2026-03832.htm
- `T1` Office of the United States Trade Representative, 2026 press releases index, retrieved 4 August 2026 — last India item is the 6 February joint statement; concluded and early-harvest agreements with other partners appear in the same index over the same period — https://ustr.gov/about/policy-offices/press-office/press-releases/2026

### B.50 · L-0205 — The India-EU agreement is concluded but not in force, and this time both sides published the same two measurements — 90.7 per cent of India's export value duty-free at once against 30.6 per cent of the EU's

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — too-early on a measure not in force.*

`too-early` · T3 · foreign/macro · lens europe · reform · 2026-01-27 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-05

**History.** Created 2026-08-05. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** Two coincidences of arithmetic in the published figures are noted so a reader does not take either for a relationship. First, the EU's immediate and phased line shares sum to 90.7, which is also the figure given for the share of India's export VALUE covered by the immediate tranche — the same number attached to two different quantities, a line count and a value share, related by no arithmetic. Second, India's 2024-25 bilateral goods trade is given as US$136.54bn against exports of US$75.85bn and imports of US$60.68bn, which sum to 136.53; the total is computed on unrounded values and the 0.01 is a rounding artefact, not a discrepancy.

**What this is.** The Prime Minister and the President of the European Commission announced the conclusion of India-EU Free Trade Agreement negotiations at the 16th India-EU Summit on 27 January 2026, after a re-launch of talks in 2022. The EU eliminates duties on about 70.4 per cent of its tariff lines at entry into force, covering 90.7 per cent of India's export value, with a further 20.3 per cent of products phased; its total offer is almost 97 per cent of lines and more than 99 per cent of trade value. India gives immediate duty-free access on about 49.6 per cent of its tariff lines covering 30.6 per cent of trade value, with 39.5 per cent of lines and 63.1 per cent of value phased over five, seven or ten years; India's total offer is around 92.1 per cent of lines and 97.5 per cent of value.

**What was claimed at announcement.** A balanced, modern and rules-based partnership delivering, on the Ministry's statement, preferential entry for over 99 per cent of Indian exports and zero duty at entry into force on almost US$33bn of exports in labour-intensive sectors — textiles, leather, marine products, gems and jewellery — that currently face duties of up to 10 per cent. On services, the EU opens 144 subsectors to India and India opens 102 to the EU.

**What can be established since.** COMMITMENT STATE (a) — NOT YET DUE, AND THE TRIGGER IS A NAMED CONDITION RATHER THAN A DATE. The FAQ states the mechanism precisely: the agreement enters into force "on the first day of the second month following the date on which India and the EU exchange written notifications confirming completion of their respective internal legal procedures, or on any other date mutually agreed". So conclusion of negotiations is not signature and is not entry into force, and no date exists for either — but unlike the Rafale-M agreement in L-0201 there IS a stated trigger, and it can be observed when it occurs. Nothing in the schedule runs until it does.

**Strongest case for.** The disclosure is the best in this arc and that deserves saying plainly. Both sides' offers are published on BOTH bases — lines and trade value — and split into immediate and phased, which is exactly what the India-UK release did not do and what makes an asymmetry visible instead of buried. On substance the asymmetry runs India's way at entry into force by 60.1 percentage points of value, the labour-intensive sectors that employ most are the ones getting immediate zero duty on around US$33bn of exports, and India's own opening is deliberately staged over five to ten years, which is the protection its sensitive sectors were promised. Concluding with the EU after a decade of stalled attempts is a substantive outcome whatever the schedule.

**Strongest case against.** Concluded is not signed and signed is not in force, and the difference is not procedural detail — nothing in the schedule binds until notifications are exchanged, and no date exists for that. The headline that circulates is "over 99 per cent of Indian exports", which is the eventual figure; the day-one figure for India's exports is 90.7 per cent of value and for the EU's is 30.6 per cent, and only the second tells a reader what changes when the agreement starts. India's phasing runs to ten years, which is longer than most governments last. And the published components do not add up to the published totals on any of the three splits, so the schedules cannot be reconstructed from what has been released.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** No competing accounts: every figure is from Indian government publications and none is disputed in anything retrieved. The asymmetry between the two offers is a property of the agreement, not a disagreement about it.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Too-early: negotiations are concluded, the obstacle is elapsed time and the procedural steps time carries. Not awaiting-adjudication — the exchange of notifications is an act of the parties themselves, not a decision by any body outside them.

**Re-test when.** Re-test on signature, and on the exchange of written notifications — the second starts the clock the FAQ describes and is what moves this out of state (a). If the tariff schedule annexes are published, the undescribed residual above becomes measurable at the same time.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The residual between the published immediate and phased shares and the published totals, on all three splits — The components do not sum to the stated totals and no third category is described. EU lines: 70.4 immediate plus 20.3 phased is 90.7, against a stated total of almost 97 — a gap of about 6.3 points. India lines: 49.6 plus 39.5 is 89.1 against 92.1 — a gap of 3.0. India value: 30.6 plus 63.1 is 93.7 against 97.5 — a gap of 3.8. Something is conceded that is neither immediate elimination nor phased elimination — tariff-rate quotas and partial reductions are the ordinary candidates — and nothing retrieved names it. The gaps are recorded rather than reconciled. **What would close it:** The agreement's tariff schedule annexes, or a Ministry statement of the concession categories and their shares.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The date of signature and the date of exchange of notifications — Conclusion of negotiations was announced on 27 January 2026. The entry-into-force rule is stated as a condition, not a date, and neither a signature date nor a notification-exchange date appears in anything retrieved. **What would close it:** A Ministry announcement of signature, or of the exchange of written notifications, either of which starts the clock the FAQ describes.

**Sources** (2, T1×2):

- `T1` Ministry of Commerce and Industry, "India-EU Free Trade Agreement Concluded: A Strategic Breakthrough in India's Global Trade Engagement", PIB, 27 January 2026 — the conclusion announcement, the 2022 re-launch, over 99 per cent of Indian exports gaining preferential entry, US$33bn of labour-intensive exports to zero duty at entry into force, 144 EU subsectors against 102 Indian, the EU as India's 22nd FTA partner, and the 2024-25 bilateral trade figures — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2219065
- `T1` Ministry of Commerce and Industry, "Frequently Asked Questions: India and European Union Free Trade Agreement", PIB — the EU offer (70.4 per cent of lines immediate covering 90.7 per cent of India's export value, 20.3 per cent phased, almost 97 per cent of lines and over 99 per cent of value in total), India's offer (49.6 per cent of lines immediate covering 30.6 per cent of value, 39.5 per cent of lines and 63.1 per cent of value phased over 5/7/10 years, 92.1 per cent of lines and 97.5 per cent of value in total), and the entry-into-force rule at question 58 — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2220413

### B.51 · L-0206 — The Maldives currency swap is an eligibility with a size and an expiry, and nothing published says how much of it was drawn

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — no-objective on a record that states a target.*

`no-objective` · T3 · foreign/macro · lens neighbourhood · institutional · 2024-10-07 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-05

**History.** Created 2026-08-05. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** The two windows are stated in different currencies — US$400 million and INR 30 billion — and this record does not convert between them or add them. The RBI's announcement gives no exchange rate and no combined figure, and the SAARC framework's own design operates windows within an overall facility size that this announcement does not state.

**What this is.** The Reserve Bank of India entered a Currency Swap Agreement with the Maldives Monetary Authority on 7 October 2024 in New Delhi, under the SAARC Currency Swap Framework 2024-27. The MMA is eligible for US$400 million under the US Dollar/Euro Swap Window and INR 30 billion under the INR Swap Window. The agreement is valid until 18 June 2027. The framework itself has operated since 15 November 2012 and is described by the RBI as a backstop for short-term foreign exchange liquidity or balance-of-payments stress "till longer term arrangements are made".

**What was claimed at announcement.** The framework's stated purpose, in the RBI's own words: a backstop line of funding for short-term foreign exchange liquidity requirements or short-term balance of payments stress, pending longer-term arrangements. No objective is stated for the Maldives agreement specifically beyond membership of that framework.

**What can be established since.** WHAT IS PUBLISHED IS AN ELIGIBILITY, NOT A DRAWING. The agreement establishes what the MMA may access; nothing retrieved states how much, if any, was drawn, when, or whether it has been repaid. Those are different quantities and the record does not treat the first as evidence of the second. The agreement runs to 18 June 2027 — a date, not a condition — so it is testable on its own terms when it expires.

**Strongest case for.** This is the instrument doing what it was built for. The framework has existed since 2012 and is available to SAARC members on standing terms rather than being improvised per crisis, which is what makes it a backstop rather than a rescue. Two windows in different currencies give the counterparty a genuine choice, and the INR window is the concrete form of rupee internationalisation rather than a slogan about it. The terms are published in full — date, both window sizes, expiry — which is more than most bilateral financial arrangements disclose.

**Strongest case against.** The published figure is a ceiling and is routinely read as a transfer. US$400 million is what the MMA MAY draw, and whether any of it moved is not published by either party; a reader meeting the number in isolation would reasonably take it for support extended rather than support available. The framework's own language concedes that it is provisional — funding "till longer term arrangements are made" — so the agreement describes a bridge without stating what it bridges to. And the expiry of 18 June 2027 is common to the framework rather than negotiated for the Maldives, so it carries no information about the specific relationship.

**Note on the verdict itself.** A facility, not a measure with an announced outcome. The framework states a purpose but the bilateral agreement announces no target against which it could have worked or failed, and a swap line that is never drawn is not thereby a failure — an unused backstop may be doing exactly what a backstop does. Scoring it would require an objective neither party stated.

**Re-test when.** Re-test on 18 June 2027 when the agreement expires, and earlier if any drawing is published. Expiry without a published drawing would leave the facility's use unmeasured for its whole life, which is itself the finding.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** How much of the swap was drawn by the Maldives Monetary Authority, when, and whether it has been repaid — The RBI publishes the agreement and its ceilings, not drawings under it. Nothing retrieved from either the RBI or the MMA states utilisation. Eligibility and utilisation are different quantities, and the published number is the first. **What would close it:** RBI's Weekly Statistical Supplement or Annual Report if either discloses SAARC swap drawings, or a Maldives Monetary Authority statement of its own external liabilities.

**Sources** (1, T1×1):

- `T1` Reserve Bank of India, "RBI enters into Currency Swap Agreement with Maldives Monetary Authority", Press Release 2024-2025/1241, 7 October 2024 — the agreement date, both window sizes, the 18 June 2027 expiry, and the SAARC framework's operation since 15 November 2012 with its stated purpose — https://www.rbi.org.in/Scripts/BS_PressReleaseDisplay.aspx?prid=58839

### B.52 · L-0207 — Punatsangchhu-II exported power to India for nearly seven months before the tariff protocol was signed, and no tariff figure has been published for either period

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — worked on one or two government citations.*

`worked` · T3 · foreign/infrastructure · lens neighbourhood · reform · 2026-04-09 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-05

**History.** Created 2026-08-05. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** The 1020 MW figure is the project's installed capacity as stated by the Ministry, not a measure of energy exported, and this record derives no export quantity from it. The interval between 19 September 2025 and 9 April 2026 is stated as six months and three weeks and is an elapsed period between two published dates; it is not a claim about how long any tariff negotiation took, which is not published.

**What this is.** The 1020 MW Punatsangchhu-II Hydroelectric Project in Bhutan commenced export of surplus power to India on 19 September 2025 "at a mutually agreed starting tariff", and was jointly inaugurated by the Prime Minister of India and the King of Bhutan on 11 November 2025. The Tariff Protocol for the project was signed on 9 April 2026, during the Union Power Minister's visit to Bhutan, alongside a Methodology for Reactive Energy Accounting of reactive power exchange. Indo-Bhutan hydropower cooperation is dated by the Ministry to 1961, with an agreement on hydroelectric power cooperation in 2006.

**What was claimed at announcement.** The Ministry's framing at the signing: another step in mutually beneficial hydropower cooperation, within an enhanced bilateral institutional framework mechanism covering non-hydro energy, cross-border transmission, project financing, capacity building and institutional partnerships. No tariff, revenue or export-volume target is stated for the project in anything retrieved.

**What can be established since.** THE POWER MOVED BEFORE THE PRICE WAS SETTLED, AND THE PRICE IS NOT PUBLISHED IN EITHER PERIOD. Export began on 19 September 2025 at what the Ministry calls "a mutually agreed starting tariff" — a phrase that concedes an interim arrangement — and the Tariff Protocol was signed on 9 April 2026, six months and three weeks later. In between, on 3 February 2026, the Bhutanese Energy Minister and the Union Power Minister were still deliberating "the commercial optimization of power output" from the project, which places the commercial terms unsettled more than four months after power began flowing. Neither the starting tariff nor the protocol tariff appears in anything retrieved.

**Strongest case for.** The plant exists and is exporting, which is more than most of the pipeline can say — the companion project at Punatsangchhu-I was still awaiting commissioning in February 2026 after far longer. Beginning export on an interim tariff rather than waiting for the protocol is the choice that gets power onto the grid earliest, and Bhutan is paid from the first unit rather than from the signature. The 9 April 2026 signings are substantive rather than ceremonial: a tariff protocol and a reactive-energy accounting methodology are the two instruments a cross-border interconnection actually needs to settle accounts and hold the grid stable. The institutional framework agreed at the same time gives the relationship a standing review mechanism instead of ministerial visits.

**Strongest case against.** The sequence inverts the ordinary protection. Power flowed for six months and three weeks on an interim price, which means the smaller party's revenue for that period was determined by an arrangement neither government has published and neither parliament has seen. "Commercial optimization" was still under deliberation in February 2026, so the terms were not merely unpublished but unfinished while the asset ran. And the protocol's signature does not close the disclosure: no tariff figure exists in the public record for the interim period or the settled one, so nothing about the price — whether it rose, fell or was applied retrospectively — can be checked by anyone outside the two ministries.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Scored on delivery and on delivery only: the project was built, commissioned and is exporting, which is what a joint hydropower project is for, and the two governments inaugurated it jointly. The commercial terms are NOT scored here and cannot be — no tariff is published for either period and no revenue or volume target was announced against which the arrangement could be judged. A reader should take this score as saying the electricity flows, not that the deal is good.

**Re-test when.** Re-test if any tariff figure is published for either period, or if cross-border exchange volumes for the Bhutan interconnections are released. Either would make the value of the interim arrangement measurable for the first time; without them it stays unmeasurable however long the plant runs.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The interim tariff applied from 19 September 2025, and the tariff set by the Protocol signed 9 April 2026 — The Ministry states that export began "at a mutually agreed starting tariff" and that a Tariff Protocol was later signed, without giving either figure. The numbers exist — an interim tariff that power was sold at is a number both parties hold, and a signed protocol contains its rate — so this is not-published rather than not-collected. No identifiable refusal was located, which is what keeps it short of withheld. **What would close it:** The text of the Tariff Protocol, a Central Electricity Regulatory Commission filing on the import, or a Parliamentary answer stating the rate.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Whether the Protocol's tariff was applied retrospectively to the interim period, and how much energy was exported before it was signed — Nothing retrieved states the Protocol's effective date or whether it reopens the interim sales, and no export volume is published for the period from 19 September 2025. Without both, the value transferred under the interim arrangement cannot be bounded even approximately. **What would close it:** The Protocol's commencement clause, or Ministry of Power / Central Electricity Authority monthly cross-border exchange data for the Bhutan interconnections.

**Sources** (2, T1×2):

- `T1` Ministry of Power, "Union Minister Shri Manohar Lal Arrives in Bhutan; Reaffirms India-Bhutan Partnership on Clean Energy and Sustainable Development", PIB, 9 April 2026 — the Tariff Protocol and Reactive Energy Accounting Methodology signings, the 1020 MW capacity, the 11 November 2025 joint inauguration, the 19 September 2025 commencement of export "at a mutually agreed starting tariff", and the enhanced bilateral institutional framework mechanism — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2250426
- `T1` Ministry of Power, "India and Bhutan to Strengthen Cooperation in the Power Sector", PIB, 3 February 2026 — the deliberation on "commercial optimization of power output" from Punatsangchhu-II, the call for early commissioning of Punatsangchhu-I (1200 MW), cooperation dated to 1961 with the 2006 hydroelectric power agreement, and the assessment of cross-border transmission links till 2040 — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2222533

### B.53 · L-0208 — The Ministry of Power's account of neighbourhood cooperation lists capacity without commissioning status and agreements without dates, and two rows of the same column are demonstrably in different states

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — no-objective on a record that states a target.*

`no-objective` · T3 · foreign/infrastructure · lens neighbourhood · institutional · 2026-04-02 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-05

**History.** Created 2026-08-05. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** This record does not sum the installed-capacity column and no total is stated, because its entries are not in the same state and the shared megawatt unit does not make them commensurable. The counts given here — thirteen generation projects and thirty transmission links, distributed fifteen, nine, three, two and one — are counts of rows as printed, not of the source's serial numbers, which contain a defect: the Nepal transmission list skips serial 13 and gives serial 14 twice, and the two errors cancel so that the final serial reaches 30 correctly. The count is stated from the rows for that reason.

**What this is.** A Lok Sabha written reply of 2 April 2026 set out India's power-sector cooperation with Bhutan, Nepal, Bangladesh, Myanmar and Sri Lanka in two tables. Table-1 lists Memoranda of Understanding and agreements by the parties to them. Table-2 lists thirteen generation projects with an installed-capacity column — six in Nepal, six in Bhutan, one in Bangladesh — and thirty cross-border transmission links: fifteen with Nepal, nine with Bhutan, three with Bangladesh, two with Myanmar and one with Sri Lanka.

**What was claimed at announcement.** No objective is announced. The reply is a description of engagement — that the Ministry and its public sector enterprises "engage with neighbouring countries ... to strengthen cooperation in the power sector" — and states no target, date or outcome for any project or agreement listed.

**What can be established since.** WHAT THE TABLES OMIT IS THE COLUMN THAT WOULD MAKE THEM READABLE. Table-2 gives installed capacity in megawatts for every generation project under one heading, with no column for commissioning status, and the same for thirty transmission links. That the omission is load-bearing rather than pedantic can be shown from the Ministry's own releases in the same period: Punatsangchhu-II (1020 MW) commenced export to India on 19 September 2025, while Punatsangchhu-I (1200 MW) was the subject of a call for "early commissioning" on 3 February 2026. One row is exporting and one is not built, and they sit adjacent in the same column under the same unit. Table-1 has the parallel defect on the agreements side: it names the parties to each instrument and nothing else — no title, no date, no term, no status — so it establishes that agreements exist without disclosing what any of them says.

**Strongest case for.** The reply discloses more than it was obliged to and more than the equivalent answers in most sectors. Thirty named transmission links with voltages, circuit configurations and both endpoints is a genuinely specific inventory, and naming every counterparty enterprise — NHPC, NTPC, NVVN, SJVN, Power Grid — lets a reader trace any of them further. The tripartite Nepal-India-Bangladesh arrangement among NEA, NVVN and the Bangladesh Power Development Board is disclosed at all, which is not obvious for a three-country transaction. And a written answer is a schedule, not a report; the question asked what exists, and the tables answer it.

**Strongest case against.** A capacity column is read as capacity, and a reader who adds this one gets a number that does not exist. Built plant and unbuilt plant are set out identically in megawatts, so the shared unit conceals the difference rather than exposing it — the reader must already know which rows are real to use the table, which is the knowledge the table was supposed to supply. The same is true of the transmission list, where a 1000 MW India-Sri Lanka HVDC bipole appears beside links that have carried power for decades. Table-1 is weaker still: a list of agreements identified only by their signatories cannot be checked, dated, or matched to any project in Table-2, and it discloses the existence of obligations while withholding every one of their terms.

**Note on the verdict itself.** No-objective in its strict sense: the reply announces no target, date or outcome for any project or agreement it lists, so there is nothing against which it could have worked or failed. What it establishes is what can and cannot be measured from the Ministry's own account. Recorded so that the capacity column is not later read as a stock of operating plant, which is the reading its presentation invites and the one it does not support.

**Re-test when.** Re-test if a later Parliamentary answer on the same subject carries a commissioning-status column or dates for the Table-1 agreements. The India-Sri Lanka HVDC bipole is the single most informative row to watch, because it is the one link in the list for which no operating history exists at all.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The commissioning status and commissioning date of each of the thirteen generation projects and thirty transmission links — No status column appears in either table, and the reply describes them all under the single heading of details of projects. The Ministry necessarily holds commissioning dates for assets its own enterprises built, so the information exists and was not released in this answer. **What would close it:** A Ministry of Power or Central Electricity Authority statement of commissioning dates by project, or a subsequent Parliamentary answer carrying a status column.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The date, title, term and current status of each agreement in Table-1 — Table-1 identifies every instrument by its parties alone. Whether any is still in force, when it was executed, and what it obliges either side to do are not stated. The instruments exist and are held by the executing bodies, so this is not-published; nothing retrieved records a request for them being refused, which is what keeps it short of withheld. **What would close it:** The texts of the MoUs, or a Parliamentary answer listing them with dates and terms.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Energy actually exchanged over any of the thirty links — The tables list infrastructure, not flows. Capacity and connection are what is published; how much electricity moved in either direction, over what period, is not stated anywhere in the reply. **What would close it:** Central Electricity Authority cross-border exchange data, or a Parliamentary answer on import and export volumes by link.

**Sources** (2, T1×2):

- `T1` Ministry of Power, "COOPERATION WITH NEIGHBOURING COUNTRIES IN POWER SECTOR", PIB, 2 April 2026 — written reply of the Minister of State Shri Shripad Naik in the Lok Sabha: Table-1's list of MoUs by parties, and Table-2's generation projects with installed capacity (Nepal: Pokhra 1, Trisuli 21, Western Gandak 15, Devighat 14.1, Arun-3 900, Lower Arun 669; Bhutan: Chukha 336, Kurichu 60, Tala 1020, Mangdechhu 720, Punatsangchhu-II 1020, Punatsangchhu-I 1200; Bangladesh: Maitree STPP 1320) and the thirty cross-border transmission links including New Madurai-Mannar 1000 MW VSC HVDC bipole with Sri Lanka — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2248339
- `T1` Ministry of Power, "India and Bhutan to Strengthen Cooperation in the Power Sector", PIB, 3 February 2026 — the call for early commissioning of Punatsangchhu-I, which with the 19 September 2025 export commencement of Punatsangchhu-II establishes that two rows of the same capacity column are in different states — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2222533

### B.54 · L-0209 — The decision to fence the entire 1,643 km Myanmar border was announced with a starting position of 10 km and no completion date, and the Ministry's own year-end review for that year gives no progress figure at all

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — no-objective on a record that states a target.*

`no-objective` · T3 · foreign/infrastructure · lens neighbourhood · reform · 2024-02-06 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-05

**History.** Created 2026-08-05. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** No share is computed here. The announcement's four quantities are in four different states — 10 km fenced, 2 km under execution as pilots, approximately 20 km approved, 1,643 km intended — and only the first is completed work, so adding any of them or dividing them into the total would produce a figure that describes nothing. The record therefore states the 10 km and the 1,643 km side by side and derives no percentage from them. The 23rd India-Myanmar National Level Meeting readout of 8 July 2026 does not mention fencing; that is recorded as a fact about the readout and is not evidence of abandonment, since absence of news is not evidence that a commitment was dropped.

**What this is.** On 6 February 2024 the Union Home Minister announced, in a post on X reproduced as a PIB release, that the government had decided to construct a fence along the entire 1,643 kilometre Indo-Myanmar border, with a paved patrol track alongside. The same announcement gave the starting position: 10 km fenced in Moreh, Manipur; two Hybrid Surveillance System pilot projects under execution covering 1 km each in Arunachal Pradesh and Manipur; and fence works of approximately 20 km in Manipur approved, with work to start soon. No completion date was stated.

**What was claimed at announcement.** "The Modi government is committed to building impenetrable borders. It has decided to construct a fence along the entire 1643-kilometer-long Indo-Myanmar border. To facilitate better surveillance, a patrol track along the border will also be paved." The commitment is to a total length. No date, no phasing, no annual target and no cost accompany it.

**What can be established since.** A TOTAL WITHOUT A DATE CANNOT BECOME DUE. The commitment names a quantity — the entire 1,643 km — and no time by which it is to exist, so it falls outside all three commitment states this instrument uses: it is not (a) not-yet-due with a named trigger, because no trigger is named; it cannot reach (b) due-and-undelivered, because it can never fall due; and it cannot reach (c) abandoned without evidence of abandonment, which nothing supplies. WHAT CAN BE CHECKED IS WHETHER PROGRESS IS REPORTED, AND IT IS NOT. The Ministry of Home Affairs' own Year End Review 2024 — its comprehensive account of the year in which the decision was taken — contains the figure 1,643 nowhere, uses the words fence and fenced nowhere, and uses fencing once: in a recommendation that demographic data along the border "should be mapped to help in fencing the border and stopping infiltration". That is a proposal, not a progress report.

**Strongest case for.** The decision itself is specific where it matters most: a named total length, a named border, and a stated intention to pave a patrol track, which is the element that makes a fence patrollable rather than ornamental. Announcing the starting position in the same breath — 10 km already fenced, two pilots under execution, 20 km approved — is more candid than the announcement required, and it means the base against which any later figure would be read was published on day one rather than reconstructed afterwards. Fencing a 1,643 km border across Arunachal Pradesh, Nagaland, Manipur and Mizoram is genuinely hard terrain, and refusing to name a date may be honesty about that rather than evasion.

**Strongest case against.** The announcement's structure makes it permanently unfalsifiable, and that is a choice, not an accident. Every element that would let a reader test it later — a completion year, an annual target, a cost, a phasing plan — is absent, while the element that makes it quotable, the 1,643 km total, is present. The Ministry then published a year-end review of the very year the decision was taken which does not carry the total, does not carry a progress figure, and mentions fencing only in a recommendation about demographic mapping. A commitment that names no date and is not reported against in the responsible ministry's own annual account is one no reader can hold anyone to, however long it stands.

**Note on the verdict itself.** No-objective in the sense this instrument uses it: an objective is a target that can be failed. A total length with no date can never be due and therefore can never be undelivered, so it names a destination rather than a commitment. This is not a judgement that the fence is unbuilt or that the decision was insincere — it is that the announcement as made cannot be scored against itself at any future moment, and no later Ministry publication retrieved here supplies the date or the progress that would change that.

**Re-test when.** Re-test if any kilometres-fenced figure is published, or if a target year is named — the second is what would move this out of no-objective and make the commitment scoreable for the first time.

**Not measured — stated reason `never-defined`.** The date by which the 1,643 km is to be fenced — Neither the announcement nor the Ministry's Year End Review 2024 states a completion date, a phasing schedule or an annual target. This is not a figure held and unreleased: a date that was never set does not exist to be published, which is what distinguishes it from the progress figure below. **What would close it:** A Ministry of Home Affairs statement of a target year or phasing plan, or a Parliamentary answer giving one.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** How much of the 1,643 km has been fenced since 6 February 2024 — The Ministry of Home Affairs and its executing agencies necessarily hold current progress for works they commission, so the figure exists. It is absent from the place it would naturally appear: the Ministry's Year End Review 2024, checked in full, carries no fencing length, no reference to the 1,643 km total, and no progress statement of any kind. Absence from the responsible ministry's own annual account is what makes this not-published rather than merely not-retrieved. **What would close it:** A Parliamentary answer stating kilometres fenced to date, or a Ministry of Home Affairs Annual Report figure for the Indo-Myanmar border.

**Sources** (3, T1×3):

- `T1` Ministry of Home Affairs, "Union Home Minister and Minister of Cooperation, Shri Amit Shah says Modi government is committed to building impenetrable borders", PIB, 6 February 2024 — the decision to fence the entire 1,643 km Indo-Myanmar border, the paved patrol track, and the starting position of 10 km fenced at Moreh, two 1 km Hybrid Surveillance System pilots under execution, and approximately 20 km approved in Manipur — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2003199
- `T1` Ministry of Home Affairs, "Ministry of Home Affairs: Year End Review 2024", PIB — checked in full for fencing progress: the total 1,643 does not appear, the words fence and fenced do not appear, and fencing appears only in the recommendation that demographic data along the India-Myanmar border be mapped "to help in fencing the border and stopping infiltration" — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2088945
- `T1` Ministry of Home Affairs, "23rd National Level Meeting between India and Myanmar concludes in New Delhi", PIB, 8 July 2026 — the most recent bilateral home-ministry readout retrieved, covering border security, transnational crime, the Kaladan project and the trilateral highway, and not mentioning fencing — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2282625

### B.55 · L-0210 — The Ministry that announced the Free Movement Regime would be scrapped does not own the instrument, and its own text says it recommended a suspension while another ministry was still in the process

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — no-objective on a record that states a target.*

`no-objective` · T3 · foreign/governance · lens neighbourhood · reform · 2024-02-08 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-05

**History.** Created 2026-08-05. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** This record makes no claim about whether the Free Movement Regime is in force today. The retrieval limit is the reason and is stated rather than worked around: the ministry that owns the instrument publishes through a route that cannot be read in this environment, so silence on that side is uninformative here and is not treated as evidence. What is established is confined to what the Ministry of Home Affairs published and to what its own year-end review does and does not contain. The 23rd India-Myanmar National Level Meeting readout of 8 July 2026 does not mention the Free Movement Regime; as with the fencing record, that is a fact about the readout and not evidence that the commitment was dropped.

**What this is.** On 8 February 2024 the Ministry of Home Affairs announced that the Free Movement Regime between India and Myanmar — which permitted local residents on either side to cross the border without a visa — would be scrapped, to ensure internal security and to maintain the demographic structure of India's North Eastern States bordering Myanmar. The release reproduces a post on X by the Union Home Minister, whose text states that the Ministry of External Affairs "is currently in the process of scrapping it" and that MHA "has recommended the immediate suspension of the FMR".

**What was claimed at announcement.** That the FMR between India and Myanmar be scrapped, on two stated grounds: the internal security of the country, and the maintenance of the demographic structure of India's North Eastern States bordering Myanmar. No date of effect, no notification number, no replacement arrangement and no test of completion accompany it.

**What can be established since.** THREE DIFFERENT ACTS ARE COMPRESSED INTO ONE ANNOUNCEMENT, AND THE HEADLINE REPORTS ONLY THE STRONGEST. The quoted text contains a decision by MHA that the regime "be scrapped", a statement that MEA "is currently in the process of scrapping it", and a recommendation by MHA of "the immediate suspension". The instrument belongs to the Ministry of External Affairs; what the Ministry of Home Affairs did on its own authority was decide a position and recommend. The headline — "MHA decides to scrap" — reports the act of the ministry that cannot perform it. WHETHER IT WAS PERFORMED IS NOT RECORDED WHERE IT WOULD BE. The Ministry's Year End Review 2024 restates this same announcement in two separate sections, word for word, more than ten months later, and adds nothing: no notification, no date of effect, no replacement regime, no statement that the scrapping was completed.

**Strongest case for.** The grounds were stated plainly rather than left to be inferred, and both are legitimate subjects of the Ministry's responsibility: internal security, and the demographic composition of border districts during a period of acute instability across the frontier. Announcing that MEA was already in process is a disclosure of the actual division of authority rather than a concealment of it — the release could simply have said the regime was scrapped and did not. Recommending immediate suspension while the formal instrument was unwound is the mechanism that closes the gap between decision and notification, and for a border regime during an insurgency that gap is the whole risk.

**Strongest case against.** The distinction the text preserves is the one the headline destroys, and it is the headline that circulates. A recommendation by one ministry to another is a lesser thing than a decision, and presenting it as "MHA decides to scrap" attributes to the announcing body an authority the same paragraph concedes it does not hold. The evidentiary basis is a social-media post: no notification, no order number, no date of effect appears in the release or anywhere retrieved. And the Ministry's own year-end review, published more than ten months later, reproduces the announcement twice without adding a single fact about its execution — so the most recent MHA account of the matter is the announcement itself, restated.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Scored on what the announcing ministry actually committed itself to, which is a recommendation and a stated position — neither of which carries a completion test, a date or an instrument. MHA set no objective against which MHA could be measured. This is expressly NOT a finding that the FMR survives or that it was scrapped; that question is unmeasured below, and the record names both where it would be recorded and why this run cannot settle it.

**Re-test when.** Re-test when the Ministry of External Affairs route becomes retrievable, or if any Parliamentary answer states the Free Movement Regime's current status. Either would settle the question this record deliberately leaves open.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Whether the Free Movement Regime was in fact scrapped or suspended, by what instrument, and with effect from what date — The instrument belongs to the Ministry of External Affairs, and the announcement states only that MEA was "in the process". Nothing retrieved from the Ministry of Home Affairs records completion: its Year End Review 2024 restates the February announcement in two sections and adds nothing. MEA's own publications could not be retrieved in this environment at all — its press-release path returns an HTTP 200 serving a JavaScript shell, confirmed on two separate paths this cycle — so this record CANNOT distinguish between MEA having published nothing and MEA having published something unreachable here. The classification records the absence from MHA's account, which was checked; it does not assert an absence from MEA's, which was not. **What would close it:** The Ministry of External Affairs notification or gazette instrument, or a Parliamentary answer stating the FMR's current status and date of effect.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** What arrangement, if any, replaced the Free Movement Regime for border residents — The announcement states what is to be removed and nothing about what governs local crossing afterwards. No replacement scheme, pass system or transitional arrangement appears in anything retrieved, including the Year End Review 2024. **What would close it:** A Ministry of Home Affairs or Ministry of External Affairs statement of the arrangement now governing local border crossing, or the standard operating procedure issued to the Assam Rifles.

**Sources** (3, T1×3):

- `T1` Ministry of Home Affairs, "MHA decides to scrap Free Movement Regime (FMR) between India and Myanmar to ensure the internal security of the country and maintain the demographic structure of India's North Eastern States bordering Myanmar", PIB, 8 February 2024 — the decision, the two stated grounds, the statement that the Ministry of External Affairs "is currently in the process of scrapping it", and MHA's recommendation of "the immediate suspension of the FMR" — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2003884
- `T1` Ministry of Home Affairs, "Ministry of Home Affairs: Year End Review 2024", PIB — restates the 8 February 2024 announcement verbatim in two separate sections, the Northeast section and "Secured Border to Secure Bharat", with no notification, date of effect, replacement arrangement or statement of completion — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2088945
- `T1` Ministry of Home Affairs, "23rd National Level Meeting between India and Myanmar concludes in New Delhi", PIB, 8 July 2026 — the most recent bilateral home-ministry readout retrieved, not mentioning the Free Movement Regime — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2282625
