### B.56 · L-0211 — India's electricity exports to Bangladesh were published as a measured four-year series up to January 2023 and have not been published since, and the Ministry of Power's own account of 2025 does not mention Bangladesh at all

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — no-objective on a record that states a target.*

`no-objective` · T3 · foreign/infrastructure · lens neighbourhood · institutional · 2026-01-16 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-05

**History.** Created 2026-08-05. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** The 2022-23 figure of 7,234.08 Million Units covers ten months, to January 2023, and is not comparable with the twelve-month figures above which it sits. NO ANNUALISATION IS PERFORMED HERE and none should be inferred: electricity flows are seasonal, so scaling ten months to twelve would produce a figure that describes no period and belongs to no source. For the same reason the record states no trend across the four years and no growth rate. The Year End Review 2025 counts are of a scan that saw the document — 19 occurrences of "Ministry of Power", 86 of "2025", 39 of "electricity" and 42 of "transmission" over 49,271 characters, with no `Loading` placeholder — because a zero from a page that failed to load is not an absence.

**What this is.** A Parliamentary reply of 28 March 2023 published net cross-border electricity flows sourced from the Central Electricity Authority, by counterparty and financial year, in Million Units. India's exports to Bangladesh were 6,987.94 in 2019-20, 7,551.99 in 2020-21, 7,301.74 in 2021-22 and 7,234.08 in 2022-23 up to January 2023. An earlier reply of 19 March 2020 gave 6,168.14 for 2019-20 as on January 2020. No later series was retrieved: the Ministry of Power's Year End Review 2025, published 16 January 2026, does not mention Bangladesh, Nepal, Bhutan, exports or cross-border trade anywhere in its 49,271 characters, and the comprehensive Lok Sabha reply of 2 April 2026 recorded at L-0208 lists infrastructure without flows.

**What was claimed at announcement.** None stated. Both replies are answers to questions rather than announcements, and neither sets a target for cross-border trade, a volume to be reached, or a schedule for reporting.

**What can be established since.** THE QUANTITY IS COLLECTED, WAS PUBLISHED, AND HAS STOPPED APPEARING. The 2023 series is attributed to the Central Electricity Authority, which compiles it continuously; nothing suggests collection ceased. What changed is disclosure. The most recent comprehensive account of neighbourhood power cooperation lists thirteen generation projects and thirty transmission links and no volume over any of them; the Ministry's own year-end review of 2025 does not mention the neighbourhood at all. THE PUBLISHED SERIES ALSO SETS A PARTIAL YEAR BESIDE FULL ONES. The top row, 7,234.08, covers ten months to January 2023; the three rows beneath it cover twelve. Read down the column the figure appears flat against 7,301.74 for the full year before it, and that reading is unavailable — the periods differ.

**Strongest case for.** The series is better disclosure than most of this arc contains, and it deserves saying. It is attributed to a named compiler rather than floated unsourced, it gives four consecutive years on a consistent unit, it covers every land neighbour, and it marks direction per cell rather than assuming one — which is what lets a reader see that India moved from exporting 1,921.09 Million Units to Nepal in 2021-22 to importing 488.96 in the ten months following, a reversal that a one-directional table would have hidden. The two replies are also consistent with each other once their cut-offs are read: 6,987.94 for the full year 2019-20 against 6,168.14 as on January 2020 leaves 819.80 Million Units in the remainder, which is an ordinary two months. Parliamentary replies answer what is asked, and no reporting obligation was breached by not repeating a table nobody requested.

**Strongest case against.** A quantity that is compiled continuously and published intermittently is one no reader can track, and the gap is now longer than the series. The last published figure covers a period ending January 2023; three years later the Ministry's comprehensive statement of neighbourhood power cooperation gives infrastructure with no flows, and its own year-end review of 2025 does not mention Bangladesh, Nepal, Bhutan, exports or cross-border trade in any form. Physical capacity is thereby fully described and its use is not described at all — a reader can learn how many links exist and nothing about whether power crosses them. And the one series that was published contains a comparison trap: a ten-month figure sits at the head of a column of twelve-month figures, distinguished only by a parenthesis, in a release whose headline speaks of the last four years.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** The two figures for 2019-20 — 6,168.14 and 6,987.94 — are NOT a disagreement and are not filed as one. They are the same quantity measured to different cut-offs, ten months and twelve, and the difference of 819.80 Million Units is the remainder of the year. Recorded explicitly because two official figures for one financial year is the shape of a differentFacts pair, and treating it as one would manufacture a contest out of a reporting convention.

**Note on the verdict itself.** No target, volume or reporting commitment is announced in either reply, so there is nothing that could have worked or failed. What the record establishes is what is measurable about this relationship and when it stopped being published, which is the precondition for scoring anything else about it later.

**Re-test when.** Re-test if any Million Units series covering 2023-24 or later is published. That single table would close the first unmeasured, make the Godda question answerable for the first time, and turn L-0208's infrastructure inventory into something a reader can weigh.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** India's electricity exports to Bangladesh after January 2023 — The Central Electricity Authority compiles these flows — the 2023 reply attributes the series to it — so the figures exist. They are absent from the places they would appear: the Ministry of Power's Year End Review 2025, scanned in full, contains no mention of Bangladesh, exports or cross-border trade, and the 2 April 2026 Lok Sabha reply on cooperation with neighbouring countries gives no volume over any of the thirty links it lists. **What would close it:** A Central Electricity Authority cross-border exchange release, or a Parliamentary answer updating the Million Units series by counterparty and year.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Whether any officially published series accounts for the Godda arrangement — The published series ends at January 2023, which is before the period in which such an arrangement would appear in it, so the series cannot speak to the question either way. No primary establishing the arrangement was retrieved in this run or the previous one, and this record therefore asserts no absence — the point recorded is only that the measured series stops before the question becomes answerable. **What would close it:** A Ministry of Power or Central Electricity Authority statement covering financial years from 2023-24 onward, against which the question could be asked at all.

**Sources** (3, T1×3):

- `T1` Ministry of New and Renewable Energy and Ministry of Power, "Western Region leads in electrical energy generation; India exported power to Bangladesh, Nepal and Myanmar during the last four years", PIB, 28 March 2023 — net export/import of electricity by Indian entities in Million Units, attributed to the Central Electricity Authority: Bangladesh 6,987.94 (2019-20), 7,551.99 (2020-21), 7,301.74 (2021-22), 7,234.08 (2022-23 up to January 2023), all export; and Nepal 1,921.09 export in 2021-22 against 488.96 import in 2022-23 up to January 2023 — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=1911488
- `T1` Ministry of Power, "Trade in Electricity", PIB, 19 March 2020 — import/export by counterparty for financial year 2019-20 as on January 2020: Bangladesh 6,168.14 Million Units export, Nepal 1,839.25 export, Bhutan 6,165.78 import, Myanmar 7.34 export; and the Guidelines on Cross Border Trade of Electricity of 5 December 2016, substituted by the Guidelines for Import/Export (Cross Border) of Electricity 2018 issued 18 December 2018 — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=1607177
- `T1` Ministry of Power, "Year End Review of Ministry of Power - 2025", PIB, 16 January 2026 — scanned in full through the corpus-search helper: zero occurrences of Bangladesh, Nepal, Bhutan, "Million Units", export or cross-border across 49,271 characters, against positive controls of 19 "Ministry of Power", 86 "2025", 39 "electricity" and 42 "transmission" — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2215187

### B.57 · L-0212 — The debt-restructuring instrument was named in December 2024 and existed by April 2025, and the assistance around it is stated in three currencies, two of them without a rate, and never totalled

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — partly on the same multi-objective shape — the comparison set.*

`partly` · T3 · foreign/macro · lens neighbourhood · reform · 2025-04-05 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-05

**History.** Created 2026-08-05. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** NO CURRENCY CONVERSION IS PERFORMED HERE. The 2.4 billion Lankan Rupee figure is not restated in dollars and is not added to any dollar figure, because no rate appears in the source and applying an external one would attach a precision to the comparison that the publisher did not offer. For the same reason no total of assistance is stated. The 5 April 2025 document is the Prime Minister's remarks at a joint press statement — a speech, not an agreement text — and this record treats its figures as announced rather than as audited. The grid interconnection is noted here only as it was framed on that occasion, as creating opportunities for Sri Lanka to export electricity; the link itself is the single India-Sri Lanka row in the Ministry of Power table recorded at L-0208, where it is also the one entry for which no operating history can exist.

**What this is.** At the India-Sri Lanka summit of 16 December 2024 the two leaders "instructed officials to finalize discussions on the bilateral MoU on Debt Restructuring". By the joint press statement of 5 April 2025 in Colombo the Prime Minister referred to "our bilateral 'Debt Restructuring Agreement'" as an existing instrument, and announced that in the preceding six months India had converted loans worth more than US$100 million into grants, that interest rates had been reduced that day, and that a support package of approximately 2.4 billion Lankan Rupees would be provided for the Eastern Provinces. The December statement records India as co-chair of the Official Creditors' Committee, emergency financing and forex support worth US$4 billion, and US$20.66 million to settle payments due for projects completed under existing Lines of Credit.

**What was claimed at announcement.** Two things, and only the first is a commitment. The commitment: to finalise discussions on the bilateral MoU on Debt Restructuring, stated on 16 December 2024 with no date attached. The framing: a "strategic shift from debt-driven models towards investment led partnerships", which the two leaders agreed "would ensure a more sustainable path to economic recovery" — an assertion about a direction, carrying no target.

**What can be established since.** COMMITMENT STATE (a) RESOLVED, AND QUICKLY. The instrument named on 16 December 2024 as something officials were instructed to finalise is spoken of on 5 April 2025 as existing — a bilateral Debt Restructuring Agreement, referred to in the Prime Minister's own voice — which is delivery inside four months against a commitment that carried no deadline at all. THE QUANTITIES AROUND IT DO NOT COMBINE. Four figures are given across the two documents: US$4 billion of "emergency financing and forex support", US$20.66 million to settle payments due under existing Lines of Credit, "more than US$100 million" of loans converted into grants, and approximately 2.4 billion Lankan Rupees for the Eastern Provinces. They are in two currencies with no rate published, they mix a facility aggregate with specific transactions, one of them is a floor rather than a figure, and no document retrieved states a total of relief extended or debt reduced.

**Strongest case for.** This is a commitment kept faster than it was required to be, on a matter where slowness is the norm. The December statement set no date; four months later the instrument existed and interest rates had been cut on top of it. India co-chaired the Official Creditors' Committee — a role, not a cheque, and the harder of the two things to do — and the Sri Lankan President's own account credits that co-chairing as instrumental in finalising the wider restructuring in time. Converting Lines of Credit into grants is the form of relief that actually removes debt rather than deferring it, and India did it repeatedly: US$20.66 million to clear payments due on completed projects, then more than US$100 million of loans converted over six months. The April statement also puts these figures in India's own voice, which the December one did not.

**Strongest case against.** Nothing here can be added up, and the presentation makes that harder rather than easier. US$4 billion covers "emergency financing and forex support" — a disbursement and a facility, which are different quantities — with no breakdown, no period and no statement of how much was drawn or repaid, so the headline figure describes neither a stock nor a flow. "More than US$100 million" is a floor and not a measurement. And the Eastern Provinces package is stated in Lankan Rupees while everything around it is in dollars, with no rate given, which leaves the smallest headline commitment in the speech carrying its largest digits — 2.4 billion beside 20.66 million. In the December statement every figure for Indian assistance sits in the Sri Lankan President's mouth, thanked for rather than asserted; the Prime Minister's own paragraph contains no number at all.

**Different facts — flag is FALSE.** No competing accounts. The US$20.66 million of December 2024 and the "more than US$100 million" of April 2025 are not two versions of one figure and are not filed as such: the first settles payments due on completed Line of Credit projects, the second converts loans to grants over a later six-month window. Different acts, possibly overlapping, and nothing retrieved states the relationship between them — which is recorded as unmeasured rather than resolved by assumption.

**Note on the verdict itself.** The delivery half worked and the measurement half does not permit scoring. A named instrument moved from instruction to existence in four months, which is faster than most of this phase's commitments and is scored as such. What cannot be scored is the assistance: the figures are not commensurable, no aggregate is published, and the largest of them has no breakdown, so whether the relief was large or small against Sri Lanka's obligations is not answerable from anything retrieved. Partly, on that split — not as a hedge between the two.

**Re-test when.** Re-test if the Debt Restructuring Agreement's terms or signature date are published, or if any Indian statement gives cumulative assistance to Sri Lanka in a single currency. Either would make the scale of the relief measurable for the first time; the instrument's existence is already settled.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The composition of the US$4 billion — how much was emergency financing, how much forex support, over what period, and how much has been drawn or repaid — The figure appears once, as a round aggregate, in the Sri Lankan President's expression of thanks. No Indian government document retrieved breaks it down, dates it, or distinguishes facility from disbursement. As with the Maldives swap in L-0206, a facility and a drawing are different quantities and the published number does not say which it is. **What would close it:** A Ministry of Finance or Department of Economic Affairs statement of assistance to Sri Lanka by instrument and year, or a Parliamentary answer giving the breakdown.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The total value of debt relief extended to Sri Lanka, and the exchange rate applied to the 2.4 billion Lankan Rupee package — Four figures are published across the two statements in two currencies, and no document retrieved totals them or gives a rate at which the Rupee figure could be placed beside the dollar ones. The rate exists and is not stated; the total is a sum the publishers have not performed. **What would close it:** An Indian government statement of cumulative assistance to Sri Lanka in one currency, or the Eastern Provinces package restated in dollars.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The text, date of signature and terms of the bilateral Debt Restructuring Agreement — It is referred to as existing on 5 April 2025 and was still being finalised on 16 December 2024, so it was concluded between those dates. Neither the date nor any term of it appears in anything retrieved, and the April reference is a sentence in a press statement rather than an announcement of the instrument. **What would close it:** The text of the Agreement, or a Ministry of Finance announcement of its signature and principal terms.

**Sources** (2, T1×2):

- `T1` Prime Minister's Office, "India - Sri Lanka Joint Statement: Fostering Partnerships for a Shared Future", PIB, 16 December 2024 — paragraph 10: emergency financing and forex support worth US$4 billion, India as co-chair of the Official Creditors' Committee, US$20.66 million to settle payments due for projects completed under existing Lines of Credit, and the instruction to officials to finalize discussions on the bilateral MoU on Debt Restructuring; paragraph 7 on converting Line of Credit projects to grant assistance; paragraph 11 on the shift from debt-driven models to investment-led partnerships; paragraph 9 on training 1,500 Sri Lankan civil servants over five years — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2084793
- `T1` Prime Minister's Office, "Prime Minister's remarks during Joint Press Statement with the President of Sri Lanka", PIB, 5 April 2025 — "in the last 6 months alone, we have converted loans worth more than USD 100 million into grants", "our bilateral 'Debt Restructuring Agreement'" as an existing instrument, the decision to reduce interest rates, the approximately 2.4 billion Lankan Rupee support package for the Eastern Provinces, and the Grid Inter-Connectivity Agreement framed as creating opportunities for Sri Lanka to export electricity — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2119187

### B.58 · L-0213 — Nineteen months after the IMEC memorandum the corridor's published scope had grown and its deliverable list was still empty, and the two figures attached to it name neither a baseline nor a date

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — no-objective on a record that states a target; put in question by the first external review — heavily cited, non-evaluative, no source conflict.*

`no-objective` · T3 · foreign/infrastructure · lens europe · reform · 2025-04-16 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-05

**History.** Created 2026-08-05. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** The most recent Ministry of Ports, Shipping and Waterways year-end account retrieved is for 2024; a 2025 edition was not located, so the Ports-side position after December 2024 is UNCHECKED rather than established as empty, and this record does not claim otherwise. The nineteen-month and thirty-five-month intervals are elapsed periods between published dates and are not claims about how long any work took. The April 2025 document is a minister's remarks at a roundtable — a speech, not an agreement or a progress report — and its figures are recorded as announced, not as audited. This record makes no assertion about conditions in the regions the corridor would cross, because nothing bearing on them was retrieved.

**What this is.** A Memorandum of Understanding on the India-Middle East-Europe Economic Corridor was signed on 9 September 2023 at the G20 Summit in New Delhi by India, the United States, Saudi Arabia, the United Arab Emirates, the European Union, Italy, France and Germany. It described an Eastern Corridor connecting India to the Gulf and a Northern Corridor connecting the Gulf to Europe, comprising a railway and ship-rail transit network and road transport routes. An Inter-Governmental Framework Agreement with the United Arab Emirates was signed on 13 February 2024 and given ex-post facto Cabinet approval on 13 March 2024. On 16 April 2025 the Minister of Commerce and Industry told a High-Level Roundtable that IMEC would "bring down logistics costs by up to 30%, reduce transportation time by 40%", and set out five suggestions for how the initiative should be taken forward.

**What was claimed at announcement.** At signature, a description rather than a target: two corridors, a set of transport modes, and economic integration between India and Europe. The only quantified claims appear nineteen months later and are benefit claims, not commitments — costs down by up to 30 per cent and transport time down by 40 per cent. No completion date, no phasing, no cost, no route length and no segment target appears in any document retrieved.

**What can be established since.** THE SCOPE GREW WHILE THE DELIVERABLE LIST STAYED EMPTY. The 2023 memorandum described a railway and ship-rail network and road routes. By April 2025 the published account added energy pipelines, clean energy infrastructure and undersea cables, and raised the possibility of extending connectivity to Africa through the Middle East. Across the same nineteen months no kilometre built, no contract awarded, no cost, no financing arrangement and no completed segment appears in anything retrieved. WHAT THE MINISTER SET OUT WAS FIVE SUGGESTIONS, NOT FIVE DELIVERABLES: that the corridor be viewed through a public-private partnership lens because leaving it to government "would limit its efficiency and financial viability"; that regulatory connectivity be pursued; that financing instruments including "IMEC Bonds" be created; that industry bodies be engaged; and that think tanks and academia be brought into "the visioning and design process". Nineteen months after signature, the design was still being visioned and the money was still to be raised.

**Strongest case for.** Eight parties including the United States, the European Union and Saudi Arabia put their names to a single connectivity instrument, which is itself the hard part and is not nothing. India converted the multilateral memorandum into a bilateral treaty instrument with the United Arab Emirates within five months and took it to Cabinet, which is faster than most of the agreements in this phase moved and is the concrete step available to a party that cannot build the other legs alone. The five suggestions are also more candid than announcements of this kind usually are: they name the real obstacles rather than asserting them solved — that government financing alone will not carry it, that regulatory and customs harmonisation matters as much as track, that new instruments will be needed to fund it. A transcontinental corridor across sovereign jurisdictions genuinely takes longer than nineteen months, and declining to name a date may be honesty about that rather than evasion.

**Strongest case against.** The two figures that circulate are the two that cannot be checked. "Up to 30%" is a ceiling rather than an estimate, and neither figure names a baseline route, a current cost, a comparator corridor, a cargo type or a source; a reader is given the size of a saving without being told what it is a saving against. Meanwhile the corridor's described components multiplied — pipelines, clean energy infrastructure, undersea cables, a possible extension to Africa — while nothing retrieved records a metre of it being built, and an initiative whose scope grows faster than its output is the shape that most reliably outruns scrutiny. The Ministry that signed the bilateral framework recorded IMEC in its own year-end account of 2024 solely as the signing of that agreement. And a minister proposing, nineteen months in, that the private sector lead and that bonds be invented to fund the thing is describing an initiative whose financing was not settled when it was announced.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Commitment state (d) — unfalsifiable by construction, and the second instance the instrument holds after L-0209. No completion date, no phasing and no target quantity means there is no trigger, so it is not state (a); it can never fall due, so it cannot reach (b); and nothing evidences abandonment, so it is not (c). Scored no-objective on that ground: an objective is a target that can be failed. This is NOT a finding that IMEC is stalled or abandoned — it is that nothing published permits either conclusion, now or at any future date.

**Re-test when.** Re-test if any signatory publishes a route, a cost, a schedule or a completed segment, or if a Ministry of Ports, Shipping and Waterways year-end review after 2024 is retrieved. A named target year is the single thing that would move this out of state (d) and make the corridor scoreable at all.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The baseline against which logistics costs fall by up to 30 per cent and transport time by 40 per cent — Both are share-shaped figures with neither term named. No comparator route, current cost, cargo type, measurement method or source is given in the release or in anything else retrieved, so neither figure can be checked, and the direction of any error cannot even be bounded. **What would close it:** The study or model the figures are drawn from, or a Ministry of Commerce statement of the comparator route and cost base.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** Any physical or financial progress on the corridor — kilometres, contracts, cost, or a completed segment — The Ministry of Ports, Shipping and Waterways signed the bilateral framework and would build the maritime leg; its Year End Review 2024 mentions IMEC three times and all three are the signature of that agreement, with no progress attached. The Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade's Year End Review 2025 does not mention IMEC at all. Its twelve occurrences of "corridor" are the National Industrial Corridor Development Programme, a domestic scheme, which is why the word count is not treated as evidence either way. **What would close it:** A Ministry of Ports, Shipping and Waterways or Ministry of Commerce statement of IMEC works undertaken, or a Parliamentary answer on expenditure and physical progress.

**Not measured — stated reason `never-defined`.** A completion date, phasing schedule or interim milestone of any kind — None appears in the memorandum, the Inter-Governmental Framework Agreement announcement, or the April 2025 remarks. A date that was never set does not exist to be published, which is what separates this from the two items above. **What would close it:** Any published schedule for either corridor, or a target year stated by any signatory.

**Sources** (5, T1×5):

- `T1` Prime Minister's Office, "Partnership for Global Infrastructure and Investment (PGII) & India-Middle East-Europe Economic Corridor (IMEC)", PIB, 9 September 2023 — the MoU signed by India, the USA, Saudi Arabia, the UAE, the European Union, Italy, France and Germany; the Eastern and Northern Corridors; and the railway, ship-rail transit network and road transport routes — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=1955921
- `T1` Ministry of Ports, Shipping and Waterways, "Cabinet approves Inter-Governmental Framework Agreement India and United Arab Emirates ... India-Middle East Europe Economic Corridor", PIB, 13 March 2024 — the IGFA signed 13 February 2024, its ex-post facto Cabinet approval, and its stated object of exploring further potential of future joint investment and collaboration — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2014134
- `T1` Ministry of Commerce & Industry, "India poised to become a trusted bridge of global connectivity through IMEC: Shri Piyush Goyal", PIB, 16 April 2025 — "It will bring down logistics costs by up to 30%, reduce transportation time by 40%"; the added components of railways, roadways, energy pipelines, clean energy infrastructure and undersea cables and the possible extension to Africa; and the five suggestions on public-private partnership, regulatory connectivity, innovative financing including "IMEC Bonds", industry engagement, and think tanks in the visioning and design process — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2122299
- `T1` Ministry of Ports, Shipping and Waterways, "Year End Review of Ministry of Ports, Shipping and Waterways 2024", PIB — scanned in full through the corpus-search helper with morphological variants: three occurrences of IMEC across 102,898 characters, all of them the signature of the Inter-Governmental Framework Agreement, none carrying progress — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2089049
- `T1` Department for Promotion of Industry and Internal Trade, "2025 Year End Review", PIB — scanned in full with variants: zero occurrences of IMEC and of "Middle East" across 64,541 characters, against positive controls of 82 "2025", 66 "India" and 11 "Ministry"; the twelve occurrences of "corridor" are the National Industrial Corridor Development Programme — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressReleasePage.aspx?PRID=2201280

### B.59 · L-0215 — Vaccine Maitri is presented as a gift, and the doses the government names as gifted are 1.51 crore of a stated 30.12 crore supplied

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — no-objective on a record that states a target.*

`no-objective` · T3 · foreign/human-development · institutional · 2025-06-18 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-05

**History.** Created 2026-08-05. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** The stated total is "over 30.12 crore", a floor rather than an exact figure, so the residual of 23.41 crore is a MINIMUM and is stated as such — a larger true total makes the unassigned share larger, not smaller, and this record does not compute a share of a floor as though it were exact. The two share figures given — just over five per cent gifted, about twenty-two per cent across both named channels — take 30.12 crore as the denominator and the government's own 1.51 and 5.2 crore as numerators, and are stated with both terms named for that reason. "Over 50 nations" qualifies the number of countries, not the 1.51 crore doses. The explainer is a Press Information Bureau summary written in June 2025 about a period beginning January 2021; it is treated as the government's own restatement, not as the underlying record of supplies.

**What this is.** A Press Information Bureau explainer of 18 June 2025 states that since January 2021 India supplied over 30.12 crore vaccine doses to 99 countries and two United Nations bodies under the Vaccine Maitri initiative, and that this "included 1.51 crore doses gifted to over 50 nations and 5.2 crore doses through the COVAX mechanism". The two named channels together account for 6.71 crore doses, leaving at least 23.41 crore — a little over three-quarters of the stated total — in neither category. The same explainer describes the initiative as "a testament to its human-centric diplomacy" under the heading "Humanity First, Always".

**What was claimed at announcement.** No target, quantity or date is announced anywhere in the document. What is offered is a characterisation: humanity first, human-centric diplomacy, and India as "a reliable partner for global health and a compassionate voice from the Global South". The numbers are given as achievement, not as progress against anything.

**What can be established since.** THE TOTAL IS A SUPPLY FIGURE AND THE FRAMING IS A GIFT FRAMING, AND ONLY ONE OF THE THREE NUMBERS IS ABOUT GIVING. Of the 30.12 crore doses stated as supplied, 1.51 crore are described as gifted — just over five per cent, on the government's own two figures — and 5.2 crore went through COVAX, a multilateral procurement and allocation mechanism rather than a bilateral gift. The remaining 23.41 crore doses at minimum are placed in no category at all. NOTHING RETRIEVED SAYS WHAT THEY WERE. The word "included" is accurate and does not claim to exhaust the total, which is why this is recorded as a composition that cannot be reconstructed rather than as a false statement.

**Strongest case for.** The disclosure is more specific than the framing required and that cuts in the government's favour. It would have been easy to publish only the 30.12 crore total under a humanitarian heading; instead the explainer breaks out the gifted doses and the COVAX doses separately, with the gifted figure to two decimal places, which is what makes the composition question askable at all. "Included" is the correct word for a partial breakdown and does not assert that the two named channels exhaust the total. Supplying 30.12 crore doses to 99 countries and two UN bodies from a standing start in January 2021 is a real logistical achievement whatever the commercial mix, and reaching two UN bodies and over 50 nations by gift is not a trivial share of the world. COVAX doses are also not self-interested in any ordinary sense: they went to a multilateral allocation mechanism that decided their destination.

**Strongest case against.** The number that travels is 30.12 crore and the frame it travels under is a gift, and those two things do not describe the same thing. On the government's own figures the explicitly gifted portion is 1.51 crore of 30.12 crore — a little over one dose in twenty — while the heading above them reads "Humanity First, Always". More than three-quarters of the stated total is assigned to no channel whatever, so a reader cannot tell what the largest part of the programme actually was, and no other retrieved document supplies it. Four years after the period began, an explainer written to summarise India's global footprint gives a composition that cannot be reconstructed, which is a choice about presentation rather than a limit of the underlying records.

**Note on the verdict itself.** The initiative announces no target, no quantity and no date, so there is nothing against which it could have worked or failed, and the numbers are presented as accomplishment rather than as progress. What can be checked is the composition of what is reported, and the finding is that it cannot be reconstructed from the figures given. Recorded so that the 30.12 crore is not later read as a measure of doses given away, which is the reading the surrounding language invites and the figures do not support.

**Re-test when.** Re-test if any statement breaks Vaccine Maitri supplies into channels summing to the published total, or gives a value on any basis. Either would make the programme's composition reconstructable for the first time.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** What the 23.41 crore doses outside the gifted and COVAX categories were — the channel, terms and recipients — The explainer names two channels and a larger total and does not describe the difference. Nothing retrieved states what the remainder consisted of. This record does NOT name a candidate: the obvious inference is not supported by any document retrieved, and an unsourced explanation would be as much of a fabrication as an unsourced accusation. **What would close it:** A Ministry of External Affairs or Ministry of Health statement of Vaccine Maitri supplies by channel, or a Parliamentary answer breaking the total into grant, commercial and multilateral components.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The value of the doses supplied, on any basis — The programme is reported in doses only. No cost, price or value appears in the explainer or in anything else retrieved, so the scale of what was given, sold or contributed cannot be stated in money at all. **What would close it:** A budgetary figure for Vaccine Maitri, or a Parliamentary answer on expenditure and receipts under the initiative.

**Sources** (1, T1×1):

- `T1` Press Information Bureau, "Bharat's Global Footprint", Explainer, 18 June 2025 — Vaccine Maitri: over 30.12 crore doses to 99 countries and two UN bodies since January 2021, including 1.51 crore gifted to over 50 nations and 5.2 crore through COVAX, under the heading "Humanity First, Always"; and the G20 presidency from 1 December 2022 to 30 November 2023 with the 18th Leaders' Summit on 9-10 September 2023 attended by 20 member states, nine invitee countries and 14 international organisations, across over 200 meetings in 60 cities — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressNoteDetails.aspx?ModuleId=3&NoteId=154680&id=154680

### B.60 · L-0217 — The trade suspended on 23 April 2025 was already one-directional and the two governments do not agree what it was: for 2024 India reports US$1,183.10m of exports to Pakistan against Pakistan's US$304.93m of imports from India

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — no-objective on a record that states a target.*

`no-objective` · T3 · foreign/macro · lens neighbourhood · reform · 2025-04-23 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-05

**History.** Created 2026-08-05. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** The 2025 annual figures are used only to state what each source records for that year. THEY ARE NOT USED TO MEASURE THE SUSPENSION: the year straddles 23 April, no monthly series is available, and this record does not report the fall from 2024 as an effect of the decision. It also does not establish that the 2025 figures are complete years — the source carries no completeness indicator on this route, and a partial year would understate both sides. The ratio of about 184 to one is India's 2024 exports to Pakistan divided by India's 2024 imports from Pakistan, both from India's own returns in the same year and the same dataset, so it compares like with like; no ratio is formed across the two reporters. All figures are UN Comtrade annual, commodity code TOTAL, in current US dollars.

**What this is.** The Cabinet Committee on Security decision of 23 April 2025 closed the Integrated Check Post at Attari with immediate effect, requiring those who had crossed with valid endorsements to return by 1 May 2025, and suspended all bilateral trade with Pakistan. UN Comtrade, on India's own returns, gives India's exports to Pakistan as US$523.22m in 2023, US$1,183.10m in 2024 and US$352.27m in 2025, against imports from Pakistan of US$7.69m, US$6.42m and US$3.73m in the same years. Pakistan's returns for the same trade are far smaller: imports from India of US$258.20m, US$304.93m and US$311.31m, and exports to India of US$0.04m, US$0.02m and US$0.11m.

**What was claimed at announcement.** No target, value or duration is announced. The measure is presented by the Press Information Bureau as severing an economic lifeline without escalating direct military conflict, alongside the Indus Waters Treaty abeyance recorded at L-0216, and is described as halting exports of goods including onions and banning imports of cement and textiles.

**What can be established since.** THE FLOW SUSPENDED WAS ALREADY ALMOST ENTIRELY ONE-WAY. On India's own figures for 2024, exports to Pakistan of US$1,183.10m stood against imports from Pakistan of US$6.42m — India sent about 184 times what it received, so a suspension of "all bilateral trade" removes an Indian export market and almost nothing on the import side. THE TWO GOVERNMENTS DISAGREE ABOUT THE SIZE OF THAT MARKET BY MULTIPLES. For 2024 India reports US$1,183.10m of exports to Pakistan while Pakistan reports US$304.93m of imports from India — a gap of US$878.17m, with India's figure 3.88 times Pakistan's. The same disagreement runs the other way and is larger in ratio: for 2024 India records US$6.42m of imports from Pakistan against Pakistan's US$0.02m of exports to India. THE EFFECT OF THE SUSPENSION IS NOT MEASURED HERE. The 2025 annual figures straddle 23 April, and no monthly series for India is available on this route, so the year cannot be split into its pre- and post-decision parts.

**Strongest case for.** As economic coercion goes this is unusually low-cost to the party imposing it, and that is a real argument in its favour rather than a criticism. India was the seller in this relationship by a factor of roughly 184 to one on its own 2024 figures, so suspending the trade forgoes an export market while surrendering almost no import dependence — there is no supply Indian industry loses. Closing a single land crossing at Attari is also the most reversible of the measures announced that day: an Integrated Check Post can be reopened by the same decision that closed it, unlike a treaty. And the government stated what it was doing in specific terms, naming affected goods on both sides, which is more than a general announcement of economic measures would have offered.

**Strongest case against.** A government suspending a trade should be able to say how large it was, and here the two parties' figures differ by more than the whole of the smaller one — for 2024 the gap is US$878.17m against Pakistan's stated US$304.93m. Whichever figure a reader takes, the other is wrong by a margin that dwarfs any plausible valuation adjustment, and no Indian document retrieved acknowledges the divergence or explains it. The asymmetry also cuts against the framing: describing the measure as severing an economic lifeline is a claim about the effect on Pakistan, and the numbers that would support it are the numbers in dispute. And the one thing that would show whether the suspension bit — a monthly series across April 2025 — is not published on this route, so the measure's effect is unavailable at exactly the moment it matters.

**Different facts — flag is TRUE.** Both instruments measure the same quantity — trade between India and Pakistan in a given year — and disagree by multiples in both directions and in every year checked. Neither figure is adopted and no midpoint is taken. Ordinary explanations exist for a divergence of this kind, including valuation convention between export and import records and attribution of goods routed through third countries, and none is asserted here because no retrieved source states which applies or in what proportion. This is the same shape as the India-China pair at P-119 and larger: there the ratio is a fraction, here India's 2024 export figure is 3.88 times Pakistan's import figure. The gap is arithmetically computable and its magnitude is stated — US$878.17m for 2024 — but it is not treated as a measured quantity, because it is the difference between two accounts of the same trade rather than anything either party reports. Which side is short, and by how much, is exactly what is unresolved.

**Note on the verdict itself.** The decision announces no target, no value and no duration, so there is nothing that could have worked or failed. What this record establishes is the measurement position: what the suspended trade was on each side's account, that the two accounts differ by multiples, and that the suspension's own effect cannot be isolated from the series available. Deliberately NOT scored as an outcome — a fall in the 2025 annual figure is consistent with the suspension and also with a return from an exceptional 2024, and nothing retrieved separates them.

**Re-test when.** Re-test when a monthly series across April 2025 becomes available on any route, and when 2026 annual figures are published — a full year wholly after the decision is the first observation that does not straddle it. The mirror gap should be re-checked at the same time: if it narrows as direct trade stops, that is itself informative about what was causing it.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** India-Pakistan trade by month across April 2025 — The annual figures span the decision date. A monthly series would isolate the trade before and after 23 April 2025 and is the only thing that would make the suspension's effect measurable; UN Comtrade's public preview returns no monthly rows for India on this query. **What would close it:** A monthly Comtrade series, or Department of Commerce monthly trade data for Pakistan as a partner.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** Which of the two national accounts of the trade is closer to correct, and why they diverge — The divergence is present in every year checked, runs in both directions, and reaches 3.88 times on India's 2024 exports. Nothing retrieved from either government acknowledges it or states a cause, so no basis exists for preferring one series or reconciling them. **What would close it:** A reconciliation by either statistical authority, or a Comtrade partner-attribution note covering India-Pakistan trade.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** The value of trade actually forgone since the suspension, and whether goods continue to reach Pakistan through third countries — The decision suspends direct bilateral trade. Whether the goods it covered ceased to move or were re-routed is a different question from whether direct trade fell, and nothing retrieved addresses it. **What would close it:** A Department of Commerce assessment of the suspension's effect, or third-country trade data showing a corresponding rise.

**Sources** (3, T1×3):

- `T1` UN Comtrade, annual merchandise trade, reporter India (699), partner Pakistan (586), commodity TOTAL, current US dollars — exports 523,219,521.55 (2023), 1,183,104,667.26 (2024), 352,265,385.36 (2025); imports 7,694,921.24 (2023), 6,420,457.41 (2024), 3,726,123.20 (2025) — https://comtradeapi.un.org/public/v1/preview/C/A/HS?reporterCode=699&period=2024&partnerCode=586&flowCode=X,M&cmdCode=TOTAL
- `T1` UN Comtrade, annual merchandise trade, reporter Pakistan (586), partner India (699), commodity TOTAL, current US dollars — imports from India 258,201,193.28 (2023), 304,929,840.64 (2024), 311,312,741.10 (2025); exports to India 36,499.28 (2023), 16,625.29 (2024), 108,026.17 (2025) — https://comtradeapi.un.org/public/v1/preview/C/A/HS?reporterCode=586&period=2024&partnerCode=699&flowCode=X,M&cmdCode=TOTAL
- `T1` Press Information Bureau, "Operation SINDOOR: India's Strategic Clarity and Calculated Force", Backgrounder, 12 May 2025 — the closure of the Integrated Check Post Attari with immediate effect and return by 1 May 2025, the suspension of all bilateral trade with Pakistan, the halting of exports including onions and the ban on imports of cement and textiles — https://www.pib.gov.in/PressNoteDetails.aspx?NoteId=154448&ModuleId=3&reg=3&lang=1

### B.61 · L-0218 — Three Indian government publication channels cannot be read by an ordinary client, and the documents survive because a different arm of the same government republishes them

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — no-objective on a record that states a target.*

`no-objective` · T3 · governance/foreign · institutional · 2026-08-05 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-05

**History.** Created 2026-08-05. 1 commit(s) touched it afterwards: 2026-08-05 (title, summary, whatHappened, caseAgainst).

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** This record states what one client observed on 5 August 2026 and on the earlier dates noted in the verification log; it does not assert that these sites are unreachable in general, and the second unmeasured says so. The Permanent Court of Arbitration is deliberately excluded from the four: it returned HTTP 403 from both pca-cpa.org and pcacases.com and its Internet Archive snapshot resolves to the case index rather than the case, but it is not an Indian government publication channel and belongs to a different question. The 4,326-character figure is the normalised text remaining after markup is stripped, not the byte size served, and the two differ by more than an order of magnitude — which is the point: the byte size looks like a document and the text is not one.

**What this is.** Every retrieval blockage recorded during phase 14 was re-tested by varying the host. Three Indian government channels remain unreadable: the Ministry of External Affairs website, which returns HTTP 200 and a JavaScript scaffold with no document text, and the Ministry of Defence and Department of Defence Production sites, whose HTTPS connections fail outright. CORRECTED 2026-08-05 (correction cycle 6): this record originally counted FOUR and named the e-Gazette as the fourth and worst, unreachable and unduplicated. THAT WAS WRONG AND WAS A RESOLVER ARTEFACT. With an explicit resolver pin (egazette.gov.in -> 164.100.190.144) the portal serves, and a Gazette notification was retrieved from it as a PDF with a real text layer. The measurement was made with a plain request before this machine's broken resolver was understood - the very error this record warns about, committed by this record. In three of the four cases the material itself proved fully retrievable elsewhere — through the Press Information Bureau for ministry documents, and through the United Nations Treaty Series for a treaty that the Ministry's own portal serves only as an image. For the e-Gazette no alternate route was found.

**What was claimed at announcement.** No commitment is under test here. Every one of these sites is an official publication channel and is presented as the route by which the relevant documents are made public; that is the only claim involved, and it is implicit in their existence rather than announced.

**What can be established since.** THE BLOCKAGE IS AT THE MINISTRY'S OWN WEBSITE, AND THE RECORD SURVIVES BECAUSE A DIFFERENT ARM REPUBLISHES IT. mea.gov.in returns HTTP 200 with 82,719 bytes on its press-releases path, of which 4,326 characters are navigation chrome carrying the string "Loading..." and no release text; the same failure was confirmed earlier on its media-briefings path. mod.gov.in and ddpmod.gov.in do not complete an HTTPS connection at all. egazette.gov.in was originally recorded as failing to connect; on re-test with a resolver pin it serves, and that finding is corrected above. (egazette.nic.in has no A record on 1.1.1.1 at all, which is a different fact and stands.) Against this, the Press Information Bureau served a Ministry of Defence document of 1,994,703 bytes on request, and has carried Power, Home Affairs, Commerce and Prime Minister's Office material throughout this phase; and the Indus Waters Treaty — a 5MB image scan yielding 113 bytes of text on the Ministry's own legal-treaties portal — is a 215,794-character machine-readable document in the United Nations Treaty Series, where India registered it in 1962. ARCHIVING DOES NOT RESCUE A JAVASCRIPT SHELL. The Internet Archive's snapshot of the same Ministry page returns 4,326 characters, still carrying "Loading..." and still containing no release text: the crawler captured the scaffold, because the scaffold is what the server sends.

**Strongest case for.** The public record is more robust than any single channel's failure suggests, and that is the honest headline. Every ministry document sought during this phase was obtained, because the Press Information Bureau republishes across ministries and does so as server-rendered pages an ordinary client can read; the redundancy is real and it worked. For treaties the redundancy extends outside the government entirely — registration with the United Nations produced a machine-readable text of a 1960 instrument that the responsible ministry serves only as an image, which is the international registration system doing exactly what it exists for. A ministry website being slow, broken or JavaScript-heavy is also an ordinary technical condition rather than a policy of concealment, and nothing here suggests otherwise.

**Strongest case against.** A publication channel that cannot be read is not publishing, whatever it intends. Three of these are the designated route to their own material, and a citizen with a standard client gets HTTP 200 and an empty page from the Ministry of External Affairs — the failure mode that looks most like success and is hardest to distinguish from an absence. The redundancy that rescues the record is also incidental rather than designed: the Press Information Bureau carries what it carries, and a document no press release covers has no second route. The e-Gazette was offered here as the case that proves it, being unreachable and unduplicated; that example is WITHDRAWN, because the e-Gazette turned out to be reachable and its documents retrievable. The argument about incidental redundancy stands on its own; the illustration did not survive re-testing. And an archived copy of a JavaScript scaffold means the failure is being preserved rather than the document, so the loss is not merely current.

**Note on the verdict itself.** No target for the accessibility of the public record is announced by any of these bodies, so there is nothing that could have worked or failed. What this record establishes is a measurement precondition for everything else in the corpus: which official channels can be read, which cannot, and — the part that matters — that a failure to read a ministry's website is not evidence that its documents are unpublished. Recorded because the opposite inference was made three times in this phase and was wrong each time.

**Re-test when.** Re-test each channel at the start of any phase that depends on it — this record is a snapshot of a relationship between a client and four servers, and phase 14 found that two such findings had gone stale within months. The e-Gazette is the one to watch: it is the only channel here with no second route, and it is the channel of legal record.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-published`.** How much Ministry of External Affairs material has no Press Information Bureau equivalent — The redundancy that rescued this phase is opportunistic. Establishing its coverage would require enumerating what the Ministry publishes, which is precisely what cannot be read from the blocked channel. The gap is unmeasurable from the outside for the same reason it matters. **What would close it:** A server-rendered index or a machine-readable feed from the Ministry, either of which would make its output enumerable.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** Whether these channels are readable from other networks, clients or jurisdictions — Every test here ran from one location. A connection failure is a relationship between a client and a server, and this record states what one client observed rather than asserting a property of the servers. Two hosts were tried for the e-Gazette and for the arbitration site, and the Ministry site was tried on two paths, two dates and through the Internet Archive — but that is host variation, not network variation. **What would close it:** The same requests from an independent network, or a public status or uptime record for the sites.

**Sources** (2, T1×2):

- `T1` Retrieval observations, phase 14, recorded in docs/verification-log.md across cycles 2026-08-04 to 2026-08-05 — mea.gov.in press-releases and media-briefings paths returning HTTP 200 with a JavaScript scaffold; mod.gov.in and ddpmod.gov.in failing HTTPS; egazette.gov.in and egazette.nic.in failing to connect on both hosts; and the Internet Archive snapshot of the Ministry page reproducing the same scaffold — https://www.mea.gov.in/press-releases.htm
- `T1` United Nations Treaty Series, Volume 419, No. 6032 — the Indus Waters Treaty 1960 as registered by India on 16 January 1962, served as a machine-readable 215,794-character document, against the same instrument on the Ministry of External Affairs legal-treaties portal as an image scan yielding 113 bytes of extractable text — https://treaties.un.org/doc/Publication/UNTS/Volume%20419/volume-419-I-6032-English.pdf

### B.62 · L-0219 — Of 278 citations that name a publisher rather than a document, half cannot be opened and half name nothing specific enough to open — and the two halves are not the same half

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — no-objective on a record that states a target.*

`no-objective` · T3 · governance · institutional · 2026-08-05 · confidence high · last researched 2026-08-05

**History.** Created 2026-08-05. 1 commit(s) touched it afterwards: 2026-08-05 (sources).

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** EVERY FIGURE HERE DESCRIBES ONE CLIENT ON ONE DAY, and the sequence that produced it recorded three separate occasions when that mattered: ten live government hosts were measured as unreachable before this machine's broken resolver was understood; the e-Gazette was recorded in L-0218 as the corpus's one unreachable channel and is not one; and two runs of this very partition had to be discarded, the first because a failed fetch left the previous host's page on disk and three unrelated hosts were scored against the Ministry of External Affairs homepage, the second because a single-try two-second DNS lookup reported hosts as having no record when working pins for them were already on file. The 500-character threshold separating a scaffold from a document was chosen after inspecting the distribution, which has a clean gap between 102 and 894 characters, and not before. The three buckets are NOT disjoint: unaddressability is a property of the citation and reachability a property of the URL, so the 139 unaddressable citations are distributed across both other groups — 58 on hosts that answer and 81 on hosts that do not.

**What this is.** A correction sequence begun on 5 August 2026 reduced the corpus's bare-domain-root citations from 302 to 278 and then partitioned the residue rather than continuing to reduce it. Each of the 92 distinct cited roots was requested through an explicit resolver, and the response measured as text remaining after markup was stripped rather than as bytes. 139 of the 278 citations point at a URL that returns no document: 54 refused a connection despite resolving, 50 returned HTTP 200 with fewer than 500 characters of text, 23 have no DNS record, and 12 returned HTTP 403. The other 139 point at a URL that returns real content. Separately and cutting across both, 139 citations name no document at all — no year and no number anywhere in the citation — of which 58 sit on hosts that answer perfectly well.

**What was claimed at announcement.** None. No commitment governs how a research corpus cites, and this record scores nothing. It exists because the second output the retrieval-capability cycle was scoped to produce was a documented account of what cannot be retrieved by ordinary means, and this is that account.

**What can be established since.** THE PARTITION SEPARATES THREE FAILURES THAT LOOK IDENTICAL FROM A DISTANCE. A host that refuses a connection, a host that returns 200 with a JavaScript scaffold, and a citation that names a genre rather than a document all present as "the source cannot be checked", and each needs a different remedy — a route, a rendering client, or new research. THE MEASUREMENT THAT SEPARATES THE FIRST TWO IS TEXT, NOT BYTES. sansad.in returns 29,501 bytes and 25 characters of text; morth.nic.in and mospi.gov.in return nothing at all; cag.gov.in returns 43,861 characters. Ranked by bytes the first two are indistinguishable from a document. THE THIRD FAILURE IS THE LARGEST AND THE LEAST VISIBLE. "Contemporary reporting", "SHRC report coverage", "NCRB / MHA data and contemporary reporting" — 139 citations carry neither a year nor any multi-digit number, so no working host would help. 58 of those already sit on hosts that answer.

**Strongest case for.** The corpus is more checkable than the raw count suggests, and that is the honest reading. 139 of 278 cited roots do return real content, so a reader who wants the publisher can reach it; the defect is precision, not fabrication. The instrument also holds a mechanical guarantee that this class cannot grow: a gate refuses any new bare root, and refuses equally when a fixed citation's allowlist entry is left behind, so the count is monotonic and progress cannot be faked. And the 24 citations closed in this sequence were closed properly — each identified by matching the record's own values against the retrieved document, with the matching query written into the citation so a later reader re-runs the identification rather than trusting it.

**Strongest case against.** Half of the residue is not a link problem at all and no amount of link-fixing will touch it: 139 citations name a publisher or a genre, and closing them means finding out what was actually read, which for records written years ago may be irrecoverable. The corpus therefore contains a class of claim whose source is, in practice, the author's memory. The bare-root problem was also under-measured twice before it was measured properly — first at 141 because the detector filtered on one tier, then at 302 with a phantom class of 99 untiered entries that did not exist — so the count in this record should be read as the third attempt, not the first. And 42 of the 92 cited roots cannot be opened at all, which for a corpus whose discipline is that a source must be retrievable is a standing embarrassment however it is partitioned.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Nothing here was promised, so nothing can have failed. What the record establishes is the shape of a known defect and the size of each part of it, so that later work is directed at the right remedy and the instrument does not re-measure what it has already measured. The partition is the finding; the counts are its evidence.

**Re-test when.** Re-run the partition when the allowlist next falls materially, and re-probe before trusting any host classification here — the sequence that produced it changed its own conclusions three times on re-testing. The 81 citations that are both addressable and on a live host are the only group where cheap work remains.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** How many of the 139 unaddressable citations could be re-grounded, and how many are irrecoverable — Establishing it requires attempting the research behind each one, which is the work itself rather than a measurement of it. The 139 is a strict lower bound in the other direction too: it counts only citations carrying neither a year nor a multi-digit number, so a citation naming a year but no document is addressable by this test and may not be in practice. **What would close it:** A cycle that attempts a sample and reports the recovery rate.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** Whether the hosts classified here as returning no document serve documents on deeper paths — This partition measures THE CITED URL, which is a bare root, and not the host's capacity. The Press Information Bureau is the standing counter-example: its bare root refuses and www.pib.gov.in returns 24 characters, while PressReleasePage.aspx has served dozens of documents to this instrument. A root that returns nothing is evidence about the citation, not a verdict on the publisher. **What would close it:** Per-host probing of known document paths, which is the deep-linking work itself.

**Sources** (1, T1×1):

- `T1` INTERNAL REFERENCE, NOT AN OPENABLE URL — and the point is conceded rather than hidden in a record whose subject is exactly this. The repository is private and the URL given returns HTTP 404 to an unauthenticated client; it identifies the evidence rather than serving it. The evidence is three files in this repository: `tools/lib/bare-root-allowlist.json`, the ratchet frozen at 302 entries on 5 August 2026 and standing at 278 after correction cycles 2 to 8, whose `history` array records each deletion; `docs/verification-log.md`, cycles 2026-08-05p to 2026-08-05z, carrying the probe results and the two discarded runs of this partition; and `docs/assessment-audit-2026-08-05.md` with its appended corrections C1 to C6. Each of the 92 distinct cited roots was probed through an explicit resolver on 5 August 2026 and the response measured as text remaining after `htmlToText`, not as transferred bytes — https://github.com/neo999-cyber/india_government

### B.63 · L-0220 — Thirteen schemes across five domains are counted where the state hands something over and judged by what the recipient then does, and the two records that escape it are the two where the asset is itself the service

*Selected because: put in question by the first external review — no-objective on a record that states a target.*

`no-objective` · T3 · governance · institutional · 2026-08-05 · confidence medium · last researched 2026-08-05

**History.** Created 2026-08-05. Never edited since.

> **CAVEAT — this renders wherever the record appears and is never truncated.** THE EVIDENCE FOR THIS RECORD IS THE UNDERLYING RECORDS AND THEIR FIGURES ARE NOT RESTATED HERE, deliberately: a synthesis that repeats its sources' numbers invites them to drift apart. The thirteen are L-0012, L-0034, L-0035, L-0036, L-0037, L-0039, L-0044, L-0048, L-0050, L-0054, L-0055, L-0062 and L-0093; the two controls are L-0047 and L-0053. Each carries its own sourcing, and any figure cited in argument about this pattern should be taken from the record that holds it. The grouping is this record's own judgement and is contestable at its edges — the highway and UDAN cases are counts of a reclassified category rather than of a handover, and are included because the measurement stops short of use in the same way, not because the move is identical. The score regularity is stated as an observation, not as evidence: thirteen `partly` scores against two `worked` is consistent with the pattern being real and also with `partly` being the natural score for any large scheme.

**What this is.** Writing the reasoning behind every scored verdict in this corpus surfaced one shape thirteen times. A scheme announces an outcome — clean cooking, an end to open defecation, protection from catastrophic medical cost, affordable regional flying — and reports a count taken at the moment the state hands something over: a cylinder, a latrine, a tap, a card, an airport, a connection, a registration. The gap between the two is where the scheme's own purpose lives, and in every one of the thirteen it is either unmeasured or measured and short. Two records in the same files do not have the gap at all, and the difference between them and the thirteen is not effort, funding or execution.

**What was claimed at announcement.** None. This record is a synthesis over records already in the corpus and announces nothing of its own. The claims at issue are each scheme's, and are stated in the underlying records rather than repeated here.

**What can be established since.** THE DISCRIMINATOR IS WHETHER THE THING HANDED OVER BECOMES THE SERVICE WITHOUT ANYTHING FURTHER HAPPENING. Railway electrification (L-0047) is the control and its record now says so in terms: an electrified line carries electric traction from the day it energises — no behavioural change is required of anyone, no demand forecast has to be met, and no second party has to be able to afford anything. Port efficiency (L-0053) is the second control, where turnaround time halved while throughput nearly doubled, so the output metric and its utilisation counterpart moved together. THE THIRTEEN ALL REQUIRE A FURTHER STEP: a household must buy the refill, choose the latrine, be reached by water, occupy the house, be admitted to the hospital, board the flight, ride the train, receive supply, sign the contract. Each further step is where the scheme's stated purpose is realised, and each is the step the reporting stops before. A REGULARITY WORTH STATING BECAUSE IT WAS NOT ENGINEERED: all thirteen carry the score `partly` and both controls carry `worked`. Those scores were assigned in earlier phases and are unchanged by the work that found this pattern — the notes were written under an assertion that no score would move, which is checkable in the repository history.

**Strongest case for.** Measuring at the handover is not laziness and the record should not imply it is. It is the point at which the state's own action is complete and attributable to it: a ministry can build a tap and cannot make a household drink. Counts at that point are auditable, timely, and available at national scale, while use requires household surveys that are slower, costlier and contestable — the Swachh Bharat record shows two instruments disagreeing about use while nobody disputes how many latrines exist. Several of the thirteen also published the assessment that exposed their own gap: the Jal Jeevan functionality study and the Ujjwala refill audit are the government's own instruments finding against the government's own headline. And a connection is genuinely a precondition — none of the thirteen could have delivered its purpose without first delivering the thing it counted.

**Strongest case against.** The gap is not incidental to these schemes, it is where their entire stated purpose sits, and in eleven of the thirteen it was left without so much as a declared absence until this sweep. A scheme reporting the number of taps against a promise of water has substituted a measure it controls for the one it was judged on, and the substitution runs one way: no scheme in the corpus reports use while promising connection. Two of the thirteen go further and report a count that is not even a handover — a highway network expansion of which the overwhelming majority is existing state roads notified as national, and an airport total inflated by heliports and waterdromes. And the defence that use is hard to measure is undercut by the controls: where the asset is the service, the same ministries report use without difficulty, because there was no further step at which to stop.

**Note on the verdict itself.** Nothing was announced here, so nothing can have succeeded or failed. What this record establishes is a property of how the state measures itself that no single scheme's record can show: the measurement stops at the handover, and whether that is sound depends on a structural fact about the asset rather than on the diligence of the ministry reporting it. Recorded as a finding about instruments, not as a criticism of any of the thirteen — several of them delivered at a scale no predecessor approached, and the records say so.

**Re-test when.** Re-test when the next phase writes assessment notes for the 58 abstaining records: if the shape appears there too, the base-rate question in the first absence becomes answerable from inside the corpus. Re-test also if any of the thirteen publishes a use measurement, which would move that record and weaken this one by exactly one case.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** Whether the pattern holds outside this corpus, or is an artefact of which schemes it happens to record — The thirteen are the scored records this sweep read whole, not a sample drawn from all Indian schemes. A shape found in every case examined may still be a property of the selection. Nothing here establishes the base rate against which thirteen would be surprising.

**Not measured — stated reason `not-collected`.** Whether the schemes that measure at the handover do so by choice or because the use measurement does not exist to report — The two are different findings with different remedies and this record cannot separate them. Jal Jeevan and Ujjwala both commissioned use measurements and published shortfalls, which shows the choice exists; household electrification has no supply-hours series at all, which suggests in that case it may not. No general answer follows from thirteen cases.

**Sources** (1, T1×1):

- `T1` This corpus's own ledger records L-0012, L-0034 to L-0037, L-0039, L-0044, L-0048, L-0050, L-0054, L-0055, L-0062 and L-0093, and the controls L-0047 and L-0053, each read in full during the assessment-note sweep of 2026-08-05 and each carrying its own sources. INTERNAL REFERENCE, NOT AN OPENABLE URL: the repository is private and the URL below returns HTTP 404 to an unauthenticated client — https://github.com/neo999-cyber/india_government
